NCCRs of cones over del Pezzo surfaces Thanks: The first author is supported by World Premier International Research Center Initiative (WPI), MEXT, Japan. The second author is a senior researcher at the Research Foundation Flanders (FWO). This project has received funding from the European Research Council (ERC) under the European Union’s Horizon 2020 research and innovation programme (grant agreement No 885203).
Anya Nordskova, Michel Van den Bergh

Abstract
. Non-commutative crepant resolutions (NCCRs) are non-commutative versions of classical crepant resolutions in algebraic geometry. For 3-dimensional terminal Gorenstein singularities Iyama and Wemyss proved that all NCCRs are connected by mutations, which may be viewed as a non-commutative analogue of Kawamata’s result that all crepant resolutions are connected by flops. In this paper we prove the corresponding result for a class of canonical Gorenstein singularities which are not terminal, namely anticanonical cones over del Pezzo surfaces. More precisely, we first obtain a classification of NCCRs of anticanonical del Pezzo cones, showing that every NCCR arises from a geometric helix on the corresponding del Pezzo surface. We then prove that all such geometric helices are connected to each other by mutations, up to simple operations which include tensoring by line bundles and shifts. A crucial ingredient in our proofs is the polygons that can be associated to exceptional collections on del Pezzo surfaces following the works of Hille and Perling. We obtain some interesting observations about these polygons which may be of independent interest.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
16E35, 14F08, 14A22, 14E30

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Main results

Let
Z
be a normal algebraic variety with Gorenstein
singularities. A resolution of singularities (that is, a proper birational map from a non-singular variety)
{\pi:Y\to Z}
is said to be
crepant
if
{\pi^{*}\omega_{Z}=\omega_{Y}}
. Such a
Y
is often considered to be a nicest possible, “minimal” desingularisation of
Z
. A non-commutative analogue of this classical notion was introduced in
[
43
]
(see also
[
45
]
for an overview). More precisely, if
R
is a normal Gorenstein domain, then its
non-commutative crepant resolution
(
NCCR
) is an
R
-algebra
\Lambda=\operatorname{End}_{R}(M)
with
M
a finitely generated reflexive
R
-module, such that
\Lambda
is Cohen-Macaulay as an
R
-module and has finite global dimension.

Now let
X
be a smooth del Pezzo surface over
{\mathbb{C}}
and consider the graded ring of sections
R_{X}:=\bigoplus_{k\geq 0}\Gamma(X,\omega_{X}^{-k})
.
The affine variety
Z:=\operatorname{Spec}(R_{X})
is a cone over
X
with the unique singularity at the origin which is a classical example of a canonical Gorenstein singularity that is not terminal. The standard crepant resolution of
\operatorname{Spec}R_{X}
is given by the non-compact Calabi-Yau variety
Y:=\operatorname{Tot}(\omega_{X})
and the map
{p:Y\to Z}
contracting the zero section of the canonical bundle. It was established in
[
43
, Proposition 7.3]
that cones over del Pezzo surfaces also have non-commutative crepant resolutions. More precisely, an NCCR of
R_{X}
can be constructed as follows. One shows that on every del Pezzo surface
X
there exists a full exceptional collection
of vector bundles
{\mathbb{E}}=(E_{0},\dots,E_{n-1})
such that
\operatorname{Ext}^{l}_{X}(E_{i},E_{j}\otimes\omega_{X}^{-k})=0
for all
k\geq 0
,
l\neq 0
and any
i,j
(we refer to exceptional collections satisfying this condition as being
very strong
, see Definition
4.23
). Let
\pi:Y\to X
be the canonical projection and
{\mathcal{E}}:=\oplus_{i=0}^{n-1}E_{0}
. If
{\mathbb{E}}
is a very strong exceptional collection on
X
, then
\pi^{*}{\mathcal{E}}
is a tilting bundle in
{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(Y)
and
B({\mathbb{E}}):=\operatorname{End}_{{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(Y)}({\mathcal{E}})
is an NCCR of
R_{X}
. Our first result shows that all NCCRs of the completion
1
1

1

For simplicity we state our results in the introduction for the completion
\widehat{R_{X}}
. However these results have uncompleted versions, valid for
R_{X}
,
provided we restrict to graded NCCRs.

\widehat{R_{X}}
in fact arise in this way:

Theorem 1.1

(Theorem
5.13
, Proposition
5.14
)
.

Let
\Lambda
be an NCCR of
\widehat{R_{X}}
. Then there exists a very strong exceptional collection
{\mathbb{E}}=(E_{0},\dots,E_{n-1})
on
X
such that
\Lambda
is Morita equivalent to the completion of
\operatorname{End}_{{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(Y)}(\pi^{*}{\mathcal{E}})
, where
{\mathcal{E}}=\oplus_{i=0}^{n-1}E_{i}
.

Using the terminology introduced by Bridgeland and Stern in
[
10
]
, a very strong exceptional collection
{\mathbb{E}}=(E_{0},\dots,E_{n-1})
is the same as a
thread
of a
geometric helix

{\mathbb{H}}=\{E_{i}\}_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
, where
E_{i-n}=E_{i}\otimes\omega_{X}
, and an NCCR
B({\mathbb{E}})
is the
rolled-up helix algebra
2
2

2

The helix algebra was in fact already introduced in
[
6
]
as a
{\mathbb{Z}}
-algebra. The rolled-up helix algebra
as defined in
[
10
]
is a special case of a general construction which associates a graded algebra to a periodic
{\mathbb{Z}}
-algebra.
of
{\mathbb{H}}
.
As we explain in §
6
, Theorem
1.1
recovers in particular the main result of
[
10
]
.

As in the classical commutative setting, non-commutative crepant
resolutions are in general far from unique. Any NCCR can be modified
to obtain different NCCRs using so-called
mutations

defined by Iyama-Reiten, Iyama-Wemyss

[
19
,
20
]
. While mutations of NCCRs may be viewed
as non-commutative counterparts of flops, the nature of our understanding
of them is not the same.
Whereas the
conjecture of Bondal and Orlov
[
5
]
, predicting derived
equivalence for varieties related by a flop, remains open in general,
mutations of NCCRs naturally induce derived equivalences. On the
other hand, projective crepant resolutions are known to be connected
by sequences of flops
[
25
]
, but for NCCRs of Gorenstein
rings the analogous statement is at most a conjecture.

However for 3-dimensional
terminal

Gorenstein (equivalently, compound Du Val singularities) Iyama and
Wemyss
[
22
, Theorem 9.6]
,
[
46
, Corollary 4.5]
showed
that all NCCRs are indeed related by sequences of mutations.
Our second main result extends this to a natural class of canonical Gorenstein singularities,
namely the completed anti-canonical del Pezzo
cones

Theorem 1.2

(Theorem
11.3
)
.

All NCCRs of
\widehat{R_{X}}
for
X
a del Pezzo surface are related by sequences of mutations.

NCCRs of
\widehat{R_{X}}
, or, equivalently, by Theorem
1.1
, rolled-up helix algebras of geometric helices, are
3
-Calabi-Yau, and hence admit presentations as Jacobian algebras associated to quivers with potentials. Accordingly, mutations of NCCRs may be interpreted as mutations of quivers with potential in the sense of Derksen-Weyman-Zelevinsky
[
13
]
.

Now recall that there is yet another kind of “mutation” operation which induces a braid group action on the set of full exceptional collections (
[
3
]
, see also Proposition
4.3
).
These “braid” mutations do not generally preserve the property of an exceptional collection being very strong. However, in
[
10
]
it was shown that mutations of rolled-up helix algebras can nevertheless be realised as compositions of successive braid mutations of the underlying exceptional collections
(see Proposition
6.11
for the precise recipe).
We will thus use the term
quiver mutation
for three distinct but closely related concepts: (1)
mutations of rolled-up helix algebras in the sense of NCCRs; (2) DWZ mutations of quivers with potential and (3) the corresponding sequences of braid mutations of underlying very strong exceptional collections or geometric helices.

In light of Theorem
1.1
and the fact that mutations of NCCRs can be performed on the level of their underlying geometric helices, we prove Theorem
1.2
by showing that all such helices are related by mutations, modulo elementary operations that do not affect the corresponding NCCR:

Theorem 1.3

(Theorem
11.2
, see also

[
8
]
)
.

All geometric helices on a
del Pezzo surface
X
are related by quiver mutations, simultaneous
shifts in the derived category and tensoring by line bundles,
reordering of objects in orthogonal blocks and rotations (shifting
the labels of objects).

See §
1.3
for some context on this result and the relation with
[
8
]
.

1.2.
Method of proof

We say that a very strong exceptional collection is
minimal
if
it does not admit a quiver mutation reducing the sum of the ranks of
its objects. To prove Theorem
1.3
we obtain a
complete classification of (Gram matrices of) minimal very strong
exceptional collections. After this it remains to relate (a small
finite number of) minimal collections by sequences of mutations for
every del Pezzo surface, which we do by an explicit computer search.

A key ingredient which goes into the classification of minimal exceptional
collections is the beautiful toric framework introduced by Hille and
Perling
[
17
,
38
]
(see also
[
31
]
), which we recall and develop further
in §
8
. This machinery associates a star-shaped polygon in a
2
-dimensional lattice to an exceptional collection on a del
Pezzo surface
X
in such a way that very strong exceptional collections
correspond to convex polygons. In this paper we obtain some results about the HP-polygon which we think are new.

•

In Theorem
8.5
,
we show that the HP-polygon is in fact closely related to the dual exceptional collection.

•

Leveraging this, we also deduce that the quiver associated to the rolled-up helix algebra of
a very strong exceptional collection can be obtained very directly from the HP-polygon (see Theorem
8.9
).

•

If the HP-polygon does not have parallel edges (which is not a restriction for us as discussed in §
10.2
)
then the shape of the polygon and the associated quiver is very constrained. In fact in Proposition
10.17

we verify that an informal conjecture by Herzog
[
16
]
about the quiver shape holds in this case.

The
key point is now that quiver mutations of very strong exceptional collections can be interpreted as certain
simple geometric operations (see Figure
9.2
) on these convex polygons.
The minimal
very strong exceptional collections are then characterised
by the fact that the area of the polygon cannot be reduced by such operations.
This is equivalent to
the origin being inside a certain explicit “forbidden region” inside the polygon (see Corollary
9.16
and also Figure
9.4
for some examples).
It is this geometric constraint that we leverage to obtain our classification.

Some of our results depend on computer computations. The accompanying scripts can be found at
[
37
]
.

1.3.
Context for this paper

Theorem
1.2
was first formulated as a conjecture in
the first named author’s PhD thesis, where it was also proved in two
special cases using the method described in §
1.2
. Shorty
after we obtained a proof in the general case, using the same method, we were informed that
Pierrick Bousseau had also independently proved the conjecture, and later this
proof was published as the preprint
[
8
]
. We did prefer however to continue writing down our own proof, as it was at that stage, without consulting
[
8
]
, and this has resulted in the current paper. But now we
make a comparison of this paper with
[
8
]
.

As outlined in §
1.2
the main issue in the proof of Theorem
1.2
is the classification of the mutation classes of HP-polygons. We were unaware however that this classification was in fact already known
under the guise of the classification of so-called “T-polygons”, up to mutation (see
[
24
, Theorem 1.2]
for a case by case proof and
[
33
, Theorem 4.8]
for an intrinsic geometric proof, inspired by mirror symmetry).
So the material of §
12
and §
13
is in fact an independent reproof of this classification.

The fact that HP-polygons are T-polygons was already noted
in
[
38
, Theorem 11.3]
. In
[
8
, Definition 2.13]

a T-polygon is constructed directly from the dual of a very strong exceptional collection. We obtain the same construction for the HP-polygon in
§
8.3
. So the two polygons are in fact the same.

The classification of HP-polygons amounts to the classification of Gram matrices of very strong exceptional collections. Two exceptional collection with the same Gram matrix are related by the affine Weyl group. So it roughly remains to realise generators
of the Weyl group as compositions of mutations. In this paper we simply use an explicit
computer search for this because we again overlooked that this part is known as well (see
[
35
, Appendix A]
).
The treatment in loc. cit. depends on a case by case analysis but
Bousseau replaces it by a very elegant geometric argument in the proof of
[
8
, Theorem 2.12]
.

The authors are very grateful to Sasha Kuznetsov and Tom Bridgeland for useful discussions and for their numerous helpful comments on the relevant part of the first-named author’s PhD thesis.

All computer computations in this paper were carried out using SageMath
[
42
]
, a free open-source mathematics software system. The authors warmly thank the SageMath community and its contributors for developing and maintaining this resource.

3.
Notation and Conventions

Unless otherwise specified modules are left modules.

In principle our ground field is
{\mathbb{C}}
although we sometimes formulate algebraic statements over a ground field
k
, which we assume
to be algebraically closed.

A quiver
Q
with be a 4-tuple
(Q_{0},Q_{1},s,t)
where the
Q_{0}
is the set of vertices,
Q_{1}
is the set of arrows and
s,t:Q_{1}\rightarrow Q_{0}
are
the maps which relate an arrow to its source a target. We use functional composition in the path algebra
kQ
. In other words representations of
Q

correspond to left
kQ
-modules.
Sometimes we use the notation
Q(i,j)
for the set of arrows starting in vertex
i
and ending in vertex
j
.
Unless otherwise specified, a quiver is assumed to be finite.

A
polygon
is defined as a non-self-intersecting cycle of a finite number of non-degenerate intervals which we call
edges
.
The endpoints of the intervals are the
vertices
of the
polygon. A polygon
P
is a Jordan curve and we write
|P|
for the closure of the bounded connected component of its complement.
If the adjacent edges of a vertex are collinear then we call the vertex
straight
, otherwise we call it
non-straight
.
Sometimes we will refer to the line segments connecting the non-straight edges as
long edges
. The long edges are chains of collinear edges.
The vertices of
|P|
(in the usual sense) are the non-straight vertices of
P
and similarly the edges of
|P|
(also in the usual sense)
correspond to the long edges of
P
.

Deleting a vertex

y
from a polygon is defined as replacing the adjacent edges
[xy]
and
[yz]
by a single edge
[xz]
.

We denote the area of a region in a plane, equipped with a volume form, by
\operatorname{\Delta}(A)
. If
A=\operatorname{conv}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
then we will also write
\Delta(a_{1}\cdots a_{n})

for
\Delta(A)
.

If
A
is a graded algebra and the
(e_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
is a complete system of idempotents of degree zero then a
regrading
of
A
is a graded
algebra of the form
\tilde{A}=\operatorname{End}_{A}(\oplus_{i=1}^{n}(e_{i}A)(n_{i}))
for
n_{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. It is obvious that
\operatorname{Gr}(A)
and
\operatorname{Gr}(\tilde{A})
are equivalent where
\operatorname{Gr}(?)
denotes
the category of graded modules. In fact regrading is a particular case of
graded Morita equivalence
which is defined in a similar way as ordinary Morita equivalence.

We have
A=\oplus_{i,j}e_{j}Ae_{i}
and
\tilde{A}=\oplus_{i,j}(e_{j}Ae_{i})(n_{j}-n_{i})
. In other words, regrading amounts to shifting the pieces
e_{j}Ae_{i}
by
n_{j}-n_{i}
degrees
to the left.
If
A
has a graded presentation
kQ/(R)
and
(e_{i})_{i}
are the standard idempotents corresponding to vertices then regrading amounts to changing the
degree of an arrow
e:i\rightarrow j
to
\deg e-n_{i}+n_{j}
.

4.
Preliminaries on exceptional collections

4.1.
Exceptional collections

Let
X
be a smooth projective variety.

Definition 4.1
.

An object
E\in{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(X)
is
exceptional
if
\operatorname{Hom}^{i}(E,E)=0
for any
i\neq 0
and
\operatorname{Hom}^{0}(E,E)\cong{\mathbb{C}}
. An ordered collection
{\mathbb{E}}=(E_{0},\dots,E_{n-1})
of objects in
{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(X)
is called an
exceptional collection
if each
E_{i}
is exceptional and
\operatorname{Hom}^{\bullet}(E_{j},E_{k})=0
for all
j>k
. An exceptional collection
{\mathbb{E}}
is
full
if
{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(X)
is the smallest triangulated subcategory of
{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(X)
containing
E_{0},\dots,E_{n-1}
.

Definition 4.2
.

Let
(E,F)
be an exceptional pair. We define the left
mutation
of
(E,F)
as
(L_{E}F,E)
, where the object
L_{E}F
is given by the distinguished triangle

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}^{\bullet}(E,F)\otimes E\to F\to L_{E}F.

Dually, the right mutation of
(E,F)
is defined to be
(F,R_{F}E)
, where
R_{F}E
is given by the distinguished triangle

\displaystyle R_{F}E\to E\to\operatorname{Hom}^{\bullet}(E,F)^{\ast}\otimes F.

Proposition 4.3

(Bondal
[
3
, Assertion 2.3]
)
.

The braid group
B_{n}
on
n
strands acts on the set of exceptional collections in
{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(X)
of length
n
by mutations. More precisely, the standard generator
\sigma_{i}\in B_{n}
,
1\leq i\leq n-1
acts by

\displaystyle\sigma_{i}(E_{0},\dots,E_{n-1})=(E_{0},\dots,E_{i-2},L_{E_{i-1}}E_{i},E_{i-1},E_{i+1},\dots,E_{n-1})

Proposition 4.4

(Bondal
[
3
, Lemma 5.6]
)
.

Let
{\mathbb{E}}=(E_{0},\dots,E_{n-1})
be a full exceptional collection in
{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(X)
. Define

\widecheck{E_{i}}:=R_{E_{n-1}}\dots R_{E_{i+1}}(E_{i})

for all
0\leq i\leq n-1
. Then
\widecheck{{\mathbb{E}}}=(\widecheck{E_{n-1}},\dots,\widecheck{E_{0}})
is a full exceptional collection such that
\operatorname{Hom}_{{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(X)}(\widecheck{E_{j}},E_{i}[l])\cong{\mathbb{C}}
if
l=0,i=j
and
0
otherwise. We say that the collection
\widecheck{{\mathbb{E}}}
is
right dual
to
{\mathbb{E}}
. Likewise, the
left dual
exceptional collection of
{\mathbb{E}}
is defined as
\widecheck{{\mathbb{E}}}^{\prime}=(\widecheck{{\mathbb{E}}_{n-1}}^{\prime},\ldots,\widecheck{{\mathbb{E}}_{0}}^{\prime})
with
\widecheck{{\mathbb{E}}_{i}}^{\prime}:=L_{{\mathbb{E}}_{0}}\dots L_{{\mathbb{E}}_{i-1}}({\mathbb{E}}_{i})
. One has
\operatorname{Hom}_{{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(X)}(E_{i},\widecheck{E_{j}}^{\prime}[l])\cong{\mathbb{C}}
if
l=0,i=j
and
0
otherwise.

Remark 4.5
.

It is an elementary exercise in Serre duality that the left and right dual of an exceptional collection
are related by the Serre functor.

Remark 4.6
.

Let the notations be as in Proposition
4.4
. Then one may check that
\widecheck{E_{n-1}}=E_{n-1}
and
\widecheck{E_{0}}=\omega_{X}^{-1}\otimes E_{0}[-\dim X]
. Likewise, for the left dual exceptional collection one has
\widecheck{E_{0}}^{\prime}=E_{0}
and
\widecheck{E_{n-1}}^{\prime}=\omega_{X}\otimes E_{n-1}[\dim X]
.

For use below we make the following definition.

Definition 4.7
.

The
Gram matrix

M
of an exceptional collection
{\mathbb{E}}
is the
n\times n
matrix such that
M_{ij}=\chi_{X}(E_{i},E_{j})
.

4.2.
Exceptional collections on del Pezzo surfaces

Here we recall some classical results on exceptional objects and exceptional collections on del Pezzo surfaces, mostly following the works of Gorodentsev and Kuleshov-Orlov
[
30
]
. Throughout
X
denotes a smooth del Pezzo surface.

Definition 4.8
.

Let
{\mathcal{F}}
be a coherent sheaf. The
degree
of
{\mathcal{F}}
is defined by

d({\mathcal{F}})=c_{1}({\mathcal{F}})\cdot(-K_{X})

For a torsion-free sheaf
{\mathcal{F}}\in\mathop{\text{{coh}}}(X)
we define the
slope
of
{\mathcal{F}}
as

\mu({\mathcal{F}})=\frac{d({\mathcal{F}})}{r({\mathcal{F}})}

and denote

s({\mathcal{F}})=\frac{c_{1}({\mathcal{F}})}{r({\mathcal{F}})}\in\operatorname{Pic}(X)\otimes{\mathbb{Q}}

For
{\mathcal{F}}
a torsion sheaf we set
\mu({\mathcal{F}})=+\infty
.

For two torsion-free sheaves
{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}}
we will also use the notations

\displaystyle s({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}}):=s({\mathcal{F}})-s({\mathcal{E}})

\displaystyle\mu({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}}):=(-K_{X})\cdot s({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}})=\mu({\mathcal{F}})-\mu({\mathcal{E}}).

The following can be derived from the Riemann-Roch formula:

Proposition 4.9
.

Let
{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}}
be exceptional torsion-free sheaves on
X
. Then

(4.1)

\chi({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}})=\frac{r({\mathcal{F}})r({\mathcal{E}})}{2}\bigg(s({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}})^{2}+\mu({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}})+\frac{1}{r({\mathcal{E}})^{2}}+\frac{1}{r({\mathcal{F}})^{2}}\bigg).

If in addition
({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}})
form an exceptional pair, then

(4.2)

\chi({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}})=r({\mathcal{E}})d({\mathcal{F}})-r({\mathcal{F}})d({\mathcal{E}})=r({\mathcal{E}})r({\mathcal{F}})\mu({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}}).

The following proposition claims that the only exceptional objects in derived categories of del Pezzo surfaces are shifts of exceptional vector bundles and torsion sheaves supported on
(-1)
-curves.

Proposition 4.10

(Kuleshov-Orlov,
[
30
, Propositions 2.9, 2.10]
)
.

Let
X
be a del Pezzo surface.

(1)

An object
A\in{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(X)
is exceptional if and only if it is isomorphic to a shift
{\mathcal{F}}[i]
of some exceptional sheaf
{\mathcal{F}}
on
X
,
i\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.

(2)

Let
{\mathcal{F}}
be an exceptional sheaf on
X
. Then
{\mathcal{F}}
is either a vector bundle or a torsion sheaf of the form
{\mathcal{O}}_{C}(d)
, where
C
is a
(-1)
-curve, i.e. an irreducible rational curve
C
with
C^{2}=C\cdot K_{X}=-1
.

Exceptional pairs of sheaves are classified into three types and the type can be determined by the order of their slopes.

Proposition 4.11

(Gorodentsev,
[
15
, Propositions 5.3.1, 5.3.3]
, see also Kuleshov-Orlov
[
30
, Corollary 2.11]
)
.

Let
({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}})
be an exceptional pair of sheaves on a del Pezzo surface. Then
\operatorname{Ext}^{2}({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}})=0
and at most one of the spaces
\operatorname{Ext}^{i}({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}})
with
i=0,1
is non-zero. Moreover,

(1)

\operatorname{Hom}({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}})\neq 0
if and only if
\mu({\mathcal{E}})<\mu({\mathcal{F}})
(in this case we say that
({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}})
is a
\operatorname{Hom}
-pair
),

(2)

\operatorname{Ext}^{1}({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}})\neq 0
if and only if
\mu({\mathcal{E}})>\mu({\mathcal{F}})
(in this case we say that
({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}})
is a
\operatorname{Ext}
-pair
),

(3)

\operatorname{Hom}({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}})=\operatorname{Ext}^{1}({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}})=0
if and only if
\mu({\mathcal{E}})=\mu({\mathcal{F}})
(a
null-pair
or an
orthogonal pair
).

Theorem 4.12

(Gorodentsev
[
15
]
, 2.4)
.

An exceptional vector bundle
{\mathcal{F}}
on a del Pezzo surface
X
is
\mu
-stable (Mumford-Takemoto stable with respect to
-K_{X}
), i.e.
\mu({\mathcal{F}}^{\prime})<\mu({\mathcal{F}})
for any proper non-zero subbundle
{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathcal{F}}
.

Theorem 4.13

(Kuleshov-Orlov
[
30
]
, Theorem 5.2)
.

Any rigid bundle
{\mathcal{T}}
on a del Pezzo surface
X
(i.e.
\operatorname{Ext}_{X}^{1}({\mathcal{T}},{\mathcal{T}})=0
) is a direct sum of exceptional bundles.

Convention 4.2.1
.

We adopt the following notations.
If
(E,F)
is an exceptional pair of sheaves on a del Pezzo surface
X
, then
L_{E}F=F^{\prime}[a]
and
R_{F}E=E^{\prime}[b]
, where
F^{\prime},E^{\prime}
are sheaves and
a,b\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. We denote
\widetilde{L_{E}}F=F^{\prime}
and
\widetilde{R_{F}}E=E^{\prime}
.
Hence if we define

\displaystyle\tilde{\sigma}_{i}(E_{0},\dots,E_{n-1})=(E_{0},\dots,\widetilde{L_{E_{i-1}}}E_{i},E_{i-1}\dots,E_{n-1}),

\displaystyle\tilde{\sigma}_{i}^{-1}(E_{0},\dots,E_{n-1})=(E_{0},\dots,E_{i},\widetilde{R_{E_{i}}}E_{i-1},\dots,E_{n-1}).

then we obtain a braid group action on the
set of exceptional collections of sheaves on
X
.
In addition, we define

\displaystyle\tilde{\sigma}_{n}(E_{0},\dots,E_{n-1})=(E_{n-1}\otimes\omega_{X},E_{1},\dots,E_{n-2},\widetilde{L_{E_{n-1}}}(E_{0}\otimes\omega_{X}^{-1})),

\displaystyle\tilde{\sigma}_{n}^{-1}(E_{0},\dots,E_{n-1})=((\widetilde{R_{E_{0}\otimes\omega_{X}^{-1}}}E_{n-1})\otimes\omega_{X},E_{1},\dots,E_{n-2},E_{0}\otimes\omega_{X}^{-1}).

Here
\tilde{\sigma}_{n}^{\pm 1}
is the composition of the right rotation (see Definition
4.29
), followed by
\tilde{\sigma}_{n-1}^{\pm 1}
and the left rotation.

4.3.
The symmetry group of a del Pezzo surface

It is natural to ask to what extent an exceptional collection is determined by its Gram matrix (see Definition
4.7
). In Proposition
4.22

below we answer this question for del Pezzo surfaces.

Definition 4.14
.

For
X
a del Pezzo surface, denote the stabiliser of the canonical class
K_{X}
in
O(\operatorname{Pic}(X))
by

S(X):=\{f\in O(\operatorname{Pic}(X)\,|\,f(K_{X})=K_{X}\}.

A
root
is an element
\rho
of
\operatorname{Pic}(X)
such that
K_{X}\cdot\rho=0
and
\rho\cdot\rho=-2
.

For
X_{n}=\operatorname{Bl}_{p_{1},\dots,p_{n}}{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
with
0\leq n\leq 8
we use the standard basis

(4.3)

\displaystyle(H,E_{1},\dots,E_{n})

of
\operatorname{Pic}(X_{n})
, where
H
is the pullback of the line class
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{2}}(1)
and
E_{i}
are the classes of the exceptional divisors. In this basis the intersection form is given by

H^{2}=1,\qquad E_{i}^{2}=-1,\qquad H\cdot E_{i}=0,\qquad E_{i}\cdot E_{j}=0\ \ (i\neq j),

and the canonical class is

K_{X}=-3H+E_{1}+\cdots+E_{n},

so that
K_{X}^{2}=9-n
.

For
X={\mathbb{P}}^{1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
we use the basis
(F_{1},F_{2})
given by the classes of the two rulings. In this basis one has
F_{1}^{2}=F_{2}^{2}=0,F_{1}\cdot F_{2}=1
and
K_{X}=-2F_{1}-2F_{2}
.

Theorem 4.15

(
[
34
, Theorem 23.9]
)
.

For
X=\operatorname{Bl}_{r}({\mathbb{P}}^{2})
with
3\leq r\leq 8
the set of roots
R_{r}
forms a root system equal to
A_{1}\times A_{2},A_{4},D_{5},E_{6},E_{7},E_{8}
respectively.
For
n\geq 3
, the simple roots may be taken to be

\alpha_{1}=H-E_{1}-E_{2}-E_{3},\qquad\alpha_{i}=E_{i-1}-E_{i}\ \ (2\leq i\leq n).

Moreover the group
S(X)
coincides with the corresponding Weyl group
W(R_{r})
.

It turns out that Theorem
4.15
also holds for the remaining del Pezzo surfaces. This is e.g. stated without proof in
[
32
, §2.3]
.

Lemma 4.16
.

For
X=\operatorname{Bl}_{2}({\mathbb{P}}^{2})
and
X={\mathbb{P}}^{1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
one has
S(X)=W(A_{1})\cong{\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}

and the simple roots may respectively be taken to be
F_{1}-F_{2}
and
E_{1}-E_{2}
.
For
X={\mathbb{F}}_{1}
and
X={\mathbb{P}}^{2}
the group
S(X)
is trivial.

Proof.

First consider the case
X=\operatorname{Bl}_{2}({\mathbb{P}}^{2})
. Consider the basis
H,E_{1},E_{2}
of
\operatorname{Pic}(X)
as in
4.3
. By definition
f\in S(X)
is an automorphism of
\operatorname{Pic}(X)
preserving the intersection form and the canonical class
K_{X}
, thus it should also preserve the set
{\mathcal{C}}:=\{C\in\operatorname{Pic}(X)\,|\,C^{2}=-1,C\cdot K_{X}=-1\}
of the classes of
(-1)
-curves. Let
C=aH+bE_{1}+cE_{2}
be in
{\mathcal{C}}
. Since
K_{X}=-3H+E_{1}+E_{2}
, one has
C^{2}=a^{2}-b^{2}-c^{2}=-1
and
C\cdot K_{X}=-3a-b-c=-1
. It is a straightforward exercise to show that the only integer solutions to this system of equations are
(a,b,c)=(0,1,0),(0,0,1)
and
(1,-1,-1)
, i.e. the set
{\mathcal{C}}
has
3
elements
E_{1}
,
E_{2}
and
L:=H-E_{1}-E_{2}
(the strict transform of the line through the
2
points of the blow-up). Hence
f
induces a permutation on this set of 3 elements. Moreover, since the
3
elements
E_{1},E_{2},L
generate the whole
\operatorname{Pic}(X)
, this permutation determines an automorphism uniquely. We have
E_{1}\cdot E_{2}=0
,
E_{1}\cdot L=1
and
E_{2}\cdot L=1
, so the permutation induced by
f
can either fix both
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
or swap them. Swapping
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
is exactly the reflection with respect to the root
E_{1}-E_{2}
. For
X={\mathbb{F}}_{1}
one has
\operatorname{Pic}(X)={\mathbb{Z}}H\oplus{\mathbb{Z}}E
, where
E
is the unique
(-1)
-class and
K_{X}=-3H=E
, so a similar argument shows that any
f\in S(X)
is forced to be the identity. For
X={\mathbb{P}}^{1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
the orthogonal complement to
K_{X}
is generated by the simple root
F_{1}-F_{2}
, where
F_{1},F_{2}
are the classes of the two ruling, hence any
f\in S(X)
can either be the identity or swap
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
. For
X={\mathbb{P}}^{2}
the claim is trivial since
\operatorname{Pic}(X)
has rank
1
.
∎

Definition 4.17
.

A
numerical full exceptional collection
on
X
is an ordered basis
(e_{0},\dots,e_{n-1})
in
K_{0}^{\operatorname{num}}(X)
such that
\chi(e_{i},e_{i})=1
,
\chi(e_{i},e_{j})=0
for
i>j
.

Proposition 4.18
.

Let
(e_{0},\ldots,e_{n-1})
be a full exceptional collection in
K_{0}^{\operatorname{num}}(X)
such that
r(e_{i})\geq 0
for all
i
. Then there exists a unique
full exceptional collection
(E_{0},\ldots,E_{n-1})
of sheaves on
X
such that
[E_{i}]=e_{i}
for all
i
.

Proof.

By
[
38
, Theorem 10.9]
any numerical full exceptional collection can be transformed by mutations into a collection consisting of objects of rank
1
. Such a collection is clearly realised by a collection of line bundles on
X
.

Applying the inverse of the corresponding sequence of mutations on the level of derived categories, we conclude that every numerical full exceptional collection on a del Pezzo surface is realised by an actual full exceptional collection. Moreover, such a collection is unique up to even shifts (see e.g.
[
23
, §1.3]
). Since
we require that the lift consist of sheaves, this fixes the shift.
∎

Remark 4.19
.

It is not true that every numerical exceptional class can be lifted to an exceptional object. The class
18[O]-9[O(1)]+[O(2)]
in
K_{0}^{\operatorname{num}}({\mathbb{P}}^{2})
is numerically exceptional but its rank is 10, which is not a Markov number. So it does not lift by
[
40
]
.

The following proposition shows that the exact sequence in the statement of
[
29
, Lemma 2.10]
is canonically split (this can also be extracted from the proof in loc. cit.).

Proposition 4.20
.

Let
S(X)
act on
K_{0}^{\operatorname{num}}(X)
in the following way. First identify

h:K_{0}^{\operatorname{num}}(X)\rightarrow{\mathbb{Z}}\oplus\operatorname{Pic}(X)\oplus{\mathbb{Z}}:e\mapsto(r(e),c_{1}(e),\chi(e))

and then for
s\in S(X)
,
e\in K_{0}^{\operatorname{num}}(X)
put
s(e)=(r(e),sc_{1}(e),\chi(e))
. This action is compatible with the Euler form and moreover it preserves
the rank and degree of a class and hence also its slope.

Proof.

That the action preserves the rank is straight from the definition. The degree of an object
e
is
(-K_{X})\cdot c_{1}(e)
which is clearly also preserved.
For any
e,f\in K^{\operatorname{num}}_{0}(X)
one obtains from Riemann-Roch

(4.4)

\chi(e,f)=r(e)\chi(f)+r(f)\chi(e)-r(e)r(f)-c_{1}(e)\cdot c_{1}(f)+r(f)(K_{X}\cdot c_{1}(e)).

Since
S(X)
preserves the intersection form and
K_{X}
, we get
\chi(e,f)=\chi(s(e),s(f))
for any
s\in S(X)
.
∎

Corollary 4.21
.

The action of
S(X)
on
K_{0}^{\operatorname{num}}(X)
induces an action on the set of full exceptional collections consisting of sheaves.

Proof.

The corresponding numerical statement follows from Proposition
4.20
.
We then invoke Proposition
4.18
.
∎

Proposition 4.22
.

Let
{\mathbb{E}}
and
{\mathbb{E}}^{\prime}
be two full exceptional collections of sheaves on
X
having the same Gram matrix. Then
{\mathbb{E}}
and
{\mathbb{E}}^{\prime}
are related by tensoring with a line bundle and the action of the symmetry group
S(X)
.

Proof.

Sending
{\mathbb{E}}
to
{\mathbb{E}}^{\prime}
defines an
\alpha
automorphism of
K_{0}^{\operatorname{num}}(X)
compatible with the Euler form. By
[
29
, Lemma 2.10]
together with the splitting
constructed in Proposition
4.20

of the exact sequence given in loc. cit. we obtain that
\alpha
can be written as a composition of an element of
S(X)
, tensoring with a line bundle and possibly
a global sign change. However this last factor cannot occur since both
{\mathbb{E}}
and
{\mathbb{E}}^{\prime}
consist of sheaves and hence they have total rank
>0
.
This
gives what we want.
∎

4.4.
Geometric helices on del Pezzo surfaces

Definition 4.23
.

We say that a full exceptional collection of sheaves
{\mathbb{E}}=(E_{0},\dots,E_{n-1})
is
very strong
if it satisfies

(4.5)

\operatorname{Ext}^{l}_{X}(E_{i},E_{j}\otimes\omega_{X}^{-k})=0\text{ for all }k\geq 0,\ l\neq 0,\ i,\ j.

Remark 4.24
.

An exceptional collection
({\mathcal{E}}_{0},\dots,{\mathcal{E}}_{n-1})
is very strong if and only if

\mu({\mathcal{E}}_{0})\leq\dots\leq\mu({\mathcal{E}}_{n-1})\leq\mu({\mathcal{E}}_{0}\otimes\omega_{X}^{-1})=\mu({\mathcal{E}}_{0})+K_{X}^{2}.

The “only if” direction follows immediately from Proposition
4.11
.
For the “if” direction we first note that (
4.5
) for
l=2
follows from Serre
duality and Theorem
4.12
.
For
l=1
we observe that if
\mu(F)>\mu(E)
then by restriction to a smooth anti-canonical divisor
as in the proof of
[
30
, Lemma 3.7]
we have a surjection
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(E,F\otimes\omega_{X})\rightarrow\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(E,F)
. This allows us to go from
k
to
k+1
in (
4.5
), which reduces us
to the case
k=0
where we can invoke Proposition
4.11
.

From this observation we obtain the following corollary.

Corollary 4.25
.

The action exhibited in Corollary
4.21
restrict to an action of
S(X)
on very strong exceptional collections.

Proof.

This follows from Remark
4.24
together with the fact that as stated in Proposition
4.20
the action
of
S(X)
on
K_{0}^{\operatorname{num}}(X)
preserves slopes.
∎

Proposition 4.26
.

Let
{\mathbb{E}}=(E_{0},\dots,E_{n-1})
be a very strong exceptional collection on a del Pezzo surface
X
. Then each
E_{i}
,
i=0,\dots,n-1
is a vector bundle.

Proof.

By Proposition
4.10
every exceptional sheaf is either a vector bundle or of the form
{\mathcal{O}}_{C}(d)
, where
C\subset X
is a
(-1)
-curve. Suppose
E_{i}={\mathcal{O}}_{C}(d)
for some
i
. By assumption the collection
{\mathbb{E}}
is full, so it cannot consist entirely of torsion sheaves. Hence without loss of generality we can assume that
E_{i+1}=E
is a vector bundle. By Lemma 3.1 of
[
30
]
the restriction
E|_{C}
of an exceptional vector bundle
E
to a
(-1)
-curve
C
has the form
n{\mathcal{O}}_{C}(t)\oplus m{\mathcal{O}}_{C}(t+1)
. Then the condition (
4.5
) implies that for all
k\geq 0
we have

\displaystyle 0=\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{X}(E_{i},E_{i+1}\otimes\omega_{X}^{-k})=\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{X}({\mathcal{O}}_{C}(d),E\otimes\omega_{X}^{-k})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Hom}_{C}({\mathcal{O}}_{C}(d-k-1),E|_{C})=\operatorname{Hom}_{S}({\mathcal{O}}_{C}(d-k-1),n{\mathcal{O}}_{C}(t)\oplus m{\mathcal{O}}_{C}(t+1))

But for
k
large enough the last
\operatorname{Hom}
is clearly non-zero, so we get a contradiction.
∎

A full exceptional collection of sheaves
{\mathbb{E}}=(E_{0},\dots,E_{n-1})
is very strong if and only if it generates a
geometric helix
in the sense of
[
10
]
:

Definition 4.27
.

Let
{\mathbb{E}}=(E_{0},\dots,E_{n-1})
be an exceptional collection. We define the corresponding infinite
helix

{\mathbb{H}}=\{E_{j}\}_{j\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
generated by
{\mathbb{E}}
by setting
E_{i-n}=E_{i}\otimes\omega_{X}
for all
i\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. An exceptional collection
(E_{j},\dot{,}E_{j+n-1})
for any
j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
is called a
thread
of the helix
{\mathbb{H}}
. A helix
{\mathbb{H}}=\{E_{j}\}_{j\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
is said to be
geometric
if
\operatorname{Hom}(E_{i},E_{j}[k])=0
for all
i,j
and
k\neq 0
.

Remark 4.28
.

Definition
4.27
does not require a helix to consist of sheaves. However, a geometric helix on a del Pezzo surface automatically consists sheaves, modulo a simultaneous shift. Indeed, suppose
E
and
F[k]
, with
E
and
F
sheaves and
k\neq 0
, are two exceptional objects in the same thread of a geometric helix. Since
\mu(E\otimes\omega^{-1}_{X})=K^{2}_{X}+\mu(E)>\mu(E)
, one has either
\mu(F)>\mu(E)
or
\mu(F)<\mu(E\otimes\omega^{-1}_{X}
(note that
\mu(F)=\mu(E)
automatically implies the second case and
\mu(F)=\mu(E\otimes\omega^{-1}_{X})
automatically implies the first). If
\mu(F)>\mu(E)
, Proposition
4.11
implies that
\operatorname{Ext}_{X}^{-k}(E,F[k])=\operatorname{Hom}_{X}(E,F)\neq 0
which contradicts the helix being geometric. Similarly, if
\mu(F)<\mu(E\otimes\omega^{-1}_{X})
, then
\operatorname{Ext}^{k}_{X}(F[k],E\otimes\omega^{-1}_{X})=\operatorname{Hom}_{X}(F,E\otimes\omega^{-1}_{X})\neq 0
.

In what follows we will usually talk of exceptional collections rather than the helices they generate, but nevertheless keep in mind that the resulting graded algebra is independent on the choice of a thread in the helix, up to regrading (see §
3
) which does not change the category of graded modules. Therefore we are always allowed to “rotate” an exceptional collection:

Definition 4.29
.

The
left rotation
of an exceptional collection
{\mathbb{E}}=(E_{0},\dots,E_{n-1})
is defined to be
(E_{n-1}\otimes\omega_{X},E_{0},\dots,E_{n-2})
. Similarly, the
right rotation
of
{\mathbb{E}}
is
(E_{1},\dots,E_{n-1},E_{0}\otimes\omega_{X}^{-1})
. By
[
3
, §4]
the left rotation can be achieved by a composition of left mutations as
(\widetilde{L_{E_{0}}}\dots\widetilde{L_{E_{n-2}}}E_{n-1},E_{0},\dots,E_{n-2})
. Likewise, the the right rotation can be expressed as
(E_{1},\dots,E_{n-1},\widetilde{R_{E_{n-1}}}\dots\widetilde{R_{E_{1}}}E_{0})
.

5.
NCCRs and very strong exceptional collections

5.1.
Non-commutative crepant resolutions

Definition 5.1

(Van den Bergh
[
43
]
)
.

Let
R
be a normal Gorenstein domain. An
R
-algebra
\Lambda
is a
non-commutative crepant resolution
(NCCR) of
R
if

(1)

\Lambda=\operatorname{End}_{R}(M)
for some non-zero
M\in\operatorname{ref}(R)
, where
\operatorname{ref}(R)
denotes the category of finitely generated reflexive
R
-modules.

(2)

\Lambda
is Cohen-Macaulay as an
R
-module and has finite global dimension.

We will mainly use this definition in two situations:

•

“the complete context”
:
R
is a complete local ring.

•

“the graded context”
:
R
is a graded ring of the form
k+R_{1}+R_{2}+\cdots
and
M
is a graded
R
-module.

Convention 5.2
.

If we are in the complete context then we implicitly assume that all operations are completed. This applies for example to the path algebra of a quiver.
If we are in the graded context then we implicitly assume that all constructions take the grading (which is extra data) into account.

Definition 5.3
.

Being in the graded or complete context we say that
\Lambda=\operatorname{End}_{R}(M)
is
basic
if all simples are one-dimensional, or equivalently, if the indecomposable summands of
M

occur with multiplicity one.

Remark 5.4
.

In the graded or complete context all NCCRs are Morita equivalent to a basic one.

Remark 5.5
.

If
\Lambda
,
\Gamma
are basic graded NCCRs, then
\Lambda
,
\Gamma
are graded Morita equivalent if and only if they can be obtained from each other
by regrading (see §
3
).

5.2.
From very strong exceptional collections to NCCRs

Let
X
be a del Pezzo surface,

Y=\operatorname{Tot}(\omega_{X})
, and let
\pi:Y\to X
be the projection. Let
{\mathbb{E}}=(E_{0},\dots,E_{n-1})
be a very strong exceptional collection of sheaves and set
{\mathcal{E}}=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{d}E_{n-1}
. Define

(5.1)

B({\mathbb{E}}):=\operatorname{End}_{{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(Y)}(\pi^{*}({\mathcal{E}})).

which we view as a graded algebra (the grading being inherited from the
G_{m}
-action on
Y
).

If
{\mathbb{H}}
is a geometric helix generated by
{\mathbb{E}}
, then by
[
10
, Theorem 3.6]

B({\mathbb{E}})
is isomorphic to the
rolled-up helix algebra

B({\mathbb{H}})
, which is defined to be the graded subalgebra of the graded algebra
\bigoplus_{k\geq 0}\prod_{j-i=k}\operatorname{Hom}_{X}(E_{i},E_{j})
consisting of elements invariant under the
{\mathbb{Z}}
-action induced by
-\otimes\omega_{X}
.
Hence following Bridgeland and Stern we sometimes refer to
B({\mathbb{E}})
as defined in (
5.1
) as a rolled-up helix algebra as well.

Theorem 5.6

(
[
43
, §7]
, see also the proof of Theorem
5.13
)
.

The object
\pi^{*}({\mathcal{E}})
is a tilting bundle in
{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(Y)
and
B({\mathbb{E}})
is an NCCR of the affine cone
R_{X}
given by
\omega_{X}
.

Hence, we have an equivalence of categories

(5.2)

\operatorname{RHom}_{{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(Y)}(\pi^{*}({\mathcal{E}}),-):{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(Y)\to{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(B({\mathbb{E}})).

In particular,
B({\mathbb{E}})
is
3
-Calabi–Yau (see
[
9
, Proposition 4.1]
,
[
10
, Theorem 3.6]
)
and can be encoded as a Jacobi algebra. We will discuss this in the next section.

5.3.
Relation with Jacobi algebras

Definition 5.7
.

Let
Q
be a quiver and let
kQ
its path algebra. A
potential
on
Q
is an element

W\in kQ/[kQ,kQ].

The
Jacobi algebra
associated to
(Q,W)
is

{\mathcal{J}}(Q,W):=kQ/(\partial W),

where
(\partial W)
denotes the two-sided ideal generated by the cyclic derivatives
\partial_{a}W
for all arrows
a\in Q_{1}
.

Remark 5.8
.

In the complete context (see §
5.1
) by our conventions
kQ
is completed (this is the setting of
[
13
]
) and so is the space of commutators

[kQ,kQ]
. This implies that
W
is represented by
a (usually) infinite convergent linear combination of cyclic paths.
In the graded context we assume that
Q
is graded (i.e. the arrows are equipped with a degree) and
W
is homogeneous.

Now let
\Lambda
be a
3
-Calabi-Yau algebra and assume that we are in the complete or graded context. Then
[
2
, Theorem 3.1]
(see also
[
44
, Theorems A,B]
) implies that
\Lambda
can be represented as the Jacobi algebra
{\mathcal{J}}(Q,W)
associated to a quiver
Q
with a homogeneous potential
W
in the sense of Definition
5.7
.

Remark 5.9
.

Note that
Q
is the
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}
-graph of
\Lambda
. Let
(S_{i})_{i=0}^{n-1}
be the non-isomorphic simples of
\Lambda
, where in the graded
context,
S_{i}
is taken to be in degree zero. Then
Q=\{0,\ldots,n-1\}
and the arrows from
i
to
j
correspond to a basis of
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\Lambda}(S_{i},S_{j})
.

If
(\widecheck{E_{j}})_{j}
denotes the left dual collection then the equivalence (
5.2
) maps
\iota_{*}(\widecheck{E_{j}})
, where
\iota:X\hookrightarrow Y
is the inclusion of the zero-section, to the simple
B({\mathbb{E}})
-modules (see also
[
9
, §3]
).
Using some elementary algebra based on Remark
5.9
this leads to the following description of
Q
:

Proposition 5.10
.

The vertices of
Q
are in bijection with objects of the exceptional collection
{\mathbb{E}}
, and the arrows are obtained from the left/right dual (see Remark
4.5
)
exceptional collection
\widecheck{{\mathbb{E}}}=(\widecheck{E_{n-1}},\ldots\widecheck{E_{0}})
(see Proposition
4.4
) as follows:

(1)

There are no loops, i.e. there are no arrows
i\rightarrow j
if
i=j
.

(2)

If
i<j
then the arrows correspond to a basis for
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(\widecheck{E_{j}},\widecheck{E_{i}})^{*}
. Those arrows have degree zero.

(3)

if
i>j
then the arrows correspond to a basis for
\operatorname{Ext}^{2}(\widecheck{E_{i}},\widecheck{E_{j}})
. Those arrows have degree one.

Moreover the superpotential
W
has degree one (for the construction of the superpotential see
[
26
, Theorem 6.3]
).

For use below we note that
Q
can also be obtained from
\widecheck{{\mathbb{E}}}
in the following “numerical” way.

Lemma 5.11
.

Let
Y\subset X
be a smooth anti-canonical divisor. Then the number of arrows
i\rightarrow j
in
Q
is equal to
\chi_{Y}(\widecheck{E_{i,Y}},\widecheck{E_{j,Y}})
, with
the convention that a negative number corresponds to arrows from
j
to
i
.

Proof.

Since
A({\mathbb{E}}):=\operatorname{End}(\oplus_{i}E_{i})
has global dimension two (see e.g.
[
11
, Lemma A.3]
) the only possible
\operatorname{Ext}
’s between the simples
of
A({\mathbb{E}})
are in degree
\leq 2
. The simples of
A({\mathbb{E}})
correspond to the elements of
\widecheck{{\mathbb{E}}}
. Hence we have
\operatorname{Ext}^{k}(\widecheck{E_{i}},\widecheck{E_{j}})=0

for
k=0
and
k\geq 3
. By Proposition
4.11
there is at most a single non-zero
\operatorname{Ext}
between any
\widecheck{E_{i}}
,
\widecheck{E_{j}}
. It follows
that the number of arrows
i\rightarrow j
in
Q
(counting arrows from
j\rightarrow i
as negative) is equal to

\begin{cases}0&\text{if $i=j,$}\\
-\chi(\widecheck{E_{j}},\widecheck{E_{i}})&\text{if $i<j,$}\\
\chi(\widecheck{E_{i}},\widecheck{E_{j}})&\text{if $i>j$.}\end{cases}

By
[
36
, Lemma 3.12]
this may be rewritten as

\begin{cases}0&\text{if $i=j$}\\
-\chi_{Y}(\widecheck{E_{j,Y}},\widecheck{E_{i,Y}})&\text{if $i<j$}\\
\chi_{Y}(\widecheck{E_{i,Y}},\widecheck{E_{j,Y}})&\text{if $i>j$}\end{cases}

We finish with the observation that
\chi_{Y}(\widecheck{E_{i,Y}},\widecheck{E_{i,Y}})=0
and
\chi_{Y}(\widecheck{E_{i,Y}},\widecheck{E_{j,Y}})=-\chi_{Y}(\widecheck{E_{j,Y}},\widecheck{E_{i,Y}})
.
∎

Remark 5.12
.

It follows from the explicit description of
Q
that it has no
loops or oriented 2-cycles. This was first observed in
[
10
, Proposition 7.5]
. An independent proof was given in
[
11
, Theorem 4.2.2]
.

5.4.
From NCCRs to very strong exceptional collections

In this section we prove the following result:

Theorem 5.13
.

Let
X
be a del Pezzo surface and
R_{X}:=\bigoplus_{k\leq 0}\Gamma(X,\omega_{X}^{\otimes k})
.
If
\Lambda
is a basic (see Definition
5.3
)
graded NCCR of
R_{X}
then there exists a
very strong exceptional collection
{\mathbb{E}}=(E_{0},\dots,E_{n-1})
on
X
such that
\Lambda
is obtained by regrading (see §
3
)
from
\operatorname{End}_{{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(Y)}(\pi^{*}({\mathcal{E}}))
, where
{\mathcal{E}}=\oplus_{i=0}^{n-1}E_{i}
and
\pi:Y\to X
the canonical projection from
Y=\operatorname{Tot}(\omega_{X})
.

Using the following proposition one may also use this result to describe NCCRs of
\widehat{R_{X}}
.

Proposition 5.14
.

Let
R=k+R_{1}+R_{2}+\cdots
be a three-dimensional graded normal Gorenstein domain with an isolated singularity. Then a NCCR of the completion
\widehat{R}
is the completion of a graded NCCR of
R
.

First we require some preparation.
Let
X
be a smooth projective variety and
{\mathcal{L}}\in\operatorname{Pic}(X)
an ample line bundle. Let
R:=R_{X}:=\Gamma^{*}(X,{\mathcal{L}})=\bigoplus_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\Gamma(X,{\mathcal{L}}^{\otimes k})
be the corresponding graded ring of sections. It is known that
R
is a finitely generated normal
k
-algebra. The affine variety
Z:=\operatorname{Spec}(R_{X})
is a cone over
X
with the unique singularity at the origin. For
M\in\operatorname{gr}(R_{X})
denote by
{\mathcal{M}}=\widetilde{M}\in\mathop{\text{{coh}}}(X)
the corresponding coherent sheaf on
X
.

Proposition 5.15
.

Let
{\mathcal{M}}\in\mathop{\text{{coh}}}(X)
be such that
{\mathcal{M}}=\widetilde{M}
, where
M
is a reflexive
R
-module with
\operatorname{End}_{R}(M)
Cohen-Macaulay. Assume that
{\mathcal{M}}
is a vector bundle. Then

\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{X}({\mathcal{M}},{\mathcal{M}}\otimes_{X}{\mathcal{L}}^{\otimes k})=0

for any
0<i<\dim(X)
,
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. In particular, if
\dim X\geq 2
,
{\mathcal{M}}
is rigid.

Proof.

For any
0<i<\dim(X)
we have

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{X}({\mathcal{M}},{\mathcal{M}}\otimes_{X}{\mathcal{L}}^{\otimes k})=\bigoplus_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}H^{i}(X,\HHom_{X}({\mathcal{M}},{\mathcal{M}})\otimes_{X}{\mathcal{L}}^{\otimes k})

\displaystyle=H^{i+1}_{R_{\geq 1}}(\Gamma^{*}(X,\HHom_{X}({\mathcal{M}},{\mathcal{M}}))=H^{i+1}_{R_{\geq 1}}(\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(M,M))=0,

where in the first equality we used that
{\mathcal{M}}
is a vector bundle and in the last equality we used
i+1<\dim(X)+1=\dim(R)
and the fact that
\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(M,M)
is Cohen-Macaulay (also see
[
14
, Theorem A4.1]
for the statement relating global and local cohomology).
3
3

3

Alternatively to prove Proposition
5.15
one may use that
\widetilde{\operatorname{End}_{R}(M)}\cong{\mathcal{E}nd}_{X}({\mathcal{M}})
is arithmetically Cohen–Macaulay by
[
1
, Proposition 1.2]
. We thank Alexander Kuznetsov for pointing this out to us.

∎

Remark 5.16
.

If
\dim X=2
, the condition that
{\mathcal{M}}
is a vector bundle is automatic, because in this case reflexive sheaves are locally free and
\widetilde{(-)}
preserves reflexivity.

Now assume that
X
is a del Pezzo surface and let
{\mathcal{L}}=\omega_{X}^{-1}
. Then
R
is Gorenstein. Let
{M\in\operatorname{ref}(R)}
be a reflexive module such that
\Lambda:=\operatorname{End}_{R}(M)
is an NCCR of
R
. The following theorem implies that
M
is rigid.

Theorem 5.17

(Iyama-Reiten,
[
19
, Theorem 8.15]
)
.

Let
R
be a three-dimensional normal Gorenstein domain with isolated singularities and let
M\in\operatorname{ref}(R)
a reflexive module such that
\operatorname{End}_{R}(M)
is Cohen-Macaulay. Then
M
is rigid, i.e.
\operatorname{Ext}_{R}^{1}(M,M)=0
.

For convenience of the reader we give a direct proof.

Proof.

Consider an exact sequence of
R
-modules with
P
finitely generated projective

\displaystyle 0\to N\to P\to M\to 0.

Applying
\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(-,M)
we get an exact sequence

\displaystyle 0\to\operatorname{End}_{R}(M)\xrightarrow{\varphi}\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(P,M)\to\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(N,M)\to\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{R}(M,M)\to 0.

By assumption the module
\operatorname{End}_{R}(M)
is Cohen-Macaulay, hence it has depth
3
. Since
M
is reflexive, so are
\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(P,M)
and
\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(N,M)
. Therefore these modules have depth at least
2
. Let
L=\operatorname{coker}(\varphi)
. Then

\displaystyle\operatorname{depth}(L)\geq\min\{\operatorname{depth}(\operatorname{End}_{R}(M))-1,\operatorname{depth}(\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(P,M))\}\geq 2.

Assuming
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{R}(M,M)
is non-zero, the same argument then gives

\operatorname{depth}(\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{R}(M,M))\geq 1.

But this is impossible since
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{R}(M,M)
has finite length as an
R
-module. Indeed, this follows from the fact that over algebras of global dimension
2
every reflexive module is projective, hence
M_{\mathfrak{p}}
is projective over
R_{\mathfrak{p}}
for every
{\mathfrak{p}}
in the regular part of
\operatorname{Spec}(R)
.
∎

It is well-known (see e.g.
[
18
, Exercise 8.8]
) that for
Z
a smooth projective Calabi-Yau variety (i.e.
\omega_{Z}\simeq{\mathcal{O}}_{Z}
), its derived category
{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(Z)
admits no non-trivial semiorthogonal decompositions. The reason is that
{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(Z)
has trivial Serre functor (up to shift), so any semiorthogonal decomposition is automatically orthogonal. We will require an analogous statement for
Y=\operatorname{Tot}(\omega_{X})
with
X
a del Pezzo surface or, more generally, a smooth projective Fano variety. As mentioned above,
Y
is a
non-compact
Calabi-Yau variety, hence its derived category does not admit a Serre functor unless we restrict to the subcategory of objects with compact support. However,
{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(Y)
has trivial
Serre functor relative to
R_{X}
which means that for every
F,G\in{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(Y)
there is a functorial isomorphism in
{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(R_{X})
:

\operatorname{RHom}_{R_{X}}(\operatorname{RHom}_{Y}(F,G),R_{X})\cong\operatorname{RHom}_{Y}(G,F)

See e.g.
[
21
, Lemma 4.13]
. Hence we can conclude in the same manner:

Lemma 5.18
.

Let
X
be a smooth projective Fano variety and
Y=\operatorname{Tot}(\omega_{X})
. Then
{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(Y)
admits no non-trivial
R
-linear semiorthogonal decompositions.

Remark 5.19
.

If
R
is a normal Gorenstein domain and
\Lambda
is an NCCR of
R
. Then one can also show directly that
\Lambda
has trivial Serre functor relative to
R
, i.e. for every
M,N\in{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(\Lambda)
there is a functorial isomorphism

\operatorname{RHom}_{\Lambda}(M,N)\cong\operatorname{RHom}_{R}(\operatorname{RHom}_{\Lambda}(N,M),R).

Indeed, it is sufficient to consider
M=N=\Lambda
since
\Lambda
has finite global dimension and thus
{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(\Lambda)=\Perf(\Lambda)
. Then the statement reduces to
\Lambda\cong\operatorname{RHom}_{R}(\Lambda,R)
which in turn follows from the fact that
\Lambda
is Cohen-Macaulay as an
R
-module.

Now we are ready to prove Theorem
5.13
:

Proof of Theorem
5.13
.

Assume that
\Lambda=\operatorname{End}_{R_{X}}(M)
is a basic graded NCCR of
R_{X}
. Denote
{\mathcal{M}}=\widetilde{M}\in\mathop{\text{{coh}}}(X)
the corresponding coherent sheaf.
Then Proposition
5.15
implies that
{\mathcal{M}}
is a rigid vector bundle.

Note also that
M
and
\Gamma^{*}(X,{\mathcal{M}})
are isomorphic in codimension one and reflexive, hence
M\cong\Gamma^{*}(X,{\mathcal{M}})
. Moreover
M(t)=\Gamma^{\ast}(X,{\mathcal{M}}\otimes\omega_{X}^{-t})
. Hence, since
M
has no repeated indecomposable summands up to shifts,
{\mathcal{M}}
has no indecomposable summands related by tensoring with a a power of
\omega_{X}
.

Recall that by Theorem
4.13
every rigid vector bundle on a del Pezzo surface is a direct sum of exceptional vector bundles, i.e.

{\mathcal{M}}={\mathcal{M}}_{0}\oplus\dots\oplus{\mathcal{M}}_{n-1},

where
{\mathcal{M}}_{j}\in\mathop{\text{{coh}}}(X)
are exceptional.
Let
{\mathcal{M}}_{j}^{\prime}:={\mathcal{M}}_{j}\otimes\omega_{X}^{t_{j}}
where
t_{j}\in{\mathbb{Z}}

is such that
\mu({\mathcal{M}}_{j}^{\prime})\in[0,K^{2}_{X})
. Then the
({\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}_{j})_{j}

are all distinct. We further reorder the
({\mathcal{M}}_{j}^{\prime})_{j}
in such a way
that the slopes are non-decreasing, i.e.

\mu({\mathcal{M}}_{i}^{\prime})\leq\mu({\mathcal{M}}_{j}^{\prime})
if
i<j
. Let

{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}=\bigoplus_{j=0}^{n-1}{\mathcal{M}}_{j}^{\prime}
. Note that

\Gamma^{*}(X,{\mathcal{M}}_{j})=\Gamma^{*}(X,{\mathcal{M}}_{j}^{\prime})(-t_{j})
, so forgetting the
grading we can replace
{\mathcal{M}}
by
{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
. Proposition

5.15
implies that
{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
is still rigid. We claim that

({\mathcal{M}}_{0}^{\prime},\dots,{\mathcal{M}}_{n-1}^{\prime})
is a very strong exceptional
collection on
X
. Indeed, since
{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
is rigid, we have

\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{X}({\mathcal{M}}_{i}^{\prime},{\mathcal{M}}_{j}^{\prime})=\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{X}({\mathcal{M}}_{j}^{\prime},{\mathcal{M}}_{i}^{\prime})=0\text{ for any }0\leq i,j\leq n-1.

Since exceptional bundles on del Pezzo surfaces are
\mu
-stable (Theorem
4.12
), the condition on the order of slopes gives
\operatorname{Hom}_{X}({\mathcal{M}}_{j}^{\prime},{\mathcal{M}}_{i}^{\prime})=0
whenever
i<j
. It remains to check that
\operatorname{Ext}^{2}_{X}({\mathcal{M}}_{j}^{\prime},{\mathcal{M}}_{i}^{\prime})=0
for
i<j
. Indeed, by Serre duality

\displaystyle\operatorname{Ext}^{2}_{X}({\mathcal{M}}_{j}^{\prime},{\mathcal{M}}_{i}^{\prime})=\operatorname{Hom}_{X}({\mathcal{M}}_{i}^{\prime},{\mathcal{M}}_{j}^{\prime}\otimes\omega_{X})^{*}

But
\mu({\mathcal{M}}_{j}^{\prime}\otimes\omega_{X})=\mu({\mathcal{M}}_{j}^{\prime})-K^{2}_{X}<\mu({\mathcal{M}}_{i}^{\prime})
, since
\mu({\mathcal{M}}_{i}^{\prime}),\mu({\mathcal{M}}_{j}^{\prime})\in[0,K^{2}_{X})
. Thus,
\operatorname{Hom}_{X}({\mathcal{M}}_{i}^{\prime},{\mathcal{M}}_{j}^{\prime}\otimes\omega_{X})
vanishes and therefore so does
\operatorname{Ext}^{2}_{X}({\mathcal{M}}_{j}^{\prime},{\mathcal{M}}_{i}^{\prime})
.

The same argument as above shows that
\operatorname{Ext}^{2}_{X}({\mathcal{M}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}\otimes\omega_{X}^{-k})=0
for all
k\geq 0
. Hence, combining this with Proposition
5.15
we have

(5.3)

\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{X}({\mathcal{M}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}\otimes\omega_{X}^{-k})=0\text{ for all }i>0,k\geq 0.

Now let
Y:=\operatorname{Tot}(\omega_{X})
and denote by
\pi:Y\to X
the canonical projection. Then
\operatorname{End}_{Y}(\pi^{*}({\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}))
is a regrading of
\operatorname{End}_{R}(M)=\Lambda
(since
\operatorname{End}_{Y}(\pi^{*}({\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}))\to\operatorname{End}_{R}(M)
is an isomorphism in codimension
1
and both sides are reflexive) and
\pi^{*}({\mathcal{M}}^{\prime})
is a tilting object in
{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(Y)
. Indeed, by (
5.3
) we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{Y}(\pi^{*}({\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}),\pi^{*}({\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}))=\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{X}({\mathcal{M}}^{\prime},\pi_{*}\pi^{*}({\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{X}({\mathcal{M}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}\otimes\omega_{X}^{-k})=0,

for all
i>0
.

Next we will show that
\pi^{*}({\mathcal{M}}^{\prime})
generates
{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(Y)
. To this aim we adopt an argument in
[
41
]
. Denote by
{\mathcal{D}}\cong{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(\operatorname{End}_{Y}(\pi^{*}({\mathcal{M}}^{\prime})))
the thick subcategory of
{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(Y)
generated by
\pi^{*}({\mathcal{M}}^{\prime})
. Since
\operatorname{End}_{Y}(\pi^{*}({\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}))
has finite global dimension, Lemma 1.1.1 in
[
39
]
implies that the subcategory
{\mathcal{D}}
is admissible in
{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(Y)
. But by Lemma
5.18

{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(Y)
does not admit any non-trivial
R
-linear semiorthogonal decompositions, so we conclude that
{\mathcal{D}}={\mathcal{D}}^{b}(Y)
. Observe that this is equivalent to
\pi^{*}({\mathcal{M}}^{\prime})
generating
{\mathcal{D}}_{\operatorname{QCoh}}(Y)
in the sense that the right orthogonal to
\pi^{*}({\mathcal{M}}^{\prime})
in
{\mathcal{D}}_{\operatorname{QCoh}}(Y)
is zero (due to a theorem by Ravenel and Neeman, see
[
7
, Theorem 2.1.2]
).

Finally, we show that the exceptional collection
({\mathcal{M}}_{0}^{\prime},\dots,{\mathcal{M}}_{r}^{\prime})
is full. Suppose to the contrary that there exists an object
{\mathcal{F}}\in\langle{\mathcal{M}}_{0}^{\prime},\dots,{\mathcal{M}}_{n-1}^{\prime}\rangle^{\perp}
. For any
{\mathcal{F}}_{1},{\mathcal{F}}_{2}\in{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(X)
we have

\operatorname{Hom}_{{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(X)}({\mathcal{F}}_{1},{\mathcal{F}}_{2})\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(Y)}(\pi^{*}{\mathcal{F}}_{1},i_{*}{\mathcal{F}}_{2}),

where
i:X\to Y
is the embedding of the zero section and we have used the adjunction
(\pi^{*},\pi_{*})
together with the fact that
\pi\circ i=id_{Y}
. Hence
i_{*}{\mathcal{F}}\in\langle\pi^{*}{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{\prime},\dots,\pi^{*}{\mathcal{M}}_{n-1}^{\prime}\rangle^{\perp}=0
and therefore
{\mathcal{F}}=0
.
4
4

4

We thank Alexander Kuznetsov for drawing our attention to this simple and clear argument. Alternatively, one can argue as follows. Note that it suffices to show that
n=\operatorname{rk}K_{0}(X)
, because on a del Pezzo surface every exceptional collection of maximal length is full
[
30
]
. Since it is already established that
\pi^{*}({\mathcal{M}}^{\prime})
is a tilting complex, we have
{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(\Lambda)\cong{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(Y)
, hence
\operatorname{rk}K_{0}(\Lambda)=\operatorname{rk}K_{0}(Y)=\operatorname{rk}K_{0}(X)
. On the other hand,
K_{0}(\Lambda)=n
, because both sides equal the number of non-isomorphic graded simple
\Lambda
-modules.

∎

To prove Proposition
5.14
we need the following fact relating rigidity to grading:

Proposition 5.20

(Keller-Murfet-Van den Bergh,
[
27
, Proposition 6.1]
)
.

Let
R=k+R_{1}+\dots
be a Noetherian graded
k
-algebra with
char(k)=0
and
M
a finitely generated
\widehat{R}
-module with
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{R}(M,M)=0
.
Then there exists a finitely generated graded
R-
module
N
such that
M
is the completion of
N
.

Proof of Proposition
5.14
.

Let
\Lambda=\operatorname{End}_{\widehat{R}}(M)
be an NCCR of
\widehat{R}
. Then by Theorem
5.17

M
is rigid.
Let
N\in\operatorname{gr}(R)
be the corresponding graded module
given by Proposition
5.20
. Since completion preserves depth with respect to the irrelevant ideal in
R
we obtain that
\Gamma=\operatorname{End}_{R}(N)

is a graded NCCR of
R
. Clearly
\Lambda
is the completion of
\Gamma
.
∎

6.
Mutations of NCCRs

Definition 6.1
.

Let
R
be a normal Gorenstein domain and
M=N\oplus L
an
R
-module. Let
f:N^{\prime}\to M
be a right
\operatorname{add}(N)
-approximation of
M
, i.e.
N^{\prime}\in\operatorname{add}(N)
and the induced map

\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(N,N^{\prime})\xrightarrow{}\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(N,M)

is surjective. Then we define
the right mutation
of
M
with respect to
N
as

\mu^{+}_{N}(M):=N\oplus\operatorname{ker}(f).

Left mutations
are defined dually by

\mu^{-}_{N}(M):=N\oplus\operatorname{ker}(g)^{*},

where
g:N^{\prime\prime}\to M^{*}
is a right
\operatorname{add}(N^{*})
-approximation of
M^{*}
.

Remark 6.2
.

It is clear that mutations can be carried out in the graded context (see §
5.1
).

Remark 6.3
.

In general, one should keep in mind that mutations are defined only up to additive closure. For instance, left and right mutations
\mu^{\pm}_{N}
are mutually inverse only up to taking
\operatorname{add}(-)
. However, if we are in the complete or graded context, mutations are unique up to isomorphism if we can consider approximations
f:N^{\prime}\to M
for which no non-trivial direct summands of
N^{\prime}
are contained in
\operatorname{ker}(f)
.

Remark 6.4
.

If we are in the complete or graded context and if we fix a decomposition of
M=\oplus_{i=1}^{n}M_{i}
into indecomposable direct summands, we can write
\mu^{\pm 1}_{j}(M)
for
\mu^{\pm 1}_{\oplus_{i\neq j}M_{i}}(M)=\bigoplus_{i\neq j}M_{i}\oplus M_{j}^{\prime}
. Note that this again gives a fixed enumeration of indecomposable direct summands of
\mu^{\pm 1}_{j}(M)
, hence we can consider iterated mutations and talk of the “
j
’th mutation”
\mu_{j}^{\pm 1}
at every step.

Theorem 6.5

(
[
20
, Theorems 1.22, 1.23, 1.25]
)
.

Let
R,M
and
N
be as in Definition
6.1
. Assume that
\Lambda:=\operatorname{End}_{R}(M)
is an NCCR of
R
. Then

(1)

\operatorname{End}_{R}(\mu_{N}^{+}(M))
and
\operatorname{End}_{R}(\mu_{N}^{-}(M))
are also NCCRs of
R
.

(2)

The algebras
\Lambda
,
\operatorname{End}_{R}(\mu_{N}^{+}(M))
and
\operatorname{End}_{R}(\mu_{N}^{-}(M))
are derived equivalent.

(3)

Assume in addition that we are in the complete or graded context and
\dim R=3
. Let
\Lambda=\operatorname{End}_{R}(M)
be an NCCR of
R
, where
M=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}M_{i}
with
M_{i}
indecomposable and pairwise non-isomorphic (i.e.
\Lambda
is basic according to Definition
5.3
). Then
\mu_{i}^{+}(M)\cong\mu_{i}^{-}(M)
(up to a shift in grading of the components in the graded context). In this case we can write
\mu_{i}:=\mu_{i}^{+}=\mu_{i}^{-}
.

In the case we will focus on, there is an alternative way to view mutations of NCCRs, namely as mutations of
quivers with potentials
in the sense of Derksen, Weyman and Zelevinsky
[
13
]
.

Definition 6.6
.

Let
(Q,W)
be a quiver with potential
and let
i\in Q_{0}
be a vertex which is not contained in a loop or oriented 2-cycle.
Define
\tilde{\mu}_{i}(Q,W)=(\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W})
to be obtained from
(Q,W)
as follows:

(1)

For every pair of arrows
\beta,\alpha
with
t(\beta)=i=s(\alpha)
, add a new arrow

[\alpha\beta]:s(\beta)\to t(\alpha).

(2)

Replace each arrow
\gamma
with
s(\gamma)=i
or
t(\gamma)=i
by an arrow
\gamma^{*}
in the opposite direction.

(3)

Define
\widetilde{W}
to be the sum of

•

the potential obtained from
W
by replacing every subpath
\alpha\beta
with
t(\beta)=i=s(\alpha)
by the corresponding arrow
[\alpha\beta]
, and

•

the additional term
\sum_{\alpha,\beta}[\alpha\beta]\beta^{*}\alpha^{*}
, where the sum ranges over all pairs
(\beta,\alpha)
with
t(\beta)=i=s(\alpha)
.

Remark 6.7
.

In the graded context we need to assign a degree to
\alpha^{\ast}
,
\beta^{\ast}
such that
\deg\alpha+\deg\beta+\deg\alpha^{\ast}+\deg\beta^{\ast}=\deg W
.
The precise rule is given in
[
11
, §2]
.

If
W
is unspecified then
\widetilde{Q}
is still well-defined and we write

\tilde{\mu}_{i}Q=\widetilde{Q}
.
Let
(Q,W)
be a quiver with potential such that
Q
has no loops or oriented
2
-cycles and assume that we are in the complete or graded context.
We let

\mu_{i}(Q,W):=(\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W})_{\operatorname{red}}:=(\widetilde{Q}_{red},\widetilde{W}_{\operatorname{red}}),

to be the
reduced part
of
(\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W})
(see
[
13
, Theorem 4.6]
; in particular,
\widetilde{W}_{\operatorname{red}}
contains no 2-cycles). We write

\mu_{i}{\mathcal{J}}(Q,W):={\mathcal{J}}(\mu_{i}(Q,W)).

We will also define
\mu_{i}Q
as the quiver obtained from
\widetilde{\mu}_{i}{Q}
by deleting
all
2
-cycles.

Theorem 6.8

(Keller-Yang,
[
28
, Theorem 3.2]
)
.

Let
\Lambda
be a 3-Calabi-Yau algebra with presentation
\Lambda\cong{\mathcal{J}}(Q,W)
with
Q
having no loops or
2
-cycles.
For
j\in Q_{0}
denote by
P_{j}=\Lambda e_{j}
the corresponding indecomposable projective module. Then there is an algebra isomorphism

\operatorname{End}(\mu_{\oplus_{j\neq i}{P_{j}}}(\Lambda))^{\circ}\cong\mu_{i}{\mathcal{J}}(Q,W)={\mathcal{J}}(\mu_{i}(Q,W)).

In particular, in this setting the mutation of NCCRs defined in Definition
6.1
agrees with DWZ mutations of quivers with potential.

Since the quiver
Q
for
B({\mathbb{E}})
contains no loops or oriented two-cycles (see Remark
5.12
) Theorem
6.8
allows us to describe mutations of the NCCR
B({\mathbb{E}})
by the combinatorial DWZ mutation procedure in Definition
6.6
. For this reason in our situation we will often refer to mutations of NCCRs as
quiver mutations
.
This will also help us distinguish these mutations from mutations of exceptional collections on
X
inducing the braid group action.

Note that it is not guaranteed in general that the quiver of
\mu_{i}{\mathcal{J}}(Q,W)
again contains no loops or two-cycles (there is only such a guarantee for
W
). However, Bridgeland and Stern
[
10
, Theorem 1.7]
showed that this is indeed the case for mutations of
3
-Calabi–Yau algebras obtained from very strong exceptional collections on del Pezzo surfaces:

Theorem 6.9

(Bridgeland-Stern,
[
10
, Theorem 1.7]
, see also
[
11
]
for another proof)
.

Let
B({\mathbb{E}})={\mathcal{J}}(Q,W)
be as above with
{\mathbb{E}}
a very strong exceptional collection on a del Pezzo surface
X
. Let
i\in Q_{0}
be a vertex and
\mu_{i}B({\mathbb{E}})={\mathcal{J}}(Q^{\prime},W^{\prime})
the algebra obtained by mutation at
i
. Then there exists a very strong exceptional collection
{\mathbb{E}}^{\prime}
on
X
such that
\mu_{i}B({\mathbb{E}})=B({\mathbb{E}}^{\prime})
. In particular,
Q^{\prime}
does not contain loops or two-cycles.

This result may be obtained from Theorem
5.13
:

Proof of Theorem
6.9
.

By Theorem
6.5

\mu_{i}B({\mathbb{E}})
is an NCCR of
R_{X}
. Hence by Theorem
5.13

\mu_{i}B({\mathbb{E}})\cong B({\mathbb{E}}^{\prime})
for some very strong exceptional collection
{\mathbb{E}}^{\prime}
on
X
.
∎

It is useful to understand explicitly how
{\mathbb{E}}
and
{\mathbb{E}}^{\prime}
are related in Theorem
6.9
. First we introduce some notations. Rotating the helix if necessary, we can always assume that
i=n-1
. The following is proved in
[
10
]
:

Proposition 6.10
.

Let
{\mathbb{E}}=(E_{0},\dots,E_{n-1})
be a very strong exceptional collection on del Pezzo surface. Then there exists
j\in\{1,\dots,n-2\}
such that

{\mathbb{E}}^{\prime}=(E_{1},E_{2},\ldots,E_{j},\widetilde{R_{E_{j}}}\cdots\widetilde{R_{E_{1}}}E_{0},E_{j+1},\ldots,E_{n-1}).

is a very strong exceptional collection with

\mu_{0}B({\mathbb{E}})\cong B({\mathbb{E}}^{\prime})
.

To be consistent with the terminology introduced above, we will call
{\mathbb{E}}^{\prime}
the quiver mutation of
{\mathbb{E}}
at
E_{0}
.
Quiver mutations at other
E_{i}
are defined by rotation.
The following proposition strengthens Proposition
6.10

Proposition 6.11
.

We have

{\mathbb{E}}^{\prime}=(E_{1},E_{2},\ldots,E_{j},\widetilde{R_{E_{j}}}\cdots\widetilde{R_{E_{1}}}E_{0},E_{j+1},\ldots,E_{n-1}).

where
j
is the smallest index such that
{\mathbb{E}}^{\prime}

is very strong and
\operatorname{Hom}_{X}(E_{0},E_{j})\neq 0
.

Proof.

This is a rephrasing of Proposition
9.12
below.
∎

In other words, a quiver mutation amounts to applying several consequent braid group mutations to a very strong exceptional collection until it becomes very strong again for the first time. The only exception to this rule is when the first mutation is a mutation in an orthogonal pair. Such a mutation trivially keeps the collection very strong and does not affect the corresponding NCCR. In this case one has to mutate “through” the whole orthogonal block.
In what follows, we usually identify a quiver mutation (a mutation of an NCCR) and its corresponding sequence of mutations on the level of exceptional collections, as described in Proposition
6.10
.

Remark 6.12
.

If
{\mathbb{E}}^{\prime}
is the quiver mutation of
{\mathbb{E}}
at
E_{i}

then using the Convention
4.2.1
we may write
{\mathbb{E}}^{\prime}
as

\tilde{\sigma}_{j}^{-1}\cdots\tilde{\sigma}_{i+2}^{-1}\tilde{\sigma}_{i+1}^{-1}{\mathbb{E}}
for suitable
j
where the indices are wrapped to remain in the interval
[1,n]
.
Thus it makes sense to refer to
{\mathbb{E}}^{\prime}
as the
right
a quiver mutation of
{\mathbb{E}}
and
E_{i}
. The left quiver mutation
at
E_{i}
can be defined symmetrically and it will be equal to
\tilde{\sigma}_{j^{\prime}+1}\cdots\tilde{\sigma}_{i-1}\tilde{\sigma}_{i}{\mathbb{E}}
for suitable
j^{\prime}
.
Using the analogue of Proposition
6.11
one sees that left and right quiver mutations at
E_{i}
are each other’s inverse up to rotation
and permuting the exceptional objects in a block.

7.
Blocks and block mutations

7.1.
Blocks and block quivers

Definition 7.1
.

If
Q
is a quiver then a
block
is a set of vertices which are
not connected by edges. We say that a block is not incident to loops or oriented 2-cycles
if none of its members are.

Definition 7.2
.

A
block quiver

(Q,I)
is a quiver
Q
together with a decomposition

Q_{0}=I_{0}\coprod\cdots\coprod I_{k-1}
where the
I_{i}
are blocks.

In the sequel we will encounter a particular kind of block quiver.

Definition 7.3
.

A quiver with
vertex multiplicities
is
(Q,(\alpha_{i})_{i\in Q_{0}})
is a pair
consisting of a quiver
Q
and for each vertex
i\in Q_{0}
a “multiplicity”
\alpha_{i}\geq 1
.

If
(Q,(\alpha_{i})_{i\in Q_{0}})
is a quiver with vertex multiplicities, without loops, then
Q^{e}
is the block quiver obtained by replacing each vertex
i
in
Q
by a block

I_{i}
of
size
\alpha_{i}
and by letting for every
u\in I_{i}
,
v\in I_{j}
,
i\neq j
,
Q^{e}(u,v)
be a distinct copy of
Q(i,j)
. If a block quiver
\Sigma
is of the form
Q^{e}
then we call
\Sigma

regular
and we call
Q
the
reduced quiver
associated
to
\Sigma
. We denote it by
\Sigma_{\operatorname{/}}
.

7.2.
Block quiver mutations

Let
(Q,W)
be a quiver with potential.
Let

I=\{i_{0},\ldots,i_{\alpha-1}\}\subset Q_{0}
be a block not incident to loops or oriented 2-cycles. Then it is clear that

\widetilde{\mu}_{I}(Q,W):=\widetilde{\mu}_{i_{0}}\cdots\widetilde{\mu}_{i_{\alpha-1}}(Q,W)
(see Definition
6.6
)
is well defined. In other words it does not depend on the chosen ordering on
I
.
If
Q
has no loops or oriented
2
-cycles then we also put
\mu_{I}(Q,W)=\widetilde{\mu}_{I}(Q,W)_{\operatorname{red}}

and we call
\mu_{I}(Q,W)
a
block mutation
of
(Q,W)
.
One may show that
\mu_{I}
is the composition of
(\mu_{i_{j}})_{j}
. Following the conventions
in Definition
6.6
we also use the notations
\widetilde{\mu}_{I}Q
and
\mu_{I}Q
when appropriate.

In case
(Q,I)
is a block quiver, we write
\mu_{i}
for
\mu_{I_{i}}
.

7.3.
Block mutations of NCCRs

Assume that we are in the graded or complete context.
Let
\Lambda
be an NCCR of
R
which is represented as
\Lambda
. Let
(S_{i})_{i=0}^{n-1}
be the non-isomorphic simples of
\Lambda
(concentrated in degree zero in the graded
context), and let
Q
be the associated

\operatorname{Ext}^{1}
-graph as
in Remark
5.9
.

A
block
is a collection
of simples
(S_{i})_{i\in I}
for
I=\{i_{0},\ldots,i_{\alpha-1}\}\subset\{0,\ldots,n-1\}
such that
\operatorname{RHom}_{\Lambda}(S_{i_{k}},S_{i_{l}})=0
for
k\neq l
.
If this is the case and
I
is not adjacent to any loops and oriented
2
-cycles in
Q

then we claim that
\mu_{I}(\Lambda):=(\mu_{i_{0}}\cdots\mu_{i_{\alpha-1}})(\Lambda)
is again well defined. This follows by representing
\Lambda
as
{\mathcal{J}}(Q,W)
(see §
6
) and invoking §
7.2
.

Remark 7.4
.

Presumably this result will also hold without the restriction that
I
is not adjacent to any loops or oriented
2
-cycles.

7.4.
Block exceptional collections

Definition 7.5
.

A
block
is a collection of exceptional objects
(E^{(0)},\ldots,E^{(\alpha-1)})
which are pairwise orthogonal.

Definition 7.6
.

A
block exceptional collection
is a sequence of blocks

(7.1)

{\mathbb{E}}=((E_{0}^{(0)},\ldots,E_{0}^{(\alpha_{0}-1)}),\ldots,(E_{k-1}^{(0)},\ldots,E_{k-1}^{(\alpha_{k-1}-1)}))

which becomes an exceptional collection when we forget the subdivision into blocks.
If
k=2
then we call
({\mathbb{E}}_{0},{\mathbb{E}}_{1})=((E_{0}^{(0)},\ldots,E_{0}^{(\alpha_{0}-1)}),(E_{1}^{(0)},\ldots,E_{1}^{(\alpha_{1}-1)}))
a
block exceptional pair
. We say that a block exceptional collection
{\mathbb{E}}
is
full
if the associated exceptional collection is full.

Definition 7.7
.

If
({\mathbb{E}},{\mathbb{F}})=((E^{(0)},\ldots,E^{(\alpha-1)}),(F^{(0)},\ldots,F^{(\beta-1)}))
is a block exceptional pair then we define

(7.2)

\displaystyle L_{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbb{F}}

\displaystyle=(L_{E^{(0)}}L_{E^{(1)}}\cdots L_{E^{(\alpha-1)}}F^{(0)},\ldots,L_{E^{(0)}}L_{E^{(1)}}\cdots L_{E^{(\alpha-1)}}F^{(\beta-1)})

\displaystyle R_{\mathbb{F}}{\mathbb{E}}

\displaystyle=(R_{F^{(0)}}R_{F^{(1)}}\cdots R_{F^{(\beta-1)}}E^{(0)},\ldots,R_{F^{(0)}}R_{F^{(1)}}\cdots R_{F^{(\beta-1)}}E^{(\alpha-1)})

The following result is easy to verify.

Lemma 7.8
.

Let
{\mathbb{E}}=({\mathbb{E}}_{0},\cdots,{\mathbb{E}}_{k-1})
be a full block exceptional collection. Define the
right dual
block exceptional collection

\widecheck{{\mathbb{E}}}=(\widecheck{{\mathbb{E}}_{k-1}},\ldots,\widecheck{{\mathbb{E}}_{0}})
via

\widecheck{{\mathbb{E}}_{i}}:=R_{{\mathbb{E}}_{k-1}}\dots R_{{\mathbb{E}}_{i+1}}({\mathbb{E}}_{i})

Then the underlying exceptional collections of
\widecheck{{\mathbb{E}}}
, with the order of the exceptional objects in the blocks inverted, is the right dual (see Proposition
4.4
) of the underlying exceptional collection of
{\mathbb{E}}
.

Definition 7.9
.

We say that a block exceptional collection has
broken blocks
if
{\mathbb{E}}_{i}
,
{\mathbb{E}}_{i+1}
are orthogonal for some
i
with
{\mathbb{E}}_{k}:=\omega_{X}^{-1}\otimes_{X}{\mathbb{E}}_{0}
.

Definition 7.10
.

Any full exceptional collection
{\mathbb{E}}
may be transformed using a suitable rotation into a block exceptional collection
{\mathbb{E}}^{\prime}
without broken blocks. If
{\mathbb{E}}^{\prime}
has block sizes
(\alpha_{i})_{i=0}^{k-1}
then we say that
{\mathbb{E}}
is
k
-block and
we call the
(\alpha_{i})_{i}
the
block multiplicities
of
{\mathbb{E}}
. The block multiplicities are well defined up to cyclic permutation.

Remark 7.11
.

The blocks related properties of an exceptional collection can be characterised solely in terms of slopes. See Proposition
4.11
.

Convention 7.12
.

Below terminology and notation, when applied to a block exceptional collection, are understood to apply
to the associated exceptional collection, unless defined otherwise.

For a block exceptional pair
({\mathbb{E}},{\mathbb{F}})
we define
\widetilde{L_{\mathbb{E}}}{\mathbb{F}}
,
\widetilde{R_{\mathbb{F}}{\mathbb{E}}}
by replacing
L
and
R
in (
7.2
) by
\widetilde{L}
and
\widetilde{R}
.
Proposition
6.10
generalises in the following way:

Proposition 7.13
.

Let
{\mathbb{E}}=({\mathbb{E}}_{0},\dots,{\mathbb{E}}_{k-1})
be a very strong block exceptional collection on a del Pezzo surface. Then there exists
j\in{1,\dots,k-2}
such that

{\mathbb{E}}^{\prime}:=({\mathbb{E}}_{1},{\mathbb{E}}_{2},\ldots,{\mathbb{E}}_{j},\widetilde{R_{{\mathbb{E}}_{j}}}\cdots\widetilde{R_{{\mathbb{E}}_{1}}}{\mathbb{E}}_{0},{\mathbb{E}}_{j+1},\ldots,E_{k-1}).

is a very strong block exceptional collection with

\mu_{0}B({\mathbb{E}})\cong B({\mathbb{E}}^{\prime})
.

We will call
{\mathbb{E}}^{\prime}
a
block quiver mutation
of
{\mathbb{E}}
. The obvious analogue or Proposition
6.11
holds.

Remark 7.14
.

If
{\mathbb{E}}_{i}=(E_{i}^{(0)},\ldots,E_{i}^{(\alpha_{i}-1)})
then it is easy to see that a block quiver mutation as in Proposition
7.13

is the composition of quiver mutations at
E_{i}^{(\alpha_{i}-1)},\allowbreak E_{i}^{(\alpha_{i}-2)},\allowbreak\ldots,\allowbreak E_{i}^{(0)}
.

7.5.
Block quivers associated to block exceptional collections

If
{\mathbb{E}}
is a block exceptional collection

{\mathbb{E}}=((E_{0}^{(0)},\ldots,E_{0}^{(\alpha_{0}-1)}),\ldots,(E_{k-1}^{(0)},\ldots,E_{k-1}^{(\alpha_{k-1}-1)}))

on a del Pezzo surface
X
, then the reduced Gram matrix
M_{\operatorname{/}}
is the
k\times k
-matrix such that
M_{\operatorname{/},ij}=\chi(E_{i}^{(?)},E_{j}^{(?)})
.

If
{\mathbb{E}}
is in addition very strong
then
the quiver associated to
{\mathbb{E}}
, as constructed in Proposition
5.10
becomes
naturally a block quiver
Q
with blocks
(\{(i,0),\ldots,(i,\alpha_{i}-1)\})_{i=0}^{k-1}
. This block quiver is actually regular so it may equivalently be viewed as a quiver with vertex multiplicities
(Q_{\operatorname{/}},(\alpha_{i})_{i=0}^{k-1})
.

8.
Toric machinery of exceptional collections

8.1.
Introduction

This section is based on the framework developed by Perling in
[
38
]
(see also
[
31
]
,
[
17
]
) which allows to associate a toric system in
\operatorname{Pic}(X)\otimes{\mathbb{Q}}
and a fan in a two-dimensional lattice to an exceptional collection on a rational surface
X
. Moreover, very strong exceptional collections on del Pezzo surfaces correspond to fans generating convex polygons. Via this correspondence mutations of exceptional collections as well as quiver mutations can be seen as operations on toric systems or fans. For simplicity we will formulate everything for del Pezzo surfaces using the notations introduced in §
4.2
.

8.2.
Toric systems and their Gale duals

Proposition 8.1

(
[
38
, §5]
,
[
31
, Proposition 4.9]
)
.

Let
{\mathbb{E}}=(E_{0},\dots,E_{n-1})
be a full exceptional collection of vector bundles on a del Pezzo surface
X
and denote
r_{i}:=r(E_{i})
.

Define
T_{i,i+1}\in\operatorname{Pic}(X)\otimes{\mathbb{Q}}
with
0\leq i\leq n-2
by

(8.1)

\displaystyle T_{i,{i+1}}

\displaystyle:=s(E_{i},E_{i+1})=\frac{c_{1}(E_{i+1})}{r_{i+1}}-\frac{c_{1}(E_{i})}{r_{i}},\,\,i=0,\dots,n-2

\displaystyle T_{n-1,n}

\displaystyle:=T_{-1,0}:=T_{n-1,0}:=s(E_{n-1},E_{0}\otimes\omega_{X}^{-1})

Denote also
T_{ij}=T_{i,{i+1}}+\dots+\dots T_{{j-1},j}
for
i<j
Then
(T_{i,{i+1}})_{i=0}^{n-1}
form a
toric system
, which means that

(1)

T_{i-1,i}\cdot T_{i,i+1}=\frac{1}{r_{i}^{2}}
for all
0\leq i\leq n-1

(2)

T_{i-1,i}\cdot T_{j,j+1}=0
for
0\leq i<j\leq n-1
.

(3)

\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}T_{i,i+1}=-K_{X}

(4)

r_{i}r_{j}T_{ij}
is integral for all
0\leq i<j\leq n-1
(hence
{a_{ij}:=(r_{i}r_{j}T_{ij})^{2}\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
).

Moreover,

(8.2)

\chi(E_{i},E_{j})=\frac{a_{ij}+r_{i}^{2}+r_{j}^{2}}{r_{i}r_{j}}.

Definition 8.2
.

Let
V
be a vector space over a field
k
and let
(v_{i})_{i=1,\ldots,n}\in V
. Assume that
(v_{i})_{i}
spans
V
.
The
Gale dual
of
(V,(v_{i})_{i})
is a pair
(W,(w_{i})_{i=1,\ldots,n})
where
W
is a
k
-vector space and
(w_{i})_{i}\in W
such that

(1)

\dim W=n-\dim V
;

(2)

(w_{i})_{i}
spans
W
;

(3)

\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}\otimes v_{i}=0\text{ in }W\otimes V
.

Remark 8.3
.

One verifies that the Gale dual is unique up to unique isomorphism.

It is easy to see that in the toric system corresponding to
{\mathbb{E}}
, as defined in Proposition
8.1
, the vectors
(T_{i,i+1})_{i=0}^{n-1}
generate

\operatorname{Pic}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}
as
{\mathbb{R}}
-vector space. Thus the Gale dual of
(\operatorname{Pic}(X)_{\mathbb{R}},(T_{i,i+1})_{i=0}^{n-1})
is well defined (see
[
31
,
38
]
) and we write it as
(L,(l_{i,i+1})_{i=0}^{n-1})

where indices are taken modulo
n
, i.e.
l_{n-1,0}:=l_{n-1,n}
.
In Theorem
8.5
below we give an explicit description
of said Gale dual.

Remark 8.4
.

Note that since
n-\dim\operatorname{Pic}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}=\operatorname{rk}K_{0}(X)-(\operatorname{rk}K_{0}(X)-2)=2
the Gale dual lives in a two dimensional space.

8.3.
Gale duals of toric systems and dual exceptional collections

In this section we show how the Gale dual
(L,(l_{i,i+1})_{i})
of
(T_{i,i+1})_{i=0}^{n-1}
can be directly obtained from the dual exceptional collection of
{\mathbb{E}}
. This result appears to be new.
