We use this to establish a direct connection between
(L,(l_{i,i+1})_{i})
and the quiver associated to
B({\mathbb{E}})
(see §
5.3
). This appears to be new as well.

Theorem 8.5
.

Let
{\mathbb{E}}=(E_{0},\dots,E_{n-1})
be a full exceptional collection of vector bundles on a del Pezzo surface
X
. Let
(F_{n-1},\dots,F_{0})
be the right dual collection of
{\mathbb{E}}

(see Proposition
4.4
). For
Y\subset X
a smooth anti-canonical divisor let
L:=K^{\operatorname{num}}_{0}(Y)_{{\mathbb{R}}}
,
y\in Y
and

(8.3)

l_{i,i+1}=-[{\mathcal{O}}_{y}]+\sum_{j=0}^{i}r_{j}[F_{j,Y}]\text{ for all }i\in\{0,\dotsm n-1\}.

The Gale dual of the toric system
(T_{i,i+1})_{i=0}^{n-1}
corresponding to
{\mathbb{E}}
, as defined in Proposition
8.1
, can be taken to be
(L,(l_{i,i+1})_{i=0}^{n-1})
.
Moreover

(8.4)

\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}r_{j}[F_{j,Y}]=0

so that if we extend the formula (
8.3
) to all
i\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, we have
l_{i+n,i+n+1}=l_{i,i+1}
.

Proof.

First we verify (
8.4
).
Let
\alpha:Y\hookrightarrow X
be the inclusion. We claim that

(8.5)

\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}r_{j}[F_{j}]=[\alpha_{\ast}{\mathcal{O}}_{y}]\text{ in }K_{0}(X).

Indeed, it is sufficient to check that (
8.5
) holds after applying
\chi_{X}(-,[E_{i}])
for every
i=0,\dots,n-1
, but this gives just
r_{i}=r(E_{i})
.
Since
[\alpha^{\ast}\alpha_{\ast}{\mathcal{O}}_{y}]=0
, applying
\alpha^{\ast}
to (
8.5
) we get (
8.4
).

Now we prove the first part. Note that
\dim K^{\operatorname{num}}_{0}(Y)_{{\mathbb{R}}}=2=n-\dim\operatorname{Pic}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}
(see Remark
8.4
) and moreover it is easy to see that
{\alpha^{\ast}:K_{0}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}\to K^{\operatorname{num}}_{0}(Y)_{{\mathbb{R}}}}
is surjective.
So the
[F_{j,Y}]
span
K_{0}^{\operatorname{num}}(Y)_{{\mathbb{R}}}
. Therefore
(l_{i,i+1})_{i}
(as defined by (
8.3
)) also spans
K^{\operatorname{num}}_{0}(Y)_{{\mathbb{R}}}
and hence
it remains to prove that

\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}l_{i,i+1}\otimes T_{i,i+1}=0\quad\text{ in }K^{\operatorname{num}}_{0}(Y)_{{\mathbb{R}}}\otimes\operatorname{Pic}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}.

or concretely

(8.6)

\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}l_{i,i+1}\otimes(c_{1}(E_{i+1})/r_{i+1}-c_{1}(E_{i})/r_{i})=0,

where
E_{n}:=E_{0}\otimes\omega_{X}^{-1}
. Let us write
E^{\prime}_{i}=E_{i\operatorname{mod}n}
.
Then (
8.6
) can be
rewritten as

\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}l_{i,i+1}\otimes\bigg(\frac{c_{1}(E_{i+1}^{\prime})}{r_{i+1}}-\frac{c_{1}(E_{i}^{\prime})}{r_{i}}\bigg)=-l_{n-1,0}\otimes c_{1}(\omega_{X}^{-1})

or equivalently

\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\frac{(l_{i-1,i}-l_{i,i+1})}{r_{i}}\otimes c_{1}(E_{i})=-l_{n-1,0}\otimes c_{1}(\omega_{X}^{-1}).

So we have to verify

(8.7)

\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}[F_{i,Y}]\otimes c_{1}(E_{i})=-[{\mathcal{O}}_{y}]\otimes c_{1}(\omega_{X}^{-1})\quad\text{ in }K^{\operatorname{num}}_{0}(Y)_{{\mathbb{R}}}\otimes\operatorname{Pic}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}.

Consider the standard filtration on
K_{0}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}
by codimension of supports
F^{\ast}(X):=F^{\ast}K_{0}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}
.
We have

\displaystyle F^{0}(X)/F^{1}(X)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{R}}[{\mathcal{O}}_{X}]

\displaystyle F^{2}(X)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{R}}[\alpha_{\ast}{\mathcal{O}}_{y}]

for an arbitrary
y\in Y
. Moreover there is a natural
identification

F^{1}K_{0}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}/F^{2}K_{0}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}\cong\operatorname{Pic}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}

such that for
E\in K_{0}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}
we have

\displaystyle c_{1}(E)\cong[E]-\operatorname{rk}(E)[{\mathcal{O}}_{X}]\qquad\operatorname{mod}F^{2}(X).

In particular

(8.8)

c_{1}(\omega_{X}^{-1})\cong[{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(Y)]-[{\mathcal{O}}_{X}]\cong[\alpha_{\ast}{\mathcal{O}}_{Y}(Y)]\cong[\alpha_{\ast}{\mathcal{O}}_{Y}]\qquad\operatorname{mod}F^{2}(X).

By (
8.4
) we have

\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}[F_{i,Y}]\otimes r_{i}[{\mathcal{O}}_{X}]=0.

Hence by (
8.8
) we find that (
8.7
) is equivalent to

\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}[F_{i,Y}]\otimes[E_{i}]=-[{\mathcal{O}}_{y}]\otimes[\alpha_{\ast}{\mathcal{O}}_{Y}]\quad\operatorname{mod}K^{\operatorname{num}}_{0}(Y)_{{\mathbb{R}}}\otimes{\mathbb{R}}[\alpha_{\ast}{\mathcal{O}}_{y}]

in
K^{\operatorname{num}}_{0}(Y)_{{\mathbb{R}}}\otimes K_{0}(X)_{{\mathbb{R}}}
. In fact we have

\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}[F_{i,Y}]\otimes[E_{i}]=-[{\mathcal{O}}_{y}]\otimes[\alpha_{\ast}{\mathcal{O}}_{Y}]+[{\mathcal{O}}_{Y}]\otimes[\alpha_{\ast}{\mathcal{O}}_{y}].

Indeed, after applying
\operatorname{id}\otimes\chi_{X}([F_{i}],-)
this is equivalent to

[F_{i,Y}]=\chi(F_{i,Y})[{\mathcal{O}}_{y}]+\operatorname{rk}(F_{i,Y})[{\mathcal{O}}_{Y}]

which is clear.
∎

Lemma 8.6
.

Let
(L,(l_{i,i+1})_{i=0}^{n-1})
be as in Theorem
8.5
. Then for all
i
we have

\chi_{Y}(l_{i-1,i},l_{i,i+1})=r_{i}^{2}.

Proof.

By
[
31
, Proposition 4.17]
there exists a non-zero
h\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that

(8.9)

\chi_{Y}(l_{i-1,i},l_{i,i+1})=hr_{i}^{2}.

for all
i
. We only need to compute
h
for a single
i
. Let
i=n-1
. It follows from (
8.4
) and Remark
4.6

that

\displaystyle l_{n-2,n-1}

\displaystyle=-[{\mathcal{O}}_{y}]-r_{n-1}[F_{n-1,Y}]

\displaystyle=-[{\mathcal{O}}_{y}]-r_{n-1}[E_{n-1,Y}]

so that

\displaystyle\chi_{Y}(l_{n-2,n-1},l_{n-1,0})

\displaystyle=\chi_{Y}(-[{\mathcal{O}}_{y}]-r_{n-1}[E_{n-1,Y}],-[{\mathcal{O}}_{y}])

\displaystyle=r_{n-1}^{2}.\qed

Corollary 8.7
.

Let
(L,(l_{i,i+1})_{i})
be the Gale dual of
(\operatorname{Pic}(X)_{\mathbb{R}},(T_{i,i+1})_{i=0,\ldots,n-1})
(not necessarily the model of Theorem
8.5
). Choose a volume form
\omega\in\Lambda^{2}L\cong{\mathbb{R}}
such
that
\omega(l_{i-1,i},l_{i,i+1})>0
for all
i
(see
[
31
, Proposition 4.17]
). Then we have for
i>j
.

(8.10)

\chi_{X}(F_{i},F_{j})=\chi_{Y}([F_{i,Y}],[F_{j,Y}])=\omega(\overline{m}_{i},\overline{m}_{j})

where

\overline{m}_{i}=\frac{l_{i,i+1}-l_{i-1,i}}{\sqrt{\omega(l_{i-1,i},l_{i,i+1})}}.

Proof.

We may use the concrete realisation of
(L,(l_{i,i+1})_{i})
constructed in Theorem
8.5
. Moreover,
\omega
is a scalar multiple of
\chi_{Y}
, since any two volume forms differ only by a scalar. Since the right-hand side
of (
8.10
) is independent of
\omega
, we may assume
\omega\in\{-\chi_{Y},\chi_{Y}\}
and by Lemma
8.6
we then must take
\omega=\chi_{Y}
.
Then we compute

\displaystyle\omega(\overline{m}_{i},\overline{m}_{j})

\displaystyle=\frac{\chi_{Y}(r_{i}[F_{i,Y}],r_{j}[F_{j,Y}])}{\sqrt{\omega(l_{i-1,i},l_{i,i+1})}\sqrt{\omega(l_{j-1,j},l_{j,j+1})}}=\frac{\chi_{Y}(r_{i}[F_{i,Y}],r_{j}[F_{j,Y}])}{r_{i}r_{j}}=

\displaystyle=\chi_{Y}([F_{i,Y}],[F_{j,Y}]),

where we have used Lemma
8.6
again for the second equality. Since
i>j
and therefore
(F_{i},F_{j})
is an exceptional pair, one has
\chi_{Y}([F_{i,Y}],[F_{j,Y}])=\chi_{X}(F_{i},F_{j})
(see e.g.
[
36
, Lemma 3.12]
).
∎

Lemma 8.8
.

Let
(L,(l_{i,i+1})_{i})
be as in Theorem
8.5
.
Then the lattice
L_{{\mathbb{Z}}}
in
L
spanned by
(l_{i,i+1})_{i}
is
K^{\operatorname{num}}_{0}(Y)
.

Proof.

Clearly
L_{{\mathbb{Z}}}\subset K^{\operatorname{num}}_{0}(Y)
. For a point
p\in X
the map
{\chi_{X}(-,[{\mathcal{O}}_{p}]):K_{0}(X)\rightarrow{\mathbb{Z}}}
is surjective since
\chi_{X}([{\mathcal{O}}_{X}],[{\mathcal{O}}_{p}])=1
. One has
r_{i}=\chi_{X}([E_{i}],[{\mathcal{O}}_{p}])
, hence
we conclude
\gcd(\{r_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n-1})=1
. If
[K^{\operatorname{num}}_{0}(Y):L_{{\mathbb{Z}}}]:=d
, then
d
divides
\chi_{Y}(-,-)
when evaluated on
L_{{\mathbb{Z}}}
.
It follows from Lemma
8.6
that
d
divides
r_{i}^{2}
for all
i
which contradicts the fact that the
r_{i}
are coprime, unless
d=1
.
∎

Theorem 8.9
.

Let
(L,(l_{i,i+1})_{i=0}^{n-1})
be the Gale dual of
(\operatorname{Pic}(X)_{\mathbb{R}},(T_{i,i+1})_{i=0}^{n-1})
and let
L_{{\mathbb{Z}}}
be the lattice generated by
(l_{i,i+1})_{i}
.
Let
\omega
be the volume form on
L
such that the fundamental volume of the lattice
L_{{\mathbb{Z}}}
is
1
, satisfying in addition
\omega(l_{i-1,i},l_{i,i+1})>0
for some or, equivalently, all
i
.
Then

(1)

m_{i}=l_{i,i+1}-l_{i-1,i}
is
r_{i}
times a primitive element of
L_{{\mathbb{Z}}}
(hence
(r_{i})_{i}
are determined by
(l_{i,i+1})_{i}
).

(2)

l_{i,i+1}
is primitive in
L_{{\mathbb{Z}}}
.

(3)

r_{i}^{2}=\omega(l_{i-1,i},l_{i,i+1})
.

(4)

For
i<j
put
\chi_{ij}=\chi_{X}(E_{i},E_{j})
. Then

(8.11)

\chi_{i,i+1}=\frac{\omega(m_{i},m_{i+1})}{r_{i}r_{i+1}}

and

(8.12)

\chi_{i,j}=r_{i}r_{j}\sum_{k=i}^{j-1}\frac{\chi_{k,k+1}}{r_{k}r_{k+1}}=r_{i}r_{j}\sum_{k=i}^{j-1}\frac{\omega(m_{k},m_{k+1})}{(r_{k}r_{k+1})^{2}}.

(5)

The number of arrows from
i
to
j
in the quiver (see Lemma
5.11
) corresponding to
(E_{i})_{i}
is equal to

\frac{\omega(m_{i},m_{j})}{r_{i}r_{j}}

with the convention that a negative number corresponds to arrows from
j
to
i
.

Proof.

By Lemma
8.8
we may, and we will, assume that
L_{\mathbb{Z}}=K^{\operatorname{num}}_{0}(Y)
and
\omega=\chi_{Y}
.
Then (
1
) follows from (
8.3
) and the fact that
F_{j,Y}
are spherical and hence have primitive
(\text{rank},\text{degree})
-vectors.

To prove (
2
) we note that
(\operatorname{Pic}(X)_{\mathbb{R}},(T_{i,i+1})_{i=0}^{n-1})
is compatible with rotation of
(E_{i})_{i}
and hence so is its Gale dual

(L,(l_{i,i+1})_{i})
. Hence we may assume
i=n-1
. It follows from (
8.3
) that
l_{n-1,0}=-[{\mathcal{O}}_{p}]
which is clearly primitive.

(
3
) is just Lemma
8.6
.

(
5
) follows from Corollary
8.7
and Lemma
5.11
taking (
3
) into account.

(
8.11
) in (
4
) is a special case of (
5
). (
8.12
) in (
4
) follows from (
4.2
).
∎

Definition 8.10
.

The volume form
\omega
as introduced in Theorem
8.9
will be called the
normalised volume form
on
L
.

Below we will use the following geometric reinterpretation of Theorem
8.9
(
3
,
4
).

Lemma 8.11
.

Let
\omega
be an a volume form on
L
such that
\omega(l_{i-1,i},l_{i,i+1})>0
.
Define the triangles
A=\operatorname{conv}(0,l_{i-1,i},l_{i,i+1})
,
B=\operatorname{conv}(0,l_{i,i+1},l_{i+1,i+2})
,
C=\operatorname{conv}(l_{i-1,i},l_{i,i+1},l_{i+1,i+2})
. Then

(8.13)

\chi(E_{i},E_{i+1})=\frac{\operatorname{\Delta}(C)}{\sqrt{\operatorname{\Delta}(A)\operatorname{\Delta}(B)}}.

where
\Delta(C)
should be interpreted as a signed area.

Proposition 10.7 in
[
38
]
shows that
(l_{i,i+1})_{i=0}^{n-1}
defines a complete fan in
L
.
It follows that the intervals
[l_{i-1,i},l_{i,i+1}]
are the edges of a polygon
P
(see §
3
) that contains the origin.
We call
P
the
polygon associated to the full exceptional collection
{\mathbb{E}}
.
We think of the edges of
P
as being indexed by the elements of the full exceptional collection (i.e. the edge
[l_{i-1,i},l_{i,i+1}]
corresponds to
E_{i}
).
We recall the following beautiful result:

Proposition 8.12

(
[
38
, §9]
)
.

The collection
{\mathbb{E}}
is very strong if and only if
P
is convex.

The following observation will very useful for us.

Lemma 8.13
.

Let

{\mathbb{E}}=((E_{0}^{(0)},\ldots,E_{0}^{(\alpha_{0}-1)}),\ldots,(E_{k-1}^{(0)},\ldots,E_{k-1}^{(\alpha_{k-1}-1)}))

be a very strong block exceptional collection (see §
7.4
) without broken blocks (see Definition
7.9
).
Let
P
be the polygon associated to
{\mathbb{E}}
. Then the long edges of
P
(see §
3
)
correspond to the blocks in
{\mathbb{E}}
. The edges are obtained by dividing every long edge
e_{i}
, corresponding to the block
{\mathbb{E}}_{i}
, into
\alpha_{i}
equal segments
corresponding to
E_{i}^{(0)},\ldots,E_{i}^{(\alpha_{i}-1)}
, respectively.

Proof.

The right dual to the exceptional collection underlying
{\mathbb{E}}
can be made into a block exceptional collection as
stated in Lemma
7.8
.
Let

{\mathbb{F}}=({\mathbb{F}}_{k-1},\ldots,{\mathbb{F}}_{0})

be the result.
5
5

5

Recall that
{\mathbb{F}}
is constructed by first performing appropriate block mutations on
{\mathbb{E}}
and then inverting the order of the exceptional objects
in the blocks.

We claim that
{\mathbb{F}}
has no broken blocks.
The blocks
{\mathbb{F}}_{i},{\mathbb{F}}_{i-1}
for
i=k-1,\ldots,1
are not
orthogonal, since if they were they could be joined into a single block to form a new full block exceptional collection
{\mathbb{F}}^{\prime}
. Then the left dual
{\mathbb{E}}^{\prime}
of
{\mathbb{F}}^{\prime}
would have the same underlying exceptional collection as
{\mathbb{E}}
, but fewer blocks, contradicting the hypothesis that
{\mathbb{E}}
has no broken blocks.

It remains to show that
{\mathbb{F}}_{0}
is not orthogonal to
\omega_{X}^{-1}\otimes{\mathbb{F}}_{n-1}
. This follows from Lemma
8.14
below and Proposition
4.11
.

Using (
8.10
) applied to pairs
(F_{i+1},F_{i})
we see that the straight vertices of
P
are given by the intra block pairs and the non-straight vertices are given by pairs
joining two adjacent blocks. It remains to show that the straight vertices divide the long edges of
P
into equal segments. This follows from
the explicit model (
8.3
) and the fact that if
E,F
is an orthogonal exceptional pair then
[E_{Y}]=[F_{Y}]
in
K^{\operatorname{num}}_{0}(Y)_{{\mathbb{R}}}
. Indeed by (
4.2
)

[E_{Y}]
and
[F_{Y}]
are proportional. But since
E_{Y}
,
F_{Y}
are spherical objects on
Y
, their (rank,degree)-vectors are primitive. This finishes the proof.
∎

Lemma 8.14
.

Let

{\mathbb{E}}=(E_{0},\ldots,E_{n-1})

be a full exceptional collection on
X
and let

{\mathbb{F}}=(F_{n-1},\ldots,F_{0})

be its right dual. Then

\operatorname{RHom}_{X}(F_{0},\omega_{X}^{-1}\otimes F_{n-1}[-\dim X])=\operatorname{RHom}_{X}(E_{0},E_{n-1})

Proof.

According to Remark
4.6
one has
F_{n-1}=E_{n-1}
and
F_{0}=\omega_{X}^{-1}\otimes E_{0}[-\dim X]
. This yields what we want.
∎

9.
Mutations on the level of toric systems and polygons

9.1.
Description of mutations

Let
{\mathbb{E}}=(E_{0},\dots,E_{n-1})
be a full exceptional collection of vector bundles on a del Pezzo surface,
(T_{0,1},\dots,T_{n-1,0})
the corresponding toric system in
\operatorname{Pic}(X)_{\mathbb{Q}}
and
(L,(l_{0,1},\dots,l_{n-1,0}))
the Gale dual.
In this section we explain how mutations of
{\mathbb{E}}
act on
(l_{i,i+1})_{0}^{n-1}
. In (
9.4
) we do this for braid mutations (see §
4.1
), and in Proposition
9.12

we do this for quiver mutations (see Proposition
6.11
). See also
[
38
, Proposition 8.5]
for related formulas. As before we put
m_{i}=l_{i+1,i}-l_{i,i-1}
.

Lemma 9.1
.

Denote
h=\chi(E_{i},E_{i+1})
.

(1)

Assume
hr(E_{i})-r(E_{i+1})\neq 0
, i.e.
\tilde{\sigma}_{i+1}({\mathbb{E}})
consists of vector bundles. The toric system corresponding to

\tilde{\sigma}_{i+1}({\mathbb{E}})=(E_{0},\dots,E_{i-1},\widetilde{L_{E_{i}}}E_{i+1},E_{i},E_{i+2},\dots,E_{n-1})

is given by

(T_{0,1},\dots,T_{i-2,i-1},T_{i-1,i}-\alpha T_{i,i+1},\alpha T_{i,i+1},T_{i,i+1}+T_{i+1,i+2},T_{i+2,i+3},\dots,T_{n-1,0}),

where

\displaystyle\alpha=\frac{T_{i-1,i}\cdot T_{i,i+1}}{(T_{i,i+1}+T_{i+1,i+2})\cdot T_{i,i+1}}=\frac{r(E_{i+1})}{hr(E_{i})-r(E_{i+1})}=\frac{\omega(m_{i+1},l_{i+1,i+2})}{\omega(m_{i+1},l_{i-1,i})}.

For the Gale dual corresponding to
\tilde{\sigma}_{i+1}({\mathbb{E}})
we may take the following vectors in
L
:

(9.1)

(l_{0,1},\dots,l_{i-1,i},l_{i-1,i}+\frac{\omega(l_{i-1,i},m_{i+1})}{\omega(l_{i,i+1},m_{i+1})}m_{i+1},l_{i+1,i+2},\dots,l_{n-1,0}).

(2)

Assume
hr(E_{i+1})-r(E_{i})\neq 0
, i.e.
\tilde{\sigma}_{i+1}^{-1}({\mathbb{E}})
consists of vector bundles. The toric system corresponding to

\tilde{\sigma}_{i+1}^{-1}({\mathbb{E}})=(E_{0},\dots,E_{i-1},E_{i+1},\widetilde{R_{E_{i+1}}}E_{i},E_{i+2},\dots,E_{n-1})

is given by

(T_{0,1},\dots,T_{i-2,i-1},T_{i-1,i}+T_{i,i+1},\alpha T_{i,i+1},T_{1,2}-\alpha T_{i,i+1},T_{i+2,i+3},\dots,T_{n-1,0}),

where

\alpha=\frac{T_{i+1,i+2}\cdot T_{i,i+1}}{T_{i,i+1}\cdot(T_{i-1,i}+T_{i,i+1)}}=\frac{r(E_{i})}{hr(E_{i+1})-r(E_{i})}=-\frac{\omega(l_{i,i+1},m_{i})}{\omega(l_{i+1,i+2},m_{i})}.

Then for the Gale dual corresponding to
\tilde{\sigma}_{i+1}^{-1}({\mathbb{E}})
we may take the following vectors in
L
:

(9.2)

(l_{0,1},\dots,l_{i-1,i},l_{i+1,i+2}-\frac{\omega(l_{i+1,i+2},m_{i})}{\omega(l_{i,i+1},m_{i})}m_{i},l_{i+1,i+2},\dots,l_{n-1,0}).

Proof.

We will prove only the first part. Directly from the definition of
T_{ij}
’s we get that the toric system corresponding to

\tilde{\sigma}_{i+1}({\mathbb{E}})=(E_{0},\dots,E_{i-1},\widetilde{L_{E_{i}}}E_{i+1},E_{i},E_{i+2},\dots,E_{n-1})
is given by

(T_{0,1},\dots,T_{i-2,i-1},T^{\prime},\alpha T_{i,i+1},T_{i,i+1}+T_{i+1,i+2},T_{i+2,i+3},\dots,T_{n-1,0}),

for some
\alpha\in{\mathbb{Q}}
with
T^{\prime}+\alpha T_{i,i+1}=T_{i-1,i}
. Moreover, by Proposition
8.1
(
2
) we have
T^{\prime}\perp T_{i,i+1}+T_{i+1,i+2}
. Hence
\alpha=\frac{T_{i-1,i}\cdot T_{i,i+1}}{(T_{i,i+1}+T_{i+1,i+2})\cdot T_{i,i+1}}
. Using Proposition
8.1
(
1
,
4
) combined with (
8.2
) we have

\alpha=\frac{1/r(E_{i})^{2}}{1/r(E_{i+1})^{2}+(-1/r(E_{i})^{2}+h/r(E_{i})r(E_{i+1})-1/r(E_{i})^{2})}=\frac{r(E_{i+1})}{hr(E_{i})-r(E_{i+1})},

where
h=\chi(E_{i},E_{i+1})
. Using Theorem
8.9
(
3
,
4
) we compute

\displaystyle\frac{r(E_{i+1})}{hr(E_{i})-r(E_{i+1})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{\omega(l_{i,i+1},l_{i+1,i+2})}}{\frac{\omega(m_{i},m_{i+1})}{\sqrt{\omega(l_{i-1,i},l_{i,i+1})\omega(l_{i-1,i},l_{i,i+1})}}\sqrt{\omega(l_{i-1,i},l_{i,i+1})}-\sqrt{\omega(l_{i,i+1},l_{i+1,i+2})}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\omega(l_{i,i+1},l_{i+1,i+2})}{\omega(m_{i},m_{i+1})-\omega(l_{i,i+1},l_{i+1,i+2})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\omega(l_{i,i+1},m_{i+1})}{\omega(m_{i},m_{i+1})-\omega(l_{i,i+1},m_{i+1})}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\omega(l_{i,i+1},m_{i+1})}{\omega(l_{i-1,i},m_{i+1})}

By the definition of Gale dual (see Definition
8.2
) we have

l_{0,1}\otimes T_{0,1}+l_{1,2}\otimes T_{1,2}+\dots+l_{n-1,0}\otimes T_{n-1,0}=0\text{ in }L\otimes\operatorname{Pic}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}.

Then

\displaystyle l_{0,1}\otimes T_{0,1}+\dots+l_{i-1,i}\otimes(T_{i-1,i}-\alpha T_{i,i+1})+(l_{i-1,i}+\frac{l_{i,i+1}-l_{i+1,i+2}}{\alpha})\otimes\alpha T_{i,i+1}+

\displaystyle+l_{i+1,i+2}\otimes(T_{i,i+1}+T_{i+1,i+2})+\dots++l_{n-1,0}\otimes T_{n-1,0}=0\text{ in }L\otimes\operatorname{Pic}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}.

Invoking once again the definition of the Gale dual, we get
l_{i,i+1}^{\prime}=l_{i-1,i}-\frac{m_{i+1}}{\alpha}
. Substituting the expression for
\alpha

yields (
9.1
).
∎

Figure 9.1.
Geometric interpretation of
\tilde{\sigma}_{i+1}
on the level of
\{l_{i,i+1}\}_{0}^{n-1}
E_{i-1}
E_{i}
E_{i+1}
E_{i+2}
t
\ell^{\prime}_{i,i+1}
o
\ell_{i,i+1}
\ell_{i+1,i+2}
\ell_{i-1,i}
\ell_{i-2,i-1}

For
u,v\in L
such that
\omega(u,v)\neq 0
let
A_{uv}
be the unique affine transformation (shear mapping) of
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
such that

(1)

A_{uv}(u)=v
.

(2)

A_{uv}
is the identity on the line through the origin parallel to
[u,v]
.

Explicitly,
A_{uv}
is given by

(9.3)

A_{uv}(x)=x+\frac{\omega(x,v-u)}{\omega(u,v-u)}(v-u)

Using this notation we may rewrite the left and right mutations of
(l_{01},\ldots,l_{n-1,0})
as

(9.4)

\begin{gathered}(l_{0,1},\dots,l_{i-1,i},A_{l_{i,i+1},l_{i+1,i+2}}(l_{i-1,i}),l_{i+1,i+2},\dots,l_{n-1,0})\\
(l_{0,1},\dots,l_{i-1,i},A_{l_{i,i+1},l_{i-1,i}}(l_{i+1,i+2}),l_{i+1,i+2},\dots,l_{n-1,0}).\end{gathered}

See Figure
9.1
for an illustration of the first formula.
For use below we record the following trivial lemma.

Lemma 9.2
.

If
A_{uv}(x)=y
with
\omega(x,y)\neq 0
then
A_{uv}=A_{xy}
.

Definition 9.3
.

Assume
l_{j,j+1}
is not on the line through the origin, parallel to
m_{i}
.
The
generalised right mutation of

l_{i,i+1},\ldots,l_{i-1,i}
at
m_{i}
with respect to
l_{j,j+1}

is

(9.5)

(l_{0,1},\dots,l_{i-1,i},l^{\prime}_{i,i+1},\ldots,l^{\prime}_{j-1,j},l_{j,j+1},\ldots,l_{n-1,0}),

where
l^{\prime}_{i,i+1},\ldots,l^{\prime}_{j-1,j}:=A(l_{i+1,i+2}),\ldots,A(l_{j,j+1})
with
A:=A_{l_{i,i+1},l_{i-1,i}}
.
The expression (
9.5
) should be regarded as cyclic. I.e. the indices of the
(l^{\prime})_{u,u+1}

wrap around modulo
n
.

Remark 9.4
.

Geometrically, one can think of a generalised mutation as:

•

collapsing edge
m_{i}
;

•

displacing
m_{i+1},\ldots,m_{j}
to obtain edges
m^{\prime}_{i},\ldots,m^{\prime}_{j-1}
;

•

creating a new edge
m^{\prime}_{j}
at the vertex
l_{j,j+1}
(i.e. more explicitly
(l^{\prime}_{j-1,j},l_{j,j+1})
), parallel to the collapsed one.

Note that for
j=i+1
this operation coincides with the corresponding braid mutation described (
9.4
).
Moreover it follows from Lemma
9.2
that this operation is compatible with composition in the following sense:
the generalised right mutation at
m_{i}
with respect to
l_{j,j+1}
followed by the generalised right mutation at
l^{\prime}_{j-1,j}-l_{j,j+1}

(the newly created edge parallel to
m_{i}
) with respect to
l_{k,k+1}
is the same as the generalised right mutation at
m_{i}
with respect to
l_{k,k+1}
.

Definition 9.5
.

Let
{\mathbb{E}}=(E_{0},\ldots,E_{n-1})
be a full exceptional collection. The
generalised right mutation
of
{\mathbb{E}}
at
E_{0}
with respect to the pair
(E_{j},E_{j+1})
is

(9.6)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\sigma}_{j}^{-1}\cdots\tilde{\sigma}_{2}^{-1}\tilde{\sigma}_{1}^{-1}{\mathbb{E}}

\displaystyle=(E_{1},E_{2},\ldots,E_{j},\widetilde{R_{E_{j}}}\cdots\widetilde{R_{E_{1}}}E_{0},E_{j+1},\ldots,E_{n-1}).

The generalised right mutation of
{\mathbb{E}}
at
E_{i}
with respect to the pair
(E_{j},E_{j+1})
is obtained by first right rotating
{\mathbb{E}}

such that
E_{i}
is at the zeroth position
(and the
(E_{j},E_{j+1})
are at positions
(E_{j-i},E_{j+1-i})
),
performing the mutation as defined above and then performing the inverse left mutation.

Lemma 9.6
.

Let
{\mathbb{E}}^{\prime}
be a generalised right mutation of
{\mathbb{E}}
at
E_{i}
with respect to the pair
(E_{j},E_{j+1})
and assume both
{\mathbb{E}}
and
{\mathbb{E}}^{\prime}
consist of
vector bundles.
Let
\ell:=(l_{01},\ldots,l_{n-1,0})
and
\ell^{\prime}:=(l^{\prime}_{01},\dots,l^{\prime}_{n-1,0})
be respectively the Gale duals of the toric systems
associated to
{\mathbb{E}}
and
{\mathbb{E}}^{\prime}
. Then
(l^{\prime}_{01},\dots,l^{\prime}_{n-1,0})
is the generalised right mutation of
(l_{01},\ldots,l_{n-1,0})

at
m_{i}
with respect to
l_{j,j+1}
.

Proof.

By applying appropriate rotations we may assume that
i=0
so that we can use the formula (
9.6
). If
j=1
then the result follows from (
9.4
) (see also Figure
9.1
for the dual picture involving a left mutation).
If all
\tilde{\sigma}^{-1}_{k}\cdots\tilde{\sigma}_{1}^{-1}{\mathbb{E}}

are exceptional collections of vector bundles, then the claim follows from the compatibility of generalised right mutations with composition (see Remark
9.4
).

Sadly if some intermediate mutation involves a torsion sheaf, then things are more complicated. One possibility is to extend the machinery of toric systems to this setting
as is done in
[
38
, Definition 5.5]
. Here we choose a different route however.
We will replace
{\mathbb{E}}
,
{\mathbb{E}}^{\prime}
by the corresponding (real) numerical
exceptional collections
\mathfrak{e}
and
\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}
in
K_{0}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}
.

According to Lemma
9.10
below we may construct an embedded smooth curve
\mathfrak{e}_{t}:(-\epsilon,\epsilon)\rightarrow{\mathcal{M}}
such that
\mathfrak{e}_{0}=\mathfrak{e}

and such that for
t\neq 0
none of the
\sigma_{k}^{-1}\cdots\sigma_{1}^{-1}(\mathfrak{e}_{t})
contains
objects of rank
0
for
t\neq 0
. Let
\ell_{t}
be the Gale dual of the toric system (the latter defined via (
8.1
)) corresponding
to
6
6

6

It is easy to see that
\ell_{t}
can be constructed in such a way that it depends smoothly on
t
, e.g. using the formula (
8.3
).

\mathfrak{e}_{t}
.
Now let
\ell^{\prime}_{t}
be obtained by the generalised right mutation of
\ell_{t}
at
(m_{i})_{t}
with respect to
(l_{j,j+1})_{t}
. For
t\neq 0
it follows from
compatibility with compositions as in the first paragraph of this proof, that
\ell^{\prime}_{t}
is the Gale dual of
\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}_{t}:=\tilde{\sigma}_{j}^{-1}\cdots\tilde{\sigma}_{1}^{-1}\mathfrak{e}_{t}
.
Taking the limit
t\rightarrow 0
finishes the proof as it is clear that Definition
8.2
(
3
) is compatible with limits.
∎

Remark 9.7
.

A useful special case of this lemma is given when
l_{j,j+1}
is on the same long edge as
m_{i}
. Then
E_{i},\ldots,E_{j}
are orthogonal and the generalised
right mutation at
m_{i}
with respect to
l_{j,j+1}
corresponds to the permutation
E_{i},\ldots,E_{j}\mapsto E_{j+1},\ldots,E_{j},E_{i}
.

We now give the results that were used in the proof of Lemma
9.6
.

Lemma 9.8

(See also
[
4
, Theorem 3.2]
)
.

Let
V
be a real vector space of dimension
n
equipped with a bilinear form
\chi
.
Let
{\mathcal{M}}_{u}
be the real algebraic subvariety of
V^{u}
consisting of numerical exceptional collections of length
u
in
V
(with
{\mathcal{M}}_{0}
being just a point). Then
{\mathcal{M}}_{u}
is a submanifold
of
V^{u}
. Moreover the map
\pi:{\mathcal{M}}_{u+1}\rightarrow{\mathcal{M}}_{u}
which drops the last component is a fiber bundle over a Zariski
open subset of
{\mathcal{M}}_{u}
.

Proof.

Let
\chi^{+}
be the symmetrization of
\chi
.
Clearly
{\mathcal{M}}_{-1}
is a smooth submanifold of
V^{0}
since both sets are just single points. Assume that we have shown that
{\mathcal{M}}_{u}
is a submanifold of
V^{u}
.
Then the image of
\pi:{\mathcal{M}}_{u+1}\rightarrow{\mathcal{M}}_{u}
consists of the numerical exceptional collections
(e_{0},\ldots,e_{u-1})
which can be extended to a numerical
exceptional collection
(e_{0},\ldots,e_{u-1},e_{u})
. This is possible when
L={}^{\perp_{\chi}}(e_{0},\ldots,e_{u-1})
is not isotropic for the quadratic form
\chi^{+}
, which
is clearly a Zariski open condition. Moreover, the fiber
\pi^{-1}(e_{0},\ldots,e_{u-1})
corresponds to the
e_{u}
such that
\chi^{+}(e_{u},e_{u})=1
, which is a submanifold of
V
.
From this one deduces that
{\mathcal{M}}_{u+1}
is a submanifold of
V^{u+1}
.
∎

Corollary 9.9
.

If
v\geq u
then the projection map
{\mathcal{M}}_{v}\rightarrow{\mathcal{M}}_{u}
is a submersion.

Lemma 9.10
.

Let
V=K_{0}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}
and let
\mathfrak{e}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{n}
. Choose a generic tangent vector
v
at
\mathfrak{e}
in
{\mathcal{M}}_{n}

and let
\mathfrak{e}_{t}:(-\epsilon,\epsilon)\rightarrow{\mathcal{M}}_{n}
be an embedded smooth curve such that
\mathfrak{e}_{0}=\mathfrak{e}

and
(d\mathfrak{e}_{t}/dt)_{t=0}=v
. Then possibly after shrinking
\epsilon
, none of the numerical exceptional collections
\sigma_{k}^{-1}\cdots\sigma_{1}^{-1}(\mathfrak{e}_{t})
contains
objects of rank
0
for
t\neq 0
.

Proof.

It is sufficient to show that for any
k,l
the object
(\sigma_{k}^{-1}\cdots\sigma_{1}^{-1}(\mathfrak{e}_{t}))_{l}
does not have rank zero for
t\neq 0
for
(\mathfrak{e}_{t})_{t}

a particular embedded curve. Since
\sigma_{k}^{-1}\cdots\sigma_{1}^{-1}
is a diffeomorphism
{\mathcal{M}}_{n}\rightarrow{\mathcal{M}}_{n}
, we may assume
k=0
. Using a suitable rotation we may assume
l=0
.
If
\mathfrak{e}=(e_{0},\ldots,e_{n-1})
, then we put
(e^{\prime}_{0})_{t}=e_{0}+t[{\mathcal{O}}_{X}]
and
(e_{0})_{t}=(e^{\prime}_{0})_{t}/\sqrt{\chi((e_{0}^{\prime})_{t},(e_{0}^{\prime})_{t})}
. Then
(e_{0})_{0}=e_{0}
and for
t
small,
(e_{0})_{t}
is exceptional and has non-zero rank if
t\neq 0
.
Then, since
{\mathcal{M}}_{n}\rightarrow{\mathcal{M}}_{1}
is a submersion, a corresponding lift
(\mathfrak{e}_{t})_{t}
with the required properties exists.
∎

Recall that by Proposition
6.10
a quiver mutation can be expressed as a composition of a sequence of adjacent braid mutations. Hence
quiver mutations of very strong exceptional collections can be viewed as generalised mutations of the corresponding convex polygons. Below we make this explicit.

Definition 9.11
.

Let
H_{i}
denote the line parallel to
m_{i}
and such that it is the furthest from
m_{i}
but still intersects the polygon
\operatorname{conv}(l_{01},\ldots,l_{n-1,0})
. The vertices vertices
l_{j,j+1}
on the line
H_{i}
are called
opposing vertices
for
m_{i}
. The earliest
l_{j,j+1}
after
m_{i}
in the cyclic ordering
is called the
earliest opposing vertex
for
m_{i}
.

Proposition 9.12
.

Let
{\mathbb{E}}=(E_{0},\ldots,E_{n-1})
be a very strong exceptional collection with corresponding Gale dual

(l_{01},\ldots,l_{n-1,0})
.
Then the Gale dual of the toric
system associated to the quiver mutation at
E_{i}
is given by

(l_{0,1},\dots,l_{i-1,i},A(l_{i+1,i+2}),\ldots,A(l_{j,j+1}),l_{j,j+1},\ldots,l_{n-1,0}),

with
A:=A_{l_{i,i+1},l_{i-1,i}}
(with cyclic indices) and
l_{j,j+1}
the earliest opposing vertex for
m_{i}
.

Proof.

As usual we may assume
i=0
.
By Proposition
6.10
the quiver mutation at
0
of
{\mathbb{E}}
is of the form

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\sigma}_{j}^{-1}\cdots\tilde{\sigma}_{2}^{-1}\tilde{\sigma}_{1}^{-1}{\mathbb{E}}

\displaystyle=(E_{1},E_{2},\ldots,E_{j},\widetilde{R_{E_{j}}}\cdots\widetilde{R_{E_{1}}}E_{0},E_{j+1},\ldots,E_{n-1}).

for a suitable
j\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
such that
{\mathbb{E}}^{\prime}
is very strong.

Because both
{\mathbb{E}}
and
{\mathbb{E}}^{\prime}
are very strong, the corresponding polygons
P
,
P^{\prime}
are convex.
Moreover by Lemma
9.6

P^{\prime}
is obtained from
P
by the generalised right mutation at
m_{0}
with respect to
l_{j,j+1}
.
Direct inspection of Figure
9.2
shows that the set of
j
’s such that this generalised right mutation preserves convexity consists of
the opposing vertices for
m_{i}
.
Let
l_{j,j+1}
be the earliest opposing vertex. By compatibility with composition, the generalised right mutation at
m_{0}
with respect to another opposing vertex
l_{j^{\prime},j^{\prime}+1}
is the generalised right mutation with respect to
l_{j,j+1}
followed by a generalised right mutation within a long edge. By Remark
9.7
this corresponds to permuting the elements of a block, which has no effect on the resulting rolled-up helix algebra.
∎

Figure 9.2.
The green polygon
(l_{0,1}^{\prime},\dots,l_{j-1,j}^{\prime},l_{j,j+1},\dots,l_{n-1,0})
is obtained from the blue polygon
(l_{0,1},\dots,l_{n-1,0})
by the generalised right mutation at
m_{0}
with respect to
l_{j,j+1}
. Here this is also the quiver mutation at
m_{0}
.
o
l_{0,1}
l_{1,2}
l_{n-1,0}
l_{j,j+1}
l_{j+1,j+2}
l_{0,1}^{\prime}
l_{j-1,j}^{\prime}

9.2.
Reduction of ranks and area change

As before, let
{\mathbb{E}}=(E_{0},\dots,E_{n-1})
be a very strong exceptional collection and
P=\operatorname{conv}(l_{0,1},\dots,l_{n-1,0})
the corresponding polygon.
Assume that
\omega
is normalised. By Theorem
8.9
(
3
) (see also
[
31
, Proposition 4.17]
) we have

2\operatorname{\Delta}(P)=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\omega(l_{i-1,i},l_{i,i+1})=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}r(E_{i})^{2},

Hence a quiver mutation reduces the sum of the ranks of an exceptional collection (
the total rank
of an exceptional collection) if and only if it reduces the area of the corresponding polygon.

Let
P^{\prime}=\operatorname{conv}(l_{0,1},\dots,l_{i-1,i},l_{i,i+1}^{\prime},\dots,l_{j-1,j}^{\prime},l_{j,j+1},\dots,l_{n-1,0})
be the convex polygon obtained from
P
by the quiver mutation at
i
.

Lemma 9.13
.

\operatorname{\Delta}(P^{\prime})-\operatorname{\Delta}(P)<0
if and only if

\omega(m_{i},l_{j,j+1}+l_{i,i+1})<0

Proof.

To simplify the notations and without loss of generality we can assume
i=0
. Divide the polygon
P
into three parts

P=P_{0}\cup T\cup P_{1},

where
we put
P_{0}:=\operatorname{conv}(l_{j,j+1},l_{j+1,j+2},\ldots,l_{n-1,0})
,
P_{1}:=\operatorname{conv}(l_{0,1},l_{1,2},\dots,l_{j,j+1})
and
T:=\operatorname{conv}(l_{n-1,0},l_{0,1},l_{j,j+1})
(see the left part of Figure
9.3
).
Then for the mutated polygon
P^{\prime}
we have

P^{\prime}=P_{0}\cup T^{\prime}\cup P^{\prime}_{1},

where
T^{\prime}=\operatorname{conv}(l_{n-1,0},l_{j-1,j}^{\prime},l_{j,j+1})
and
P^{\prime}_{1}=\operatorname{conv}(l_{n-1,0},l^{\prime}_{0,1},\ldots,l^{\prime}_{j-1,j})
(see the right part of Figure
9.3
).

We have
\operatorname{\Delta}(P^{\prime}_{1})=\operatorname{\Delta}(A(P_{1}))=\operatorname{\Delta}(P_{0})
, since
A
is a shear mapping and hence preserves the area. Therefore
\operatorname{\Delta}(P^{\prime})-\operatorname{\Delta}(P)=\operatorname{\Delta}(T^{\prime})-\operatorname{\Delta}(T)
. On the other hand, we have

2\operatorname{\Delta}(T)=\omega(m_{0},l_{j,j+1}-l_{n-1,0})

and

\displaystyle 2\operatorname{\Delta}(T^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\omega(l_{j,j+1}-l_{n-1,0},l_{j,j+1}-l^{\prime}_{j-1,j})

\displaystyle=\frac{\omega(l_{j,j+1},m_{0})}{\omega(l_{0,1},m_{0})}\omega(l_{j,j+1}-l_{n-1,0},m_{0})

Hence

\displaystyle 2(\operatorname{\Delta}(T^{\prime})-\operatorname{\Delta}(T))

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\omega(l_{j,j+1},m_{0})}{\omega(l_{0,1},m_{0})}+1\right)\omega(l_{j,j+1}-l_{n-1,0},m_{0})

\displaystyle=\frac{\omega(l_{j,j+1}+l_{0,1},m_{0})}{\omega(l_{0,1},m_{0})}\omega(l_{j,j+1}-l_{n-1,0},m_{0})

One can see from the picture that
\omega(l_{j,j+1}-l_{n-1,0},m_{0})<0
and
\omega(l_{0,1},m_{0})>0
. Hence the sign of

\operatorname{\Delta}(T^{\prime})-\operatorname{\Delta}(T)
is indeed the same as the sign of
\omega(m_{0},l_{j,j+1}+l_{0,1})
.
∎

Corollary 9.14
.

No quiver mutation reduces the total rank of
{\mathbb{E}}
if and only if for all
i

(9.7)

\omega(m_{i},l_{j,j+1}+l_{i,i+1})\geq 0

where
j
is any opposing vertex for
m_{i}
.

We will now rewrite (
9.7
) in a way that is affinely invariant. In other words we will consider
L
as an affine plane instead of as
a 2-dimensional vector space. Since an affine plane does not have a fixed origin, the origin becomes part of the data. Then (
9.7
)
becomes

\omega(m_{i},l_{j,j+1}+l_{i,i+1}-2o)\geq 0

where
o
is the origin. This can be rewritten as

(9.8)

\omega(m_{i},o-l_{i,i+1})\leq\frac{1}{2}\omega(m_{i},l_{j,j+1}-l_{i,i+1}),

which gives an equation for the origin.

The line
H_{i}
(see Definition
9.11
) has equation

\omega(m_{i},x-l_{i,i+1})=\omega(m_{i},l_{j,j+1}-l_{i,i+1})

and the corresponding half plane
H^{+}
containing
l_{i,i+1}
is given by

\omega(m_{i},x-l_{i,i+1})\leq\omega(m_{i},l_{j,j+1}-l_{i,i+1}).

Comparing this with (
9.8
) we see that (
9.8
) holds if and only if
o
is in the half plane
{}^{1/2}H_{i}^{+}
containing
l_{i,i+1}
, where
{}^{1/2}H_{i}
is
the line parallel to
m_{i}
half way between
l_{i,i+1}
and
H_{i}
. This leads to the following definition.

Definition 9.15
.

The
forbidden region
of
P
is defined as
\bigcap_{i}{}^{1/2}H^{+}_{i}
.

Corollary 9.16
.

No quiver mutation reduces the total rank of
{\mathbb{E}}
if and only if
o
is in the forbidden region of
P
.

Remark 9.17
.

Clearly the notions we have defined above (such as the forbidden region) make sense for arbitrary convex polygons, and we will use them as such
without further comment. Note also that the forbidden region of a polygon
P
only depends on
|P|
(see §
3
).

In Figure
9.4
we illustrate the forbidden region for a polygon with respectively three and four non-straight vertices.

Definition 9.18
.

We say that
l_{j,j+1}
is
admissible
if it is an opposing vertex of some
m_{i}
.
In that case we will also call the edge
m_{i}
an
opposing edge
for
l_{j,j+1}
.

The significance of admissibility lies in the following easy result which we leave as an exercise for the reader.

Lemma 9.19
.

Deleting (see §
3
) a non-admissible vertex from a polygon enlarges the forbidden region.

In Figure
9.4
,
l_{3,0}
is the only non-admissible vertex in the right polygon. One checks
that deleting it indeed enlarges the forbidden region.

10.
Polygon shapes and quiver shapes

10.1.
Setting

In this section
X
is a del Pezzo surface
and
{\mathbb{E}}
is a very strong exceptional collection on
X
.
After performing a rotation we may, and we will, assume that
{\mathbb{E}}
is the underlying exceptional collection of a block exceptional collection
without broken blocks,
which we denote by the same symbol.

{\mathbb{E}}=((E_{0}^{(0)},\ldots,E_{0}^{(\alpha_{0}-1)}),\ldots,(E_{k-1}^{(0)},\ldots,E_{k-1}^{(\alpha_{k-1}-1)}))

We will discuss some restrictions on the shape of the quiver
Q
and the polygon
P
associated
to
{\mathbb{E}}
. Recall that the shape of
P
was described in Lemma
8.13
.

Definition 10.1
.

We say that
{\mathbb{E}}
or
Q
is
block-complete
if
Q_{\operatorname{/}}
is a complete quiver.

Our results will be mainly for the block-complete case, but this is not really a restriction as we show in the next section.

10.2.
Reduction to the block-complete case

Lemma 10.2
.

{\mathbb{E}}
is block-complete if and only if
P
has no parallel long edges.

Proof.

This follows immediately from Theorem
8.9
(
5
).
∎

Corollary 10.3
.

If
{\mathbb{E}}
is not block complete then it can be tranformed, via a sequence of quiver mutations, into a block-complete very strong exceptional collection
which reduces the number of blocks and does not increase the total rank.

Proof.

If
{\mathbb{E}}
is not block complete then by Lemma
10.2

this implies it has parallel long edges.
We have to prove that
P
can be mutated into a polygon without parallel long edges and fewer long edges, and this without increasing total rank.

If
e
,
e^{\prime}
are parallel long edges
respectively divided into subedges
e^{(0)},\ldots,e^{(\alpha-1)}
,
e^{\prime(0)},\ldots,e^{\prime(\alpha^{\prime}-1)}
then by performing quiver mutations at
e^{(\alpha-1)},\ldots,e^{(0)}
as described
in Proposition
9.12
(see Figure
9.2
for an illustration) we see that
e
shrinks to a point and
e^{\prime}
is replaced by a long edge divided into
\alpha+\alpha^{\prime}
edges. In other words
the resulting polygon has fewer long edges. Moreover it follows from Lemma
9.13
that the effect on the total rank of this procedure depends on the
position of the origin with respect to the line half way between
e
and
e^{\prime}
. It follows that if the procedure increases the total rank then executing the same procedure but using
e^{\prime}
instead of
e
will reduce the total rank. Hence we may assune that the prodcedure does not increase total rank.
By repeating the procedure we end up with a polygon without parallel edges without increasing the total rank.
∎

Remark 10.4
.

On the level of
{\mathbb{E}}
, the sequence of quiver mutations that eliminates parallel edges
P
is a suitable block quiver mutation as explained in Remark
7.14
. So the sequence of quiver mutations we have constructed in the proof of Corollary
10.3
can be written as a sequence of block quiver mutations.

10.3.
Quadratic quiver relations

Lemma 10.5
.

Assume
E_{0},E_{1},E_{2},E_{3}\in{\mathcal{D}}(Y)
for a smooth projective surface
Y
. Let
\chi^{-}
be the anti-symmetrization of
\chi
. Then

(10.1)

\chi^{-}(E_{0},E_{1})\chi^{-}(E_{2},E_{3})-\chi^{-}(E_{0},E_{2})\chi^{-}(E_{1},E_{3})+\chi^{-}(E_{0},E_{3})\chi^{-}(E_{1},E_{2})=0

Proof.

Let
s
be the action of the Serre functor on
K_{0}(Y)
. It is easy to see that
\operatorname{ker}(s-1)
is contained in the radical of
\chi^{-}(-,-)
.
By
[
12
, Proposition B]
,
\operatorname{rk}(s-1)\leq 2
. Hence
\chi^{-}(-,-)
factors through a vector space of dimension
\leq 2
. Then (
10.1
) follows from the Plücker identity.
∎

Corollary 10.6
.

Assume
(E_{0},E_{1},E_{2},E_{3})
is an exceptional collection in
{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(Y)
where
Y
is as above. Then

(10.2)

\chi(E_{0},E_{1})\chi(E_{2},E_{3})-\chi(E_{0},E_{2})\chi(E_{1},E_{3})+\chi(E_{0},E_{3})\chi(E_{1},E_{2})=0

Proof.

If
(E,F)
is an exceptional pair then
\chi^{-}(E,F)=\chi(E,F)
.
∎

Corollary 10.7
.

Assume that
{\mathbb{E}}=(E_{0},\ldots,E_{n-1})
is a very strong exceptional collection on
X
.
Let
c
be the incidence matrix of the corresponding quiver
Q
, i.e.
c_{ij}=|Q(i,j)|
if there are arrows
i\rightarrow j
and
c_{ij}=-|Q(j,i)|
if there are arrows
j\rightarrow i
.
Then for all 4-tuples of vertices
(i_{0},i_{1},i_{2},i_{3})
of
Q
we have

(10.3)

c_{i_{0}i_{1}}c_{i_{2}i_{3}}-c_{i_{0}i_{2}}c_{i_{1}i_{3}}+c_{i_{0}i_{4}}c_{i_{1}i_{2}}=0

Proof.

This follows by applying (
10.2
) to the dual exceptional collection of
{\mathbb{E}}
.
∎

Remark 10.8
.

For use below we make this a bit more concrete. Assume a subquiver of
Q
spanned by 4 vertices is as in Figure
10.1
, with a negative multiplicity indicating that the arrow goes
in the opposite direction.

Figure 10.1.
a
b
c
d
e
f

Then (
10.3
) becomes

(10.4)

ab-ef-cd=0.

This implies e.g. that if
a,b,c,d,e,f\geq 1
then
a=b=1
is not possible. Note that it does not matter how we order the vertices, we always get the same equation.

10.4.
Quiver shapes in the block-complete case

Lemma 10.9
.

Let
(E,(F^{(0)},\ldots,F^{(\alpha-1)}),G)
,
\alpha\geq 1
be a strong
7
7

7

According to Convention
7.12
this means that the underlying exceptional
collection is
strong
, i.e. there are no higher
\operatorname{Ext}
’s.

block exceptional collection of vector bundles on
X
(in particular
r(F^{(0)})=\cdots=r(F^{(\alpha-1)})
) such that the
quiver corresponding to
\operatorname{End}_{X}(E\oplus F^{(0)}\oplus\cdots\oplus F^{(\alpha-1)}\oplus G)
is given by

(10.5)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{(0)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{(\alpha-1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}

where
a,b,c
are the multiplicities of the corresponding arrows. Assume that

{a,b,c\geq 1}
.
Then we have

(10.6)

r(F^{(i)})\leq\max(r(E),r(G))

and moreover equality happens if and only if all of the following conditions hold

(10.7)

\begin{gathered}r(E)=r(G)\\
\alpha=1\\
a=1\text{ or }b=1\\
c=1\end{gathered}

Proof.

From (
4.2
) we have the identity

\chi(E,G)r(F^{(i)})=\chi(E,F^{(i)})r(G)+\chi(F^{(i)},G)r(E)

where

\displaystyle\chi(E,F^{(i)})

\displaystyle=a

\displaystyle\chi(F^{(i)},G)

\displaystyle=b

\displaystyle\chi(E,G)

\displaystyle=\alpha ab+c

Hence

(10.8)

r(F^{(i)})=\frac{a}{\alpha ab+c}r(G)+\frac{b}{\alpha ab+c}r(E)

We have

\displaystyle\frac{a}{\alpha ab+c}+\frac{b}{\alpha ab+c}

\displaystyle=\frac{a+b}{\alpha ab+c}

\displaystyle=1-\frac{\alpha ab+c-a-b}{\alpha ab+c}

\displaystyle=1-\frac{(\alpha-1)ab+(a-1)(b-1)+c-1}{\alpha ab+c}

\displaystyle\leq 1

which implies (
10.6
). By revisiting the
computation
it is easy to see that equality in (
10.6
)
can only happen under the conditions listed in (
10.7
).
∎

We now revert to our general setting introduced in §
10.1
. All indices are taken
\operatorname{mod}k
.

Definition 10.10
.

A long edge in
P
is
narrowing
if the sum of its interior angles with the adjacent edges is
<\pi
.

Lemma 10.11
.

The polygon
P
cannot have two non-adjacent narrowing long edges.

Proof.

Let the long edges be
e_{0},\cdots,e_{k-1}
. Then we have

(10.9)

\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\angle(e_{i},e_{i+1})=2\pi

(here
\angle(e_{i},e_{i+1})
is the exterior angle from
e_{i}
to
e_{i+1}
).
On the other hand if
e_{j}
is a narrowing long edge then
\angle(e_{j-1},e_{j})+\angle(e_{j},e_{j+1})>\pi
. This finishes the proof.
∎

The following lemma is absolutely crucial for our classification results in §
13
.

Lemma 10.12
.

Assume
{\mathbb{E}}
is block-complete. Then
P
has at least one narrowing long edge.

Proof.

Assume first that there is no narrowing long edge. By Theorem
8.9
(
5
)
this implies that the subquiver spanned by all
E_{i-1}^{(\alpha_{i-1}-1)},E_{i}^{(0)},\ldots,E_{i}^{(\alpha_{i}-1)},E_{i+1}^{(0)}
is as in (
10.5
).

By Lemma
10.9
this implies that

r(E_{i}^{(?)})\leq\max(r(E_{i-1}^{(?)}),r(E_{i+1}^{(?)}))
for all
i
(indices
\operatorname{mod}k
), with equality if and only if
r(E_{i-1}^{(?)})=r(E_{i}^{(?)})=r(E_{i+1}^{(?)})
.
This quickly implies that all
r(E_{i}^{(?)})
are equal.
Assume there is an
i
such that
|Q(i,i+1)|\neq 1
. Then again by Lemma
10.9

|Q(i-1,i)|=1
,
|Q(i+1,i+2)|=1
,
|Q(i-1,i+1)|=1
,
|Q(i,i+2)|=1
.
This is like in Figure
10.1
except that we do not know the sign of
d
. However (
10.4
) yields
d=0
which contradicts block-completeness.
Hence for all
i
we have
|Q(i,i+1)|=1
and also
|Q(i,i+2)|=1
(again using Lemma
10.9
). But this is also impossible, for exactly the same reason.
This contradiction finishes the proof.
∎

Corollary 10.13
.

Assume
{\mathbb{E}}
is block-complete.
Then exactly one of the following is true.

(1)

P
has a single narrowing long edge.

(2)

P
has exactly two narrowing long edges. Moreover these long edges are adjacent.

(3)

P
is a triangle (i.e. it has exactly 3 long edges and they are all narrowing).

Proof.

By Lemma
10.12

P
has at least on narrowing long edge.
Using Lemma
10.11
we deduce that this yields the possibilities listed in the corollary.
∎

Lemma 10.14
.

Assume
{\mathbb{E}}
is block complete and let
e
be a narrowing long edge of
P
with adjacent vertices
u
,
v
. Then
u
,
v
are admissible (see Definition
9.18
) and there is exactly one
other admissible vertex. In particular the total number of admissible vertices is three.

Proof.

Note that since
P
has no parallel edges by Lemma
10.2
a straight vertex can never be admissible. Also for the same reason the opposing
vertex to an edge is unique.

Let the vertices of
P
be numbered as
l_{-1,0},\ldots,l_{\alpha_{0}-1,\alpha_{0}},\ldots
with
u=l_{-1,0}
,
v=l_{\alpha_{0}-1,\alpha_{0}}
(indices modulo
n:=\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}
).
We first claim that if
[l_{i-1,i},l_{i,i+1}]
is an edge not on
e
then the opposing vertex is either
u
or
v
.
Recall that by Definition
9.18
the opposing vertex
l_{j,j+1}
is characterised by the fact that
\delta_{i}(x)=\omega(m_{i},x-l_{i,i+1})
takes maximal
value for
x=l_{j,j+1}
. By considering
x=l_{j-1,j}
and
x=l_{j+1,j+2}
we see that this implies
\omega(m_{i},m_{j})>0
,
\omega(m_{i},m_{j+1})<0
. By (
10.9
)
we have

(10.10)

\omega(m_{p},m_{q})\geq 0\quad\text{for $m_{p}$ not on $e$ and $q=p+1,\ldots,-1$}.

Hence
\omega(m_{i},m_{j+1})<0
implies
j+1=0
(since
l_{j,j+1}
must be a straight vertex) or
j+1=\alpha_{0},\ldots,i-1
. But if
j+1=\alpha_{0}+1,\ldots,i-1
then by (
10.10
)
\omega(m_{j},m_{i})\geq 0
, contradicting
\omega(m_{i},m_{j})>0
.

The opposing vertex for
m_{-1}
cannot be the adjacent vertex
l_{-1,0}
. Hence it must be
v=l_{\alpha_{0}-1,\alpha_{0}}
. So
v
is admissible. With a similar argument we see that
u
is admissible.

We have determined that the opposing vertex for all edges except those on
e
is either
u
or
v
. On the other hand the opposing vertex for an edge on
e
cannot be
u
or
v
. Hence it is the third admissible vertex, whose existence is asserted in the statement of the lemma. This finishes the proof.
∎

Example 10.15
.

The two possible polygon shapes and the corresponding reduced quiver shapes for
k=5
(computed via Theorem
8.9
(
5
)) are given in Figure

10.2
. The picture on the left corresponds to the case that there are two narrowing long edges while in the picture on the right there is only a single
one (i.e. these cases correspond to the possibilities
2
and
1
in Corollary
10.13
respectively). The meaning of
the flat blue dots will be explained below.

The examples in Figure
10.2
suggest the following result which follows easily from Lemma
10.14
.

Lemma 10.16
.

In the block complete case
for
k\geq 4
there are
k-3
possible reduced quiver shapes, namely, a single possibility with two narrowing long edges and
k-4
possibilities with
one narrowing long edge. These
k-4
possibilities are further distinguished by the location of the unique admissible vertex which is not on the narrowing long edge.

We also obtain the following result.

Proposition 10.17
.

Assume
{\mathbb{E}}
is block complete.
Draw the reduced quiver
Q_{\operatorname{/}}
inside
P
as in Figure
10.2
. Then it is possible to find a polygon
\Sigma
bounded by arrows of
Q_{\operatorname{/}}
so that
all arrows in
Q_{\operatorname{/}}
travel in the counter clockwise direction around
\Sigma
. Moreover
\Sigma
is adjacent to a vertex of
Q_{\operatorname{/}}
.

In Figure
10.2
the distinguished polygons
\Sigma
are marked by fat blue dots.

Remark 10.18
.

In
[
16
]
Herzog informally conjectured that a polygon like
\Sigma
should exist
8
8

8

In the middle of
[
16
, p16]
is it stated: “That well split implies the existence of such a polygon is not trivial, and
we leave it as a conjecture.”
and Proposition
10.17
in fact proves this conjecture in the block complete case. Note however that, as stated in the proposition,
the location of
\Sigma
is more constrained than suggested in loc. cit.
In particular the quiver
in the top left of Figure 1 in loc. cit. cannot
be the reduced quiver of a rolled-up helix algebra on a del Pezzo surface when all the arrow multiplicities are non-zero.

Reduced quivers for 5 blocks.

11.
All NCCRs are related by mutations

Definition 11.1
.

We say that two geometric helices (see §
4.4
) on a del Pezzo surface are
equivalent
if they can be transformed into each other by simultaneous shifts in the derived category, tensoring by line bundles, reordering of objects in orthogonal blocks and rotations (shifting the labels of objects). Two very strong exceptional collections are equivalent if the corresponding geometric helices are equivalent.

Theorem 11.2

(See also
[
8
]
)
.

All geometric helices on a del Pezzo surface can be transformed into each other by quiver mutations, up to equivalence.

Proof.

According to Remark
4.28
we may assume that a
geometric helix consist of sheaves and hence that it is obtained from
a very strong exceptional collection. By alternatingly applying Corollary
10.3

and reduction of total rank by block quiver mutations,
every very strong exceptional collection can be reduced
to a block complete very strong exceptional collection whose total rank cannot be further reduced
by block quiver mutations. Section §
12
gives a complete list of the Gram
matrices of such collections, up to
rotation. This list if obtained in §
13
.

Hence it remains to show that all the
very strong exceptional collections with these Gram matrices can be transformed into each other by quiver mutations, up to equivalence.
This explained in detail in §
12.1
.
∎

Theorem 11.3
.

All basic graded NCCRs of
R_{X}=\bigoplus_{k\leq 0}\Gamma(X,\omega_{X}^{\otimes k})
(alternatively, NCCRs of the completion
\widehat{R_{X}}
) for
X
a del Pezzo surface are related by sequences of mutations (up to regrading in the graded case).

Proof.

By Theorem
5.13
every basic graded NCCR of
R_{X}
is obtained from a very strong exceptional collection on
X
(equivalently, a geometric helix of sheaves). Moreover, mutations of NCCRs can be viewed as quiver mutations of underlying helices (see §
6
). The result now follows from Theorem
11.2

since equivalent geometric helices have isomorphic NCCRs.
∎

12.
Minimal block-complete very strong exceptional collections

12.1.
Introduction

Below we provide the data backing the proof of Theorem
11.2
.
The data is grouped by surface where
X_{i}
is the surface obtained by blowing up
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
in
i
points.

First of all, for each surface we provide the list of Gram matrices of the block-complete very strong exceptional collections of minimal total rank, up to rotation.
Each Gram matrix has a unique label of the form
(b,n)
where

b
is the number of blocks (which must be 3 or 4) and
n
is an integer.
The block sizes and the ranks are
respectively denoted by
(\alpha_{i})_{i}
and
(r_{i})_{i}
. The matrix
M_{/}
is the reduced Gram matrix and
Q_{/}
is the associated reduced quiver, which depends only on
M_{/}

and
(\alpha_{i})_{i}
(see §
7.5
). For each listed Gram matrix we provide an example of a very strong exceptional collection, appropriately broken into blocks, that realises it. Each object is written in the form
9
9

9

It follows from the Riemann-Roch formula (
4.4
) that an exceptional class is uniquely determined by

[r(E),c_{1}(E)]
, provided
r(E)\neq 0
.

[r(E),c_{1}(E)]
, where

c_{1}(E)\in\operatorname{Pic}(X)
is given by coordinates in the standard basis
(see §
4.3
). The fact that the collection is numerically exceptional and has the given Gram matrix can be verified using the formula (
4.1
).
The fact that it is uniquely realised by an actual exceptional collection follows from Proposition
4.18
.
The fact that our list is complete in the 3-block case follows directly from
[
23
]
. The fact that it is complete in the 4-block case was verified by computer
as explained in §
13.5
below. The fact that no higher block counts occur is explained in §
13.6
and §
13.7
.

For each surface, in a section entitled “Relations”, we provide sequences of quiver mutations, applied from left to right,
indexed in such a way that
i
stands for the left quiver mutation
that starts with
\tilde{\sigma}_{i}
(see Remark
6.12
),
that connect all the different Gram matrices, up to rotation. A label
(3,\ast)
refers to an unspecified
3
-block Gram matrix for that surface. Note
that this may not be the Gram matrix of a minimal collection, in which case it is not in the list. However thanks to
[
23
]
it can be further reduced to a minimal one.

For each surface we also provide a proof (see below), for one of the listed exceptional
collections, that the action of every element of the symmetry group (see
§
4.3
) yields an exceptional collection that is reachable by quiver
mutations, up to equivalence (see Definition
11.1
).

So, since for every surface all Gram matrices are connected by quiver mutations, and since for one of the Gram matrices all collections with that Gram matrix are also connected by quiver mutations, up to equivalence,
this concludes the proof that for each surface all collections are connected by quiver mutations, up to equivalence.

The proof that every exceptional collection in the symmetry group orbit of a particular very strong exceptional collection
{\mathbb{E}}
is reachable by quiver mutations, up to equivalence,
involves what we call a
certificate
.
A certificate is a set of pairs
(w,m)
where
w
is a sequence
of simple reflections indexed as in SageMath (see below) and

m
is a sequence of left quiver mutations indexed in the same way as above.
The pairs
(w,m)
satisfy
w({\mathbb{E}})\sim m({\mathbb{E}})
where all sequences (both simple reflections and quiver mutations) are applied from left to right.
Moreover the list of Weyl group element represented by the
w
’s
generates the whole group.

Our certificates are generated by computer search and it is possible, and even likely, that much shorter ones exist. In particular if
the symmetry group for a surface is trivial
or if the action of every reflection yields an equivalent collection (see Definition
11.1
) then there is a particularly trivial certificate
and therefore we flag these cases separately.

Finally, as explained above, in the certificate we index the simple reflections as in
[
42
]
.
We will clarify this now. If
(s_{i})_{i}
are the simple reflections associated with the simple roots as listed in Theorem
4.15
and
(s^{\prime}_{i})_{i}
are the simple reflections
used by SageMath then
s^{\prime}_{i}=s_{i}
except for the following modifications:

\begin{cases}s^{\prime}_{1},s^{\prime}_{3}=s_{3},s_{1}&\text{for $X_{4}$,}\\
s^{\prime}_{0},s^{\prime}_{1},s^{\prime}_{2},s^{\prime}_{3}=s_{1},s_{2},s_{3},s_{0}&\text{for $X_{5}$,}\\
s^{\prime}_{0},s^{\prime}_{1}=s_{1},s_{0}&\text{for $X_{n}$ for $n\geq 6$}.\end{cases}

\mathbb{P}^{2}

Minimal collections for ​

Label (3, 2)

\begin{aligned} \alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}&=1,2,1\\[-2.84526pt]
r_{0},r_{1},r_{2}&=1,1,1\\[-2.84526pt]

13.
Derivation of the list of minimal collections

13.1.
Main result

Theorem 13.1
.

Let
X
be a del Pezzo surface and let
{\mathbb{E}}
be a very strong block exceptional collection on
X
without broken blocks (see Definition
7.9
).
Assume that
{\mathbb{E}}
is block complete (see §
10.1
) and ‘‘minimal’’ in the sense that its total rank cannot be reduced by a block quiver mutation

10
10

10

Since the forbidden region does not depend on the straight vertices (see Remark
9.17
) this is equivalent to saying that no single quiver mutation reduces rank.
(see §
6
). Then the number of blocks in
{\mathbb{E}}
is
\leq 4
. All possible reduced Gram matrices and reduced quivers are listed
in §
12
.

The fact that §
12
lists all the Gram matrices in the 3-block case follows directly from
[
23
]
. In the subsequent sections with will treat the cases
of four blocks, five blocks and six or more blocks separately. We will start however with a discussion of the methods that we use.
In general they are a combination of plane geometry arguments, to establish various inequalities relating the areas of regions inside the polygon
P
associated on
{\mathbb{E}}
, and“integrality
constraints”, reflecting the fact that various
numbers computed from
P
need to be integral.

Some of the discussions below must be completed by computer computations. The accompanying scripts can be found at
[
37
]
.

13.2.
General approach

Let

{\mathbb{E}}=((E_{0}^{(0)},\ldots,E_{0}^{(\alpha_{0}-1)}),\ldots,(E_{k-1}^{(0)},\ldots,E_{k-1}^{(\alpha_{k-1}-1)}))

be as in the statement of Theorem
13.1
.
As usual
P
is the polygon corresponding to
{\mathbb{E}}
and
L
is its ambient space.
We will equip
L
with the lattice
L_{{\mathbb{Z}}}
generated by the vertices of
P
and with the normalised volume form
\omega
(see Definition
8.10
). The quiver associated to
B({\mathbb{E}})
is denoted by
Q
.

Convention 13.2
.

Whenever something depends only on the block, but not on the choice of an object in that block, we will index it by block number, i.e. throughout we write

\chi_{ij}=\chi(E_{i}^{(?)},E_{j}^{(?)})
and
r_{i}=r(E_{i}^{(?)})
. The vectors
(l_{i,i+1})_{i}
will
denote the non-straight vertices of
P
and
(m_{i})_{i}
will be the long edges corresponding to the blocks
({\mathbb{E}}_{i})_{i}
.

We state some relevant facts.

(1)

By Lemma
10.2
the polygon
P
has no parallel edges.
Moreover by Lemma
8.13
its long edges correspond to the blocks in
{\mathbb{E}}
and they are subdivided into
\alpha_{i}
equal segments
corresponding respectively to
E_{i}^{(j)}
for
j=0,\ldots,\alpha_{i}-1
.

(2)

The fact that
{\mathbb{E}}
is assumed to be block complete means that the shape of
P
is very constrained by Corollary
10.13
.

(3)

The hypothesis that the total rank of
{\mathbb{E}}
cannot be a reduced by a quiver mutation implies that the origin
o
is in the forbidden region (see Definition
9.15
) in
P
.

(4)

We frequently need to make assertions about the forbidden region and for this Lemma
9.19
is very useful as it allows us to delete vertices from
|P|
(recall that by Remark
9.17
the forbidden region only depends on
|P|
).

(5)

We have

(13.1)

\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{k-1}+K^{2}_{X}=12

To see this note that this equation is correct for
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
and
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
and it is preserved under blow up and blow down.

(6)

From (
4.2
) we obtain

(13.2)

\frac{\chi_{01}}{r_{0}r_{1}}+\frac{\chi_{12}}{r_{1}r_{2}}+\cdots+\frac{\chi_{k-1,0}}{r_{k-1}r_{0}}=K_{X}^{2}

where for reasons of symmetry we commit an abuse of notation by writing
\chi_{k-1,0}:=\chi(E_{k-1}^{(?)},\omega_{X}^{-1}\otimes E_{0}^{(?)})
.
Our main approach will be to use plane geometry to carefully bound the various terms in (
13.2
) e.g. using the formula (
8.13
).

(7)

The data
(r_{i})_{i}
,
(\chi_{i,i+1})_{i}
determines the full reduced Gram matrix of
{\mathbb{E}}
via

(13.3)

\frac{\chi_{ij}}{r_{i}r_{j}}=\frac{\chi_{i,i+1}}{r_{i}r_{i+1}}+\frac{\chi_{i+1,i+2}}{r_{i+1}r_{i+2}}+\cdots+\frac{\chi_{j-1,j}}{r_{j-1}r_{j}}

for
1\leq i<j\leq k-1
which also follows from (
4.2
).

(8)

It will be convenient to write
R_{i}=\omega(l_{i-1,i},l_{i,i+1})
. Then from

8.9
(
4
) we obtain

(13.4)

R_{i}=\alpha_{i}r_{i}^{2}

(9)

Likewise it will be convenient to write.

(13.5)

S_{i,i+1}=2\Delta(l_{i-1,i},l_{i,i+1},l_{i+1,i+2})=\omega(m_{i},m_{i+1})

Then
from
8.9
(
4
) we obtain

(13.6)

\chi_{i,i+1}=\frac{S_{i,i+1}}{\alpha_{i}\alpha_{i+1}r_{i}r_{i+1}}

(10)

Let
c_{ij}
be the number of arrows from
i
to
j
in
Q_{\operatorname{/}}
with the usual convention that a negative number denotes

-c_{ij}
arrows in the reverse direction. Then with a short computation using Theorem
8.9
(
3
,
5
) we find

(13.7)

c_{ij}^{2}\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}=\frac{\omega(m_{i},m_{j})^{2}}{\omega(l_{i-1,j},l_{i,i+1})\omega(l_{j-1,j},l_{j,j+1})}

Note that
c_{i,i+1}=\chi_{i,i+1}
and in that case (
13.7
) is a rewrite of (
13.6
).

(11)

Assuming we know
(\alpha_{i})_{i}
, the reduced Gram matrix determines the Gram matrix
M
. This can be used to further prune the solutions since
the “Serre matrix”
s=M^{-1}M^{t}
satisfies some very strong conditions (see e.g.
[
11
, Definition 3.2.1]
). I.e.

(a)

s
is unipotent.

(b)

\operatorname{rk}(s-1)\leq 2
.

(12)

To ultimately validate the existence of a particular Gram matrix one may supply an explicit exceptional collection realising that Gram matrix.

(13)

To manipulate sums of reciprocals as in (
13.2
) we may use the following lemma:

Lemma 13.3
.

Let
a>0
and assume that
(S_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
are subsets of
{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
so that for all
i
and for all
b>0
we have that
S_{i}\cap[b,\infty)
is finite.
Then the set

S=\{s_{1}+\cdots+s_{n}\mid\forall i:s_{i}\in S_{i},s_{1}+\cdots+s_{n}<a\}

has a maximum for all
a>0
. Moreover the set

T=\{s_{1},s_{2},\ldots,s_{n}\mid\forall i:s_{i}\in S_{i},s_{1}+\cdots+s_{n}=a\}

is finite.

Proof.

We will start with proving the first claim using induction on
n
. The case
n=1
follows from the fact that by assumption
S_{1}\cap[s,\infty)
is finite for any
s\in S_{1}
.

Now assume
n>1
. Let
m
be the maximum of

\{s_{2}+\cdots+s_{n}\mid\forall i\geq 2:s_{i}\in S_{i},s_{2}+\cdots+s_{n}<a\}

and for any
s\in S_{1}\cap[0,a)
let
m_{s}
be the maximum of

\{s_{2}+\cdots+s_{n}\mid\forall i\geq 2:s_{i}\in S_{i},s_{2}+\cdots+s_{n}<a-s\}

We need to prove that
s+m_{s}
has a maximum for
s\in S_{1}
.

Clearly if
a-s>m
then
m_{s}=m
and so
s+m_{s}=s+m
. Moreover
s<a-m
and by the case
n=1
the set
\{s<a-m\}
has a maximum.

If
a-s\leq m
then
s\geq a-m
and this can only happen for a finite number of
s\in S_{1}
. So we get

\max S=\max\left(\max_{s\in S_{1}\cap[a-m,a)}(s+m_{s}),(\max_{s\in S_{1}\cap[0,a-m)}s)+m\right)

Now we discuss the second claim. If
(s_{i})_{i}\in T
then with notations as above we have
s_{1}\geq a-m
. Hence there are only a finite number of possibilities for
s_{1}
.
We now use induction.
∎

Remark 13.4
.

The proof clearly leads to an algorithm for computing
\max S
. However it seems to be very slow.

Remark 13.5
.

A typical application of the lemma would be to take
S_{i}=\{a_{i}/k\mid k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}\}
for fixed
(a_{i})_{i}
. This implies for example that (
13.2
) has a finite number of solutions for
(r_{i})_{i}
for given

(\chi_{i,i+1})_{i}
.

13.3.
Restrictions on non-admissible vertices

Lemma
10.14
imposes strong restrictions on the non-admissible vertices of
P
.
The following preliminary result imposes further restrictions in the context of Theorem
13.1
.

Lemma 13.6
.

Let
P
be as above. Then
P
can have at most two consecutive
non-admissible non-straight vertices. Moreover if
P
has two consecutive
non-admissible non-straight vertices

l_{i,i+1}
,
l_{i+1,l+2}
then
r_{i}=r_{i+1}=r_{i+2}=1
,
\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{i+1}=\alpha_{i+2}=1
,

(13.8)

\chi_{i,i+1}=c_{i,i+1}=1,\quad\chi_{i+1,i+2}=c_{i+1,i+2}=1,\quad c_{i,i+2}=1,

and with respect to suitable coordinates the configuration is as in Figure
13.1
, where

denotes the origin.

.
m_{i}
m_{i+1}
m_{i+2}

Proof.

Assume that

has
\geq 2
consecutive non-admissible vertices

(i.e.

) with

,

being admissible.
Then the situation is like in Figure

We claim first that for

(13.9)

\frac{\omega(m_{u},m_{v})^{2}}{\omega(l_{u-1,u},l_{u,u+1})\omega(l_{v-1,v},l_{v,v+1})}<4.

We may make an

. Then the picture becomes as in Figure

Let

. I.e.

Scaling

such that

we then obtain

(13.11)

\frac{\omega(m_{u},m_{v})^{2}}{\omega(l_{u-1,u},l_{u,u+1})\omega(l_{u-1,u},l_{u,u+1})}\leq\frac{1}{(a+1)/2\cdot(b+1)/2}=\frac{4}{(a+1)(b+1)}\leq 4.

Let us now analyse when we have equality. By (

a=b=0
, i.e.
v=u+1
. Inspecting how we obtain the first inequality in (

) we see the inequalities in (

) must also be equalities.
In other words
o
is the midpoint of interval
[l_{u-1,u},l_{v,v+1}]
, which
is not in

, unless

,

. But then there is only a single

, contradicting our assumption. Hence the inequality in (

) is strict.

Since
c_{uv}
is integral we obtain from (

) together with (

)

(13.12)

c_{uv}^{2}\alpha_{u}\alpha_{v}\leq 3

which implies

(13.13)

c_{uv}=1\text{ for $i\leq u<v\leq j$}.

If
j\geq i+3
then this contradicts (
10.3
). Hence
j=i+2
. In the sequel to simplify the notations let
us put
i=1
,

. Then (
13.12
) combined with (

) yields

(13.14)

\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\leq 3,\quad\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}\leq 3,\quad\alpha_{1}\alpha_{3}\leq 3.

Put

\tilde{m}_{i}=\frac{m_{i}}{\alpha_{i}r_{i}}

According to Theorem

(

) one has
\tilde{m}_{i}\in L_{\mathbb{Z}}

and by Theorem

(

)
we have from

:

\displaystyle\omega(\tilde{m}_{1},\tilde{m}_{2})

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle\omega(\tilde{m}_{2},\tilde{m}_{3})

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle\omega(\tilde{m}_{1},\tilde{m}_{3})

\displaystyle=1

The only solution for this is (after choosing suitable coordinates for

)

\displaystyle\tilde{m}_{1}

\displaystyle=(0,-1)

\displaystyle\tilde{m}_{2}

\displaystyle=(1,-1)

\displaystyle\tilde{m}_{3}

\displaystyle=(1,0)

So the situation is as in Figure

.

Figure 13.4.

o=(x,y)

It follows from Theorem

(

), applied

and

,
that

lies on the lines

,

. In other words
o=(r_{1},r_{3})
. Moreover Theorem
8.9
(
3
) applied to the pair
(l_{12},l_{23})

yields

(13.15)

r_{2}=\frac{r_{1}+r_{3}}{\alpha_{2}+1}

The fact that
o
is in the forbidden region implies
{x\geq(\alpha_{2}r_{2}+\alpha_{3}r_{3})/2}
and
{y\geq(\alpha_{2}r_{2}+\alpha_{3}r_{1})/2}
.
From the first inequality we get

\displaystyle r_{1}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}(\alpha_{2}r_{2}+\alpha_{3}r_{3})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\alpha_{2}}{\alpha_{2}+1}(r_{1}+r_{2})+\alpha_{3}r_{3}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\alpha_{2}}{\alpha_{2}+1}r_{1}+\left(\frac{\alpha_{2}}{\alpha_{2}+1}+\alpha_{3}\right)r_{3}\right)

which yields

(13.16)

\left(2-\frac{\alpha_{2}}{\alpha_{2}+1}\right)r_{1}\geq\left(\frac{\alpha_{2}}{\alpha_{2}+1}+\alpha_{3}\right)r_{3}

Likewise

(13.17)

\left(2-\frac{\alpha_{2}}{\alpha_{2}+1}\right)r_{3}\geq\left(\frac{\alpha_{2}}{\alpha_{2}+1}+\alpha_{1}\right)r_{1}

Multiplying both formulas and cancelling
r_{1}r_{3}
yields

(13.18)

\left(2-\frac{\alpha_{2}}{\alpha_{2}+1}\right)^{2}\geq\left(\frac{\alpha_{2}}{\alpha_{2}+1}+\alpha_{1}\right)\left(\frac{\alpha_{2}}{\alpha_{2}+1}+\alpha_{3}\right)

From (
13.14
) we get
\alpha_{2}\leq 3
. Assume first
\alpha_{2}=1
. Then (
13.18
) becomes

\frac{3}{2}\times\frac{3}{2}\geq\left(\frac{1}{2}+\alpha_{1}\right)\left(\frac{1}{2}+\alpha_{3}\right)

whose only solution is
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{3}=1
. From (
13.16
)(
13.17
)
we then get
r_{1}=r_{3}
and from (
13.15
) we get
r_{2}=r_{1}=r_{3}
. From Theorem
8.9
(
2
) we
then obtain
r_{1}=r_{2}=r_{3}=1
.

If
\alpha_{2}=2
then from (
13.12
) we get
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{3}=1
and (
13.18
) becomes

\frac{4}{3}\times\frac{4}{3}\geq\left(\frac{2}{3}+1\right)\left(\frac{2}{3}+1\right),

which is false. Similarly, if
\alpha_{2}=3
then we get again
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{3}=1
and

\frac{5}{4}\times\frac{5}{4}\geq\left(\frac{3}{4}+1\right)\left(\frac{3}{4}+1\right),

which is again false. This finishes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Remark 13.7
.

The second part of Lemma
13.6
will ultimately turn out to be
empty since, as asserted in Theorem
13.1
,
P
can have at most 4 non-straight vertices.
However Lemma
13.6
will be an intermediate step in establishing this.

13.4.
Geometric lemmas for quadrangles

We consider some ad hoc geometric lemmas for convex quadrangles in the plane which will be used to bound the terms in (
13.2
)
during the treatment of 4- and 5-block collections. First assume that we are in the situation of Figure
13.5
, where
P=\operatorname{conv}(abcd)
is a convex polygon, the vertex
d
is non-admissible (see Definition
9.18
) and the “origin”
o
is in intersection of the forbidden region (see Definition
9.15
) and the interior of
P
.

Figure 13.5.
c
d
a
b
o

Note that the non-admissible vertex is unique and its existence implies that
P
has no parallel edges.

Lemma 13.8
.

The assumption that the origin is in the forbidden region is equivalent to the following conditions:

\displaystyle\frac{\operatorname{\Delta}(oab)}{\operatorname{\Delta}(abc)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}

\displaystyle\frac{\operatorname{\Delta}(obc)}{\operatorname{\Delta}(abc)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}

\displaystyle\frac{\operatorname{\Delta}(oda)}{\operatorname{\Delta}(abd)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}

\displaystyle\frac{\operatorname{\Delta}(ocd)}{\operatorname{\Delta}(bcd)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}

Lemma 13.9
.

We have the following inequalities

(13.19)

\displaystyle\frac{\operatorname{\Delta}(cda)}{\operatorname{\Delta}(oda)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\operatorname{\Delta}(oab)+2\operatorname{\Delta}(obc)-\operatorname{\Delta}(abc)}{\operatorname{\Delta}(oab)}

(13.20)

\displaystyle\frac{\operatorname{\Delta}(cda)}{\operatorname{\Delta}(ocd)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\operatorname{\Delta}(oab)+2\operatorname{\Delta}(obc)-\operatorname{\Delta}(abc)}{\operatorname{\Delta}(obc)}

Proof.

The proofs of (
13.19
) and (
13.20
) are similar, so we concentrate on (
13.19
).
We put:

x=\frac{\operatorname{\Delta}(obc)}{\operatorname{\Delta}(abc)},\qquad y=\frac{\operatorname{\Delta}(oab)}{\operatorname{\Delta}(abc)}

After applying an affine transformation we may assume that
a=(0,1),b=(0,0),c=(1,0),d=(u,v),o=(x,y)
, as
in Figure
13.6

Figure 13.6.
a(0,1)
b(0,0)
c(1,0)
o(x,y)
o^{\prime}(2x,2y)
d(u,v)

The fact that
o
is in the forbidden region implies by Lemma
9.19
that it is in the red triangle, whose vertices are the midpoints of the edges of
\operatorname{conv}(abc)
.
We construct an auxiliary point
o^{\prime}=(2x,2y)
as indicated by the light blue lines. The line
ao^{\prime}
is parallel to the line through
o
and the midpoint of
[ab]
. Likewise the line
co^{\prime}
is parallel with the line through
o
and the midpoint of
[bc]
.
The fact that
o
is in the forbidden region implies that
d
is in the triangle
\operatorname{conv}(co^{\prime}a)
.

We claim first that

(13.21)

\frac{\operatorname{\Delta}(cda)}{\operatorname{\Delta}(oda)}\leq\frac{\operatorname{\Delta}(co^{\prime}a)}{\operatorname{\Delta}(oo^{\prime}a)}.

The right hand side does not depend on
d
so we must analyse how the expression on the left changes if we move
d
. To this end we consider the following picture in Figure
13.7
, where the line
cc^{\prime}
is parallel to the line
ad
.

Figure 13.7.
a
d
c
o
c^{\prime}

One has

\frac{\operatorname{\Delta}(cda)}{\operatorname{\Delta}(oda)}=\frac{|ac^{\prime}|}{|ao|}.

Here the right hand side does not change if
d
moves on the line
ad
but it increases if
\angle dac
increases.
Since
d
is constrained to the region
\operatorname{conv}(co^{\prime}a)
we see that the maximum of the left hand side of (
13.21
) is achieved when
d=o^{\prime}
.
This proves (
13.21
). Hence it is sufficient to prove (
13.19
) with
d=o^{\prime}
since the right hand side of (
13.19
) does not depend
on
d
. We compute

\displaystyle\operatorname{\Delta}(co^{\prime}a)

\displaystyle=(2x+2y-1)/2

\displaystyle\operatorname{\Delta}(oo^{\prime}a)

\displaystyle=x/2

\displaystyle\operatorname{\Delta}(oab)

\displaystyle=x/2

\displaystyle\operatorname{\Delta}(obc)

\displaystyle=y/2

\displaystyle\operatorname{\Delta}(abc)

\displaystyle=1/2

Then (
13.19
) becomes

\frac{(2x+2y-1)/2}{x/2}\leq\frac{2(x/2)+2(y/2)-1/2}{x/2}

and we see that it is in fact an equality.
∎

Lemma 13.10
.

One has

(13.22)

\frac{\operatorname{\Delta}(cda)^{2}}{\operatorname{\Delta}(oda)\operatorname{\Delta}(ocd)}\leq 4

with equality if and only if
o
is the midpoint of
[ac]
. Furthermore,

(13.23)

\frac{\operatorname{\Delta}(abc)^{2}}{\operatorname{\Delta}(obc)\operatorname{\Delta}(oab)}\geq 4.

with equality if and only if
o
is the midpoint of
[ac]
. Finally,

(13.24)

\frac{\operatorname{\Delta}(cda)^{2}}{\operatorname{\Delta}(oda)\operatorname{\Delta}(ocd)}\cdot\frac{\operatorname{\Delta}(abc)^{2}}{\operatorname{\Delta}(oab)\operatorname{\Delta}(obc)}\leq 16.

Proof.

Both (
13.22
) and (
13.23
) are easy. To prove (
13.22
) we choose a coordinate system such that
c=(0,1)
,
a=(0,0)
,
d=(1,0)

and we put
o=(p,q)
. Then the left hand side (
13.22
) becomes
\frac{(1/4)}{(p/2)(q/2)}=\frac{1}{pq}
.
The fact that
o
is in the forbidden region implies
p\geq 1/2
,
q\geq 1/2
which yields what we want. To prove (
13.23
) we use a coordinate system as in Figure
13.6
.

Now we concentrate on (
13.24
). Put

u=\frac{\operatorname{\Delta}(cda)^{2}}{\operatorname{\Delta}(oda)\operatorname{\Delta}(ocd)}.

Hence
u\leq 4
by (
13.22
).
Multiplying (
13.19
) and (
13.20
) yields:

(13.25)

u\leq\frac{(2\operatorname{\Delta}(oab)+2\operatorname{\Delta}(obc)-\operatorname{\Delta}(abc))^{2}}{\operatorname{\Delta}(oab)\operatorname{\Delta}(obc)}

We claim that this implies (
13.24
).
To prove this we put ourselves in the setting of Figure
13.6
. Then (
13.25
)
is equivalent to

f(x,y):=(2x+2y-1)^{2}-uxy\geq 0

We have to prove that this implies
xy\geq u/16
under the conditions
0<x\leq 1/2
,
0<y\leq 1/2
,
2x+2y-1\geq 0
, which express that
o
is in the red
triangle in Figure
13.6
as well as in the interior of
\operatorname{conv}(abcd)
.

The curve
C:=\{f(x,y)=0\}
is an ellipse and

takes negative values on the interior of the region bounded by

.
Clearly,

is invariant under the reflection
x\leftrightarrow y

,

respectively
in the points
\{(1/2,0),(1/2,u/8)\}
and
\{(0,1/2),(u/8,1/2)\}
. So the situation is like depicted in Figure

.

which gives what we want.

Lemma 13.11
.

We have

(13.26)

2\leq\frac{\operatorname{\Delta}(bcd)}{\operatorname{\Delta}(ocd)}\leq\frac{1}{\frac{1}{2}-2\frac{\operatorname{\Delta}(obc)\operatorname{\Delta}(oab)}{\operatorname{\Delta}(abc)^{2}}}

Proof.

Consider the following picture in Figure
13.9
, where
oo^{\prime}
is parallel to
cd
and
oo^{\prime\prime}
is parallel to
ac
.

Figure 13.9.
c
d
a
b
o
o^{\prime}
o^{\prime\prime}

We find

\frac{\operatorname{\Delta}(bcd)}{\operatorname{\Delta}(ocd)}=\frac{|cb|}{|co^{\prime}|}\leq\frac{|cb|}{|co^{\prime\prime}|}

We first prove the upper bound in (
13.26
).
Put

(13.27)

v=\frac{\operatorname{\Delta}(abc)^{2}}{\operatorname{\Delta}(obc)\operatorname{\Delta}(oab)}

We need to find an upper bound for
|cb|/|co^{\prime\prime}|
in terms of
v
. To this end we choose the coordinates as in Figure
13.6
. This gives us the picture in Figure
13.10
, where the purple curve expresses (
13.27
) and the indicated
o^{\prime\prime}
is the one such that
|co^{\prime\prime}|
is minimal.

Figure 13.10.
a(0,1)
b(0,0)
c(1,0)
xy=\dfrac{1}{v}
\left(\dfrac{1}{2},\dfrac{2}{v}\right)
o^{\prime\prime}
\left(\dfrac{1}{2}+\dfrac{2}{v},0\right)

We see that

|co^{\prime\prime}|\geq\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{v}

so that

\frac{|cb|}{|co^{\prime\prime}|}\leq\frac{1}{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{v}}

which finishes the proof of the upper bound in (
13.26
).
The lower bound follows from Lemma
13.8
.
∎

Lemma 13.12
.

One has

(13.28)

\operatorname{\Delta}(oda)+\operatorname{\Delta}(ocd)\leq\operatorname{\Delta}(oab)+\operatorname{\Delta}(obc)

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\operatorname{\Delta}(oda)+\operatorname{\Delta}(oab)+\operatorname{\Delta}(obc)+\operatorname{\Delta}(ocd)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\Delta}(bcd)+\operatorname{\Delta}(bda)

\displaystyle\geq 2\operatorname{\Delta}(ocd)+2\operatorname{\Delta}(oda)\qquad(\text{by Lemma \ref{lem:forbidden_area})}.

This implies (
13.28
).
∎

,

.

We consider the following two cases separately.

13.5.1.
Case 1

First we assume that the origin lies on the dotted interval in Figure

.
Since the origin

(
3
)

\displaystyle\alpha_{2}r_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{1}r_{1}^{2}

\displaystyle\alpha_{3}r_{3}^{2}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{0}r_{0}^{2}

So it is sufficient to show that there are only a finite number of possibilities for
r_{0}
,
r_{1}
. Using (
13.31
) we obtain

\displaystyle K^{2}_{X}

\displaystyle=\frac{S_{01}}{\alpha_{0}\alpha_{1}r_{0}^{2}r_{1}^{2}}+\frac{S_{12}}{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{0}r_{1}^{2}r_{0}^{2}}+\frac{S_{23}}{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{0}r_{1}^{2}r_{0}^{2}}+\frac{S_{30}}{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{0}r_{1}^{2}r_{0}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{S_{01}+S_{12}+S_{23}+S_{30}}{\alpha_{0}\alpha_{1}r_{0}^{2}r_{1}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2(\alpha_{0}r_{0}^{2}+\alpha_{1}r_{1}^{2}+\alpha_{2}r_{2}^{2}+\alpha_{3}r_{3}^{2})}{\alpha_{0}\alpha_{1}r_{0}^{2}r_{1}^{2}},

where in the last line we have used that
S_{01}+S_{12}+S_{23}+S_{30}=4\Delta(P)
together with Theorem

(

).

So we finally obtain a Markov type equation

(13.37)

\alpha_{0}r_{0}^{2}+\alpha_{1}r_{1}^{2}=\frac{K^{2}_{X}\alpha_{0}\alpha_{1}}{4}r_{0}^{2}r_{1}^{2}.

This has a finite number of solutions. Indeed, after dividing by
r_{0}^{2}r_{1}^{2}
we may apply Remark
13.5
.

13.5.2.

Now we assume that the origin does not lie on the dotted interval in Figure

.
Since the first two inequalities in (

\displaystyle\leq 3

\chi_{12}=1

)

2\leq\frac{S_{01}}{\alpha_{1}r_{1}^{2}}\leq\gamma:=\frac{1}{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{\chi_{30}^{2}\alpha_{3}\alpha_{0}}}\leq\frac{1}{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{(\sqrt{5})^{2}}}=10

Similarly

(13.42)

2\leq\frac{S_{23}}{\alpha_{2}r_{2}^{2}}\leq\gamma:=\frac{1}{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{\chi_{30}^{2}\alpha_{3}\alpha_{0}}}\leq 10

So we get using (
13.6
), (
13.40
),(
13.41
) and (
13.42
)

(13.43)

K_{X}^{2}=\frac{S_{01}}{\alpha_{0}\alpha_{1}r_{0}^{2}r_{1}^{2}}+\frac{S_{12}}{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}r_{1}^{2}r_{2}^{2}}+\frac{S_{23}}{\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}r_{2}^{2}r_{3}^{2}}+\frac{S_{30}}{\alpha_{3}\alpha_{0}r_{3}^{2}r_{0}^{2}}\\
\geq\frac{2}{\alpha_{0}r_{0}^{2}}+\frac{1}{r_{1}r_{2}}+\frac{2}{\alpha_{3}r_{3}^{2}}+\frac{\chi_{30}}{r_{3}r_{0}}

and

(13.44)

K_{X}^{2}=\frac{S_{01}}{\alpha_{0}\alpha_{1}r_{0}^{2}r_{1}^{2}}+\frac{S_{12}}{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}r_{1}^{2}r_{2}^{2}}+\frac{S_{23}}{\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}r_{2}^{2}r_{3}^{2}}+\frac{S_{30}}{\alpha_{3}\alpha_{0}r_{3}^{2}r_{0}^{2}}\\
\leq\frac{\gamma}{\alpha_{0}r_{0}^{2}}+\frac{1}{r_{1}r_{2}}+\frac{\gamma}{\alpha_{3}r_{3}^{2}}+\frac{\chi_{30}}{r_{3}r_{0}}

We claim that (
13.34
), (

), (
13.44
) and (

) have only a finite number of solutions for

.

) we obtain

1&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

Then from (

) we obtain

(13.45)

\frac{10}{\alpha_{0}r_{0}^{2}}+\frac{10}{\alpha_{3}r_{3}^{2}}+\frac{\chi_{30}}{r_{3}r_{0}}\geq\frac{1}{2}

Multipying by

,

which only has a finite number
of solutions for

,

. From (

is finite.

.

.

) has a single solution:

However since

, this contradicts Theorem

(

).

We also have by (

(13.50)

\displaystyle\chi^{2}_{12}\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\frac{\Delta(l_{01},l_{23},l_{40})^{2}}{\Delta(o,l_{23},l_{40})\Delta(o,l_{40},l_{01})}

\displaystyle\leq 16
