\displaystyle\leq 16

We will need the following lemma.

Lemma 13.13
.

Assume that
x,y,z\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
satisfy

(13.51)

x^{2}+y^{2}+z^{2}=xyz

Then
x>2
and

(13.52)

y,z\geq\frac{2x}{\sqrt{x^{2}-4}}

Proof.

By symmetry it is sufficient to prove (
13.52
) only for
z
.
We see that
y
satisfies the quadratic equation

y^{2}-(xz)y+(x^{2}+z^{2})=0

Since
y
is real, this means the discriminant must be non-negative. Hence

(xz)^{2}-4(x^{2}+z^{2})\geq 0

Alternatively,

(13.53)

(x^{2}-4)z^{2}\geq 4x^{2}

This is impossible if
x\leq 2
, hence
x>2
. Moreover (
13.53
) is equivalent to (
13.52
) for
z
.
∎

Corollary 13.14
.

Let
\lambda>2
.
Assume that
x,y,z\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
satisfy

x^{2}+y^{2}+z^{2}=xyz

and
x\leq\lambda
. Then

y,z\geq\frac{2\lambda}{\sqrt{\lambda^{2}-4}}

Proof.

This follows from the fact that the right hand side of (
13.52
) is a decreasing function of
x
.
∎

From (
13.50
) we obtain in particular

(13.54)

\displaystyle\frac{\Delta(l_{01},l_{23},l_{40})}{\sqrt{\Delta(o,l_{23},l_{40})\Delta(o,l_{40},l_{01})}}

\displaystyle\leq 4

\displaystyle\frac{\Delta(l_{01},l_{23},l_{40})}{\sqrt{\Delta(o,l_{01},l_{23})\Delta(o,l_{40},l_{01})}}

\displaystyle\leq 4

Direct substitution shows that

(x,y,z):=\left(\frac{\Delta(l_{01},l_{23},l_{40})}{\sqrt{\Delta(o,l_{23},l_{40})\Delta(o,l_{40},l_{01})}},\frac{\Delta(l_{01},l_{23},l_{40})}{\sqrt{\Delta(o,l_{01},l_{23})\Delta(o,l_{40},l_{01})}},\frac{\Delta(l_{01},l_{23},l_{40})}{\sqrt{\Delta(o,l_{23},l_{40})\Delta(o,l_{01},l_{23})}}\right)

satisfies (
13.51
). Hence we get by Corollary (
13.14
) and (
13.54
):

(13.55)

\frac{\Delta(l_{01},l_{23},l_{40})}{\sqrt{\Delta(o,l_{23},l_{40})\Delta(o,l_{40},l_{01})}}\geq\frac{2\times 4}{\sqrt{4^{2}-4}}=\frac{4}{\sqrt{3}}

and similarly

\displaystyle\frac{\Delta(l_{01},l_{23},l_{40})}{\sqrt{\Delta(o,l_{01},l_{23})\Delta(o,l_{40},l_{01})}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{4}{\sqrt{3}}

Applying (
13.26
) to the polygon obtained by deleting
l_{34}
, together with Lemma
9.19
, yields

(13.56)

\frac{{\Delta}(l_{12},l_{23},l_{40})}{\Delta(o,l_{12},l_{23})}\leq\frac{1}{\frac{1}{2}-2\frac{\Delta(o,l_{23},l_{40})\Delta(o,l_{40},l_{01})}{{\Delta}(l_{01},l_{23},l_{40})^{2}}}

We also observe that

(13.57)

\Delta(l_{12},l_{23},l_{34})\leq\Delta(l_{12},l_{23},l_{40})

and, since
\omega
is normalised, we get from Theorem
8.9
(
3
)

(13.58)

\alpha_{2}r_{2}^{2}=2\Delta(o,l_{12},l_{23}).

Combining (
13.56
), (
13.57
) and (
13.58
) yields

(13.59)

2\frac{{\Delta}(l_{12},l_{23},l_{34})}{\alpha_{2}r_{2}^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{\frac{1}{2}-2\frac{\Delta(o,l_{01},l_{23})\Delta(o,l_{40},l_{01})}{{\Delta}(l_{01},l_{23},l_{40})^{2}}}.

Combining (
13.59
) with (
13.55
) yields

\frac{S_{23}}{\alpha_{2}r_{2}^{2}}=2\frac{{\Delta}(l_{12},l_{23},l_{34})}{\alpha_{2}r_{2}^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{\frac{1}{2}-2\frac{3}{16}}=8

and similarly by symmetry

\frac{S_{23}}{\alpha_{3}r_{3}^{2}}=2\frac{{\Delta}(l_{12},l_{23},l_{34})}{\alpha_{3}r_{3}^{2}}\leq 8.

Multiplying these yields by (
13.6
)

\chi_{23}^{2}\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}\leq 8^{2},

so that in particular

\chi_{23}\leq 8.

The same symmetry argument applied to the polygon obtained by deleting
l_{34}
gives

(13.60)

\frac{S_{01}}{\alpha_{1}r_{1}^{2}}=\frac{2\Delta(l_{01},l_{12},l_{40})}{\alpha_{1}r_{1}^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{\frac{1}{2}-2\frac{\Delta(o,l_{23},l_{40})\Delta(o,l_{40},l_{01})}{{\Delta}(l_{01},l_{23},l_{40})^{2}}}=8.

Similarly, using symmetry for
P
, we get

(13.61)

\frac{S_{40}}{\alpha_{1}r_{4}^{2}}=\frac{2\Delta(l_{01},l_{34},l_{40})}{\alpha_{4}r_{4}^{2}}\leq 8.

Now we restate (
13.2
) in a convenient form, using (
13.6
) (where we have already substituted (
13.49
)):

(13.62)

\frac{1}{r_{1}r_{2}}+\frac{\chi_{23}}{r_{2}r_{3}}+\frac{1}{r_{3}r_{4}}+\frac{S_{40}}{\alpha_{4}r_{4}^{2}\alpha_{0}r_{0}^{2}}+\frac{S_{01}}{\alpha_{0}r_{0}^{2}\alpha_{1}r_{1}^{2}}=K_{X}^{2}

So in particular

(13.63)

\frac{1}{r_{1}r_{2}}+\frac{\chi_{23}}{r_{2}r_{3}}+\frac{1}{r_{3}r_{4}}<K_{X}^{2}.

If we apply Lemma
13.3
to
S_{1},S_{3}=\{1/n\mid n\geq 1\}
,
S_{2}=\{\chi_{23}/n\mid n\geq 1\}

we find
from (
13.62
) that there exists
\epsilon>0
such that

\frac{S_{40}}{\alpha_{4}r_{4}^{2}\alpha_{0}r_{0}^{2}}+\frac{S_{01}}{\alpha_{0}r_{0}^{2}\alpha_{1}r_{1}^{2}}\geq\epsilon

and hence by (
13.60
) and (
13.61
) we get

\frac{16}{\alpha_{0}r_{0}^{2}}=\frac{8}{\alpha_{0}r_{0}^{2}}+\frac{8}{\alpha_{0}r_{0}^{2}}\geq\frac{S_{40}}{\alpha_{4}r_{4}^{2}\alpha_{0}r_{0}^{2}}+\frac{S_{01}}{\alpha_{0}r_{0}^{2}\alpha_{1}r_{1}^{2}}\geq\epsilon.

Hence there are only a finite number of possibilities for
r_{0}
. From (
10.6
) we obtain

(13.64)

r_{1},r_{2},r_{3},r_{4}\leq r_{0}.

Hence there are only a finite number of possibilities for
r_{0},r_{1},r_{2},r_{3},r_{4}
. It then follows from (
13.2
)(
13.3
) that there are only a finite
number of Gram matrices.

In now suffices to do an exhaustive search to show that there are no Gram matrices that satisfy the conditions of Theorem
13.1
.
However while the number of possibilities is finite, it is still very large, so we have to cut it down.

The first optimisation we can do is that once we have a bound on
r_{0}
, we have a bound on
r_{1},r_{2},r_{3},r_{4}
. Hence we can now compute an upper bound for (
13.63
)
by brute force
(i.e. just looping over those
r_{1},r_{2},r_{3},r_{4}
that satisfy (
13.64
)). This allows us to compute a new upper bound for
r_{0}
and we can repeat this procedure until it converges.

A second optimisation is to observe that Lemma
10.9
in fact implies that there exist
1\leq i\leq j\leq 4
such that

(13.65)

r_{1}>r_{2}>\cdots>r_{i}=r_{i+1}=\cdots=r_{j}<r_{j+1}<\cdots<r_{4}

which allows us to prune more potential solutions.

A third optimisation is to invoke (
13.6
), i.e.

(13.66)

\chi_{i,i+1}=\frac{S_{i,i+1}}{\alpha_{i}\alpha_{i+1}r_{i}r_{i+1}}.

Then the bound we have used

\frac{S_{40}}{\alpha_{4}r_{4}^{2}}\leq 8

becomes

(13.67)

\frac{\chi_{40}\alpha_{0}r_{0}}{r_{4}}\leq 8.

Similarly, we get

\frac{\chi_{01}\alpha_{0}r_{0}}{r_{1}}\leq 8.

These give upper bounds for
\chi_{40}
,
\chi_{01}
, which are very useful.

A fourth optimisation we can do is to note that using
(r_{1},r_{2},r_{3},r_{4},\chi_{12}=1,\chi_{23},\chi_{34}=1)
we can compute the Gram matrix for
the subsequence

(13.68)

(E_{1}^{(0)},\ldots,E_{1}^{(\alpha_{1}-1)},\ldots,E_{4}^{(0)},\ldots,E_{4}^{(\alpha_{4}-1)}).

The fact that this Gram matrix must have integral entries is a strong restriction.
Moreover, we can also compute the Gram matrix of the dual exceptional collection, which gives the arrows/relations for the corresponding quiver. In this case there
are no relations (the quiver
Q
, which may be computed using Theorem
8.9
(

), has no reverse arrows between the exceptional objects listed in (

)

{\mathbb{E}}
correspond to reverse arrows in

).
This allows us to eliminate more possibilities quickly.

13.7.
Six or more blocks

We finally show that no six- or more block collection can satisfy the conditions of Theorem
13.1
. Lemma
10.14
combined with
Lemma
13.6
leaves us only a single possibility to examine, namely, the one shown in Figure
13.15
, where the forbidden region has been coloured green.

Figure 13.15.

However this case cannot not occur
since according to Lemma
13.6
the origin should be in the purple dot,
which is not in the forbidden region.
